CMT Level I Answer Keys and Explanations

Numbered to match the practice tests in your printed study guide.

Practice Test 1 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (R squared is 0.36, so the index explains about 36 percent, and correlation is not causation): R squared is the square of r, so 0.60 squared is 0.36, and only about 36 percent of the stock's variation is statistically accounted for by the index. A moderate correlation, even a strong one, does not show that one series causes the other. The distractor that keeps 0.36 gets the arithmetic right but still draws an unsupported causal conclusion.
  2. The correct answer is B (Endowment bias, valuing the shares above market worth because she owns them, so reduce gradually): She would not buy at today's price yet will not sell, which means she values the shares more because she owns them: the endowment effect, strengthened by sentiment about her father. Because emotional biases resist argument, staged reductions fit better than debate. Status quo bias is similar but does not explain the refusal to buy, and the other two do not match what she says.
  3. The correct answer is D (It challenges weak-form efficiency since past prices predict returns, though risk may explain it): Momentum means past winners keep winning, which uses only past prices and so challenges the weak form. Because any test of efficiency also depends on a model of expected returns, the risk-premium defense cannot be dismissed outright. A persistent effect after costs is not support for weak-form efficiency, momentum is not the calendar-based January effect, and one study cannot prove a particular alternative hypothesis.
  4. The correct answer is B (Using today's constituents omits firms delisted or dropped after poor results, which inflates the past returns): Testing on today's index members is survivorship bias: companies that failed or were removed are missing, so past returns look better than an investor could have earned. Weighting choice, history length, and arithmetic versus geometric reporting are valid issues in other tests but do not explain why the result is inflated here.
  5. The correct answer is B (The median of 2.3 million shares, because the middle value resists one extreme day.): The sorted values are 2.1, 2.2, 2.3, 2.4, 9.8, so the median is 2.3 million. The mean is 18.8 divided by 5, or 3.76 million, pulled upward by the spike, and the geometric mean of about 3.0 million is still pulled up. No value repeats, so there is no mode.
  6. The correct answer is C (A fall in the standard deviation of closes around the 20-period average, so recent volatility declined.): Bollinger Bands sit a set number of standard deviations above and below a 20-period average, so band width tracks the dispersion of closes. Contraction therefore means volatility fell. ATR multiples build Keltner Channels, which tempts because both are volatility envelopes. A highest high to lowest low range belongs to Donchian Channels. Standard error describes the precision of the mean, not the spread of prices, so it does not set the bands.
  7. The correct answer is A (Hamilton refined and applied Dow's ideas in editorials, while Rhea later codified them in a book.): Dow did not write a finished theory. William Hamilton, his successor as editor, developed and applied the ideas in editorials, and Robert Rhea organized them into a book in 1932. Swapping their roles tempts because both wrote about the theory. Volume was a secondary check from the start, the secondary reaction was in Dow's own scheme, and the theory stayed focused on the averages.
  8. The correct answer is A (It fits weak form efficiency, since past price patterns gave no excess returns after costs.): The test used only past prices, which is the information set of weak form efficiency, and found no excess return after costs. That is consistent with weak form, though one test on one fund cannot prove it. It says nothing about filings or insider information, and a failed strategy is evidence for efficiency, not against.
  9. The correct answer is D (The heavy final-week volume is a selling climax, which marks the exhaustion of an advance.): A climax at the end of an advance is a buying climax, since late buyers drive volume while price stalls. A selling climax occurs at lows after a decline. Rising volume with rising price confirms a trend, and the volume-weighted average price weights each price by the shares traded, which is why it is an execution benchmark.
  10. The correct answer is D (The disposition effect, selling winners quickly and holding losers to avoid realizing a loss): Quick sales of winners combined with long holds of losers is the textbook definition of the disposition effect, driven by loss aversion and the wish to avoid realizing a loss. The endowment effect would also keep losers but cannot explain why winners were sold so fast. House money effect and mental accounting describe different behaviors, not the holding-period asymmetry.
  11. The correct answer is B (Treat it as a secondary reaction inside the primary uptrend and hold, watching the reaction low): A pullback of about one third to two thirds of the prior advance that lasts several weeks to months is a secondary reaction, so the primary uptrend stands while the prior reaction low holds. A retracement of 40 percent is within the normal range and does not by itself end a primary trend. Minor movements last under three weeks, and Dow Theory needs both averages, not one.
  12. The correct answer is D (Tighten the protective stop and watch for a higher close next session as confirmation.): A small body at the top with a long lower shadow after a decline is a hammer: sellers pushed price down but buyers rejected the lows. Adding to the short misreads that rejection. Reversing at the open acts before the next session confirms. Body color is secondary, since a black-bodied hammer still carries the warning. Tightening the stop and waiting for a higher close respects the signal without overreacting.
  13. The correct answer is D (A long black body, a small gapped-down body, then a long white body.): The morning star begins with a long black candle in the decline, followed by a small body that gaps lower to show indecision, and finishes with a long white candle that closes well into the first body. The evening star is the mirror image at tops. The other sequences change the order or mix in an engulfing pattern, which is a two-candle formation.
  14. The correct answer is C (Emotional biases stem from feelings, so information rarely corrects them and the advisor should adapt.): The reluctance described is the endowment effect, an emotional bias. Emotional biases arise from feelings, not a lack of facts, so more data rarely changes behavior and the advisor should adapt the plan. Cognitive errors respond to better information, which is why labelling this one cognitive tempts. Repetition adds little because the cause is feeling, not missing facts.
  15. The correct answer is B (Restrict the trader's activity, investigate the facts, then escalate the findings to compliance and senior management): A supervisor who learns of a possible violation should act promptly to stop it, investigate, and report through the firm's channels. Restricting the activity first prevents further harm while the facts are gathered. Waiting for compliance, going outside before acting inside, or delaying restriction until an informal chat is done each leaves the suspected conduct running longer.
  16. The correct answer is B (House money effect, taking larger risks with recent gains because they feel like the market's): Treating recent profits as free money and taking more risk with them is the house money effect, a form of mental accounting. Loss aversion would make her more cautious, not less, and the disposition effect involves selling gains, which she did not do. Regret aversion fits traders who avoid acting, and nothing in the stem mentions regret.
  17. The correct answer is C (The price-weighted index falls about 3.6 percent, while the cap-weighted and equal-weighted indexes both rise.): Prices sum to 330 and fall to 318, so the price-weighted index drops about 3.6 percent. Market caps are 3 billion and 12 billion, so the cap-weighted index rises about 7.0 percent, and the equal-weighted index rises the average of minus 5 and plus 10, or 2.5 percent. Share price drives weight only in the price-weighted index.
  18. The correct answer is B (ADX at 14 still reads as a weak trend, so its rise shows only early strengthening): ADX measures trend strength without direction, and readings below roughly 20 to 25 describe a weak trend, so 14 is not yet strong even though it is rising. The +DI cross above -DI is a bullish directional hint, but the strength is not there to justify full size. A rising ADX shows strengthening, not exhaustion, the usual strength threshold is far below 40, and direction comes from the DI lines.
  19. The correct answer is B (The simple average drops the spike abruptly as it leaves; the exponential fades it gradually): A simple average gives every bar in its window equal weight and then drops the oldest bar completely, so a large value leaves in one step and the average falls sharply. An exponential average gives older bars steadily smaller weights and never removes them in a single step, so the spike fades gradually. An EMA has no fixed window, and the two do not remove data at the same moment.
  20. The correct answer is C (Representativeness, classifying something by resemblance to a stereotype while ignoring how rarely the outcome occurs.): Representativeness leads people to judge by resemblance to a familiar type, here past winners, and to neglect base rates, which show that few young firms keep growing at that pace. Conservatism concerns slow updating and illusion of control concerns perceived influence. Availability is close, but the reasoning rests on resemblance rather than on how easy examples are to recall.
  21. The correct answer is C (The RSI condition below 70, which permits or blocks the entry without being the firing event.): A trigger is the event that fires a trade, here the close above the 50-day average. A filter is a condition that allows or blocks the trigger, so the requirement that RSI be below 70 is the filter. The 2 percent limit controls trade size, not whether the entry happens. The 14-day lookback is a setting inside the RSI calculation, not a rule of its own.
  22. The correct answer is B (The strong form is rejected, while the semi-strong form can still hold.): The strong form says prices reflect all information including private, so insider profits reject it. The semi-strong form covers only public information and is untouched by the insider finding. The forms are nested, so if semi-strong failed, strong would fail too, and the weak form concerns past prices only. Eugene Fama's framework treats each form as a stricter test of efficiency.
  23. The correct answer is C (The primary trend, the tide, which lasts a year or more and sets the major direction.): Dow's tide is the primary trend, lasting a year or more, the waves are secondary reactions lasting about three weeks to three months, and the ripples are minor movements under three weeks. Each wrong option mixes one correct piece, the metaphor, the span or the movement, with a mismatched piece from another level.
  24. The correct answer is C (Falling open interest in a rally suggests short covering rather than new buying, a warning): Rising prices with rising open interest show new money supporting the advance, while rising prices with falling open interest suggest shorts are covering rather than new longs entering. That is a warning of weakening support, not proof of a reversal. Shrinking open interest means contracts are being closed, not added, so the first and second views misread the data.
  25. The correct answer is B (Find intrinsic value of 2.00, subtract 3.00 premium, then multiply by 100: a 100 dollar loss.): At expiration the call is worth 52 - 50 = 2.00, but the buyer paid 3.00, so the net is -1.00 per share, or -100 dollars for 100 shares. The breakeven is 53.00, so a settle at 52 is below it and cannot be a gain. Adding the premium and ignoring the stock price both treat the premium incorrectly.
  26. The correct answer is A (The 0.5 ratio, and a 0.618 retracement would sit near 95.3.): The swing is 40 points. The pullback gave back 120 minus 100, or 20 points, which is half the advance, so the 0.5 ratio was reached. A 0.618 retracement takes back 0.618 times 40, about 24.7 points, which puts it near 120 minus 24.7, or 95.3. Subtracting 0.382 times 40 gives 104.7, and adding to the high describes an extension.
  27. The correct answer is D (A z-score of about 3.2, which is unusual, though five observations make the estimate unstable.): The mean is 52, the squared deviations sum to 40, and dividing by n minus 1 gives a variance of 10 and a standard deviation of about 3.16. The new close is 10 above the mean, so the z-score is 10 divided by 3.16, about 3.2. The population formula would give 3.5 and treating variance as spread gives 1.0, and with only five points even a z of 3.2 deserves caution.
  28. The correct answer is C (The ETF loses about 4 percent versus the index's 1 percent, due to daily resetting.): The index goes 1.10 times 0.90, or 0.99, a 1 percent loss. The ETF goes up 20 percent and then down 20 percent, which is 1.20 times 0.80, or 0.96, a 4 percent loss. Daily resetting means the multiple holds for each day, not for the whole period, so the compounding drag appears.
  29. The correct answer is A (Inverted, with short maturities yielding more than long ones, often read as slowing growth): The chart shows short maturities yielding more than long ones, which is an inverted curve, and the customary reading is slowing growth because markets expect lower rates later. Reading inversion as stronger growth reverses the signal, since a steep normal curve is what often accompanies expansion. Higher expected inflation tends to steepen the curve rather than invert it. Supply distortions can matter at the front end, but technicians still treat inversion as informative.
  30. The correct answer is B (Uptrend until the lower high at 49, a warning confirmed only if price breaks 45): The sequence of higher highs and higher lows to 51 defines an uptrend. The lower high at 49 is the first warning, but the trend is not reversed until price breaks the latest higher low at 45. One lower high alone does not confirm a downtrend, the relevant low is 45 and not 43, and a single failed high does not turn the whole sequence into a range.
  31. The correct answer is A (Non-confirmation: transports failed to exceed 15,200, so the industrial high is a warning, not a signal.): Confirmation under Dow requires both averages to make new highs. Industrials cleared 34,000 by 600 points, but transports fell 150 points short of 15,200, so the rally is not confirmed. That is a caution consistent with a late phase of the trend, not proof of a reversal, which would need further downside confirmation. No fixed percentage threshold exists in the theory.
  32. The correct answer is C (Price already embodies known information and expectations, so studying price captures the market's view): Dow held that the averages discount everything, meaning the combined actions of many buyers and sellers already reflect what is known and expected, so a technician can work from price. Price does not lag the news, and the tenet does not remove the need for volume confirmation. Dow said even unforeseeable events are discounted as they become known.
  33. The correct answer is B (31.00, from dividing the two pre-split closes by 3.): Dividing the pre-split closes by 3 gives 30 and 31, and the post-split close is 32, so the average is (30 plus 31 plus 32) divided by 3, or 31.00. The unadjusted average is 215 divided by 3, or 71.67, which mixes two share bases. The other values adjust the wrong bar or divide a mixed average.
  34. The correct answer is D (The bands show historical volatility from past prices; implied volatility comes from option prices.): Bollinger Bands are computed from the standard deviation of past closing prices, so they show historical volatility. Implied volatility is backed out of option prices and reflects expectations, so the two can move apart. Contracting bands do not prove implied volatility fell or will rise, and the bands use price, not volume.
  35. The correct answer is A (A double top needs a close below the middle trough; projecting now is premature.): The trough between the peaks is the confirmation level for a double top, and until price closes below it the pattern is only a possibility. Two peaks are enough for a double top, and the measured target uses the pattern's height, not volume. The second peak typically forms on lighter volume, so waiting for rising volume would misdirect the analyst.
  36. The correct answer is D (Add each day's net advances to a running total, producing the advance-decline line.): The advance-decline line accumulates daily advances minus declines, so it shows whether participation supports the index trend. A single daily difference or a daily ratio does not accumulate, and a count of new highs is a different breadth tool with its own interpretation.
  37. The correct answer is B (Draw from 80 to 60; the 0.382 line near 67.6 was just reached.): The retracement is drawn across the whole prior swing, from 80 to 60, which spans 20 points. In a downtrend the bounce is measured up from the low: 60 plus 0.382 times 20 is about 67.6, so the bounce at 68 has reached that line. The 0.618 line sits at about 72.4, and a line measured from the bounce itself does not describe the prior swing.
  38. The correct answer is C (The drop is an artifact; halving earlier prices removes it and shows no crash): A 2 for 1 split cuts the share price roughly in half while each holder owns twice as many shares, so no value is lost. An adjusted series divides all earlier prices by two, and the apparent crash disappears. Splits require adjustment just as dividends do. Heavy volume on a split date does not make the drop real, since volume cannot turn a data artifact into a decline in value.
  39. The correct answer is C (A trade at 57, three boxes below the circled column's top.): With a three-box reversal, a new column in the opposite direction needs price to move three full boxes against the current column's extreme. The X column ends at 60, so the first O appears when price reaches 57, which fills 59, 58 and 57. A one-box count gives 59, an extra box gives 56, and measuring from the earlier O column's low points to 54 instead.
  40. The correct answer is D (Volatility had been low, and a post-squeeze close outside the band can start a trend.): Band width is driven by the standard deviation of closing prices, so tight bands mean closes have been clustered and volatility is low. Low volatility tends to be followed by high volatility, and a close beyond a band after a squeeze often begins a sustained move rather than a reversal. Fading the move treats a band touch as automatic resistance. Band width responds to the dispersion of prices, not to a change in the average's direction.
  41. The correct answer is C (Sells A to lock in the gain, then holds B hoping it recovers to 50.): The disposition effect is the tendency to sell winners too early and hold losers too long, because gains are locked in readily while losses are avoided to prevent regret. Holding the winner and cutting the loser is the reverse. Tax loss selling is a deliberate plan, and averaging down is a different decision from the asymmetric pattern described.
  42. The correct answer is D (Loss aversion, because realizing the loss feels worse than the same economic loss left unrealized): The economic loss exists whether or not the position is sold, yet loss aversion makes the act of realizing it painful, so she avoids it. Overconfidence and representativeness are real biases but nothing in her statement concerns forecast precision or similar-stock streaks. The house money effect needs a prior gain, which the statement does not mention.
  43. The correct answer is D (55 divided by 89 is 0.618, and 34 divided by 89 is 0.382, both retracements): A Fibonacci number divided by the next one gives 0.618, here 55 over 89 equals 0.618. A number divided by the one two places higher gives 0.382, here 34 over 89 equals 0.382. Dividing 89 by 55 gives 1.618, the Golden Ratio itself, not a pullback level. Dividing 21 by 89 gives 0.236, not 0.382, and the two correct values cannot be swapped because 0.618 is the larger retracement.
  44. The correct answer is C (About 19 percent, found by multiplying 1.2 by the square root of 252.): Daily volatility scales with the square root of time, so 1.2 times the square root of 252 gives about 19 percent. Using 365 calendar days inflates the answer to about 23 percent because markets are closed on weekends. A weekly factor of 52 suits weekly returns, not daily ones. Doubling the result mixes up a one standard deviation volatility figure with a two standard deviation band width.
  45. The correct answer is D (The accumulation phase, in which informed buyers build positions near the end of a prolonged decline.): Dow's first phase of a bull market is accumulation, when informed investors buy from those who have given up and the public remains pessimistic. Public participation is the second phase and distribution the third, where informed holders sell to a eager public. A secondary reaction is a correction inside a trend, not a phase of the trend.
  46. The correct answer is B (The accumulation phase of a primary bull market): Poor news, a gloomy public, stalled prices, modest volume, and informed buying are the accumulation phase. Public participation comes later with better news, distribution has informed investors selling, and panic features forced selling and falling prices, which the stem says has stopped.
  47. The correct answer is C (Narrowing bands show falling volatility with no direction, though a larger move often follows.): Narrowing Bollinger Bands mean the standard deviation of closes has fallen, which measures volatility and says nothing about direction. Volatility tends to alternate between quiet and active phases, so a larger move often follows, but the squeeze alone does not reveal whether it will be up or down. Calling it trend exhaustion, a persistent calm, or an upside setup each adds a directional or timing claim the bands do not supply.
  48. The correct answer is B (Herding for the first group, and conservatism, a belief perseverance bias, for the second group.): Herding is following the crowd's actions rather than one's own analysis. Conservatism is a belief perseverance bias in which people fail to update their views enough when new facts arrive. The swapped pairing reverses the two definitions, and framing, availability, mental accounting and regret avoidance describe different mistakes.
  49. The correct answer is B (A hammer shape after an advance is a hanging man, warning of weakness, not a bottom.): A hammer is a bullish reversal only after a decline. The same shape after a six week advance is a hanging man, a bearish warning, so the analyst has the pattern name and implication backwards. A shooting star has a long upper shadow, not a long lower one. A hammer is confirmed by a higher close, not a lower one, and a higher open after a hammer is mildly supportive rather than an exhaustion gap.
  50. The correct answer is D (Independence and objectivity under Standard I(B), since the opinion changed because of a business relationship): The rating changed because of the firm's business interest, not because of new analysis, which is exactly what Standard I(B) guards against. Loyalty does not require compromising an opinion, and the stem says disclosure was made, so the disclosure Standard is not the core problem. Diligence could be raised, but the pressure on her independent judgment is the central issue.
  51. The correct answer is A (Volume bars finish after fixed trading, so quiet periods add few bars and busy ones many): A time axis adds a bar on the clock whatever the activity, which is why the lunch hour gives twelve five-minute bars. A volume axis completes a bar only after the set number of contracts has traded, so a quiet hour may produce one. The swapped answer reverses the two definitions, and the range-bar answer describes a different construction triggered by price distance.
  52. The correct answer is A (Correlation does not establish causation, and the explained share is r squared, about 72 percent.): A correlation shows that two series move together, not that one causes the other, so her causal claim fails. The share of variation explained is r squared, and 0.85 squared is about 0.72, not 0.85. Treating r as the explained share overstates the link. One minus r does not measure explained variation. Accepting causation because the figure is strong is the very inference the strength of correlation cannot support.
  53. The correct answer is C (Traders who missed the breakout see the pullback to old resistance as a second chance.): After an upside breakout, former resistance is expected to become support because buyers who stayed out, and shorts who sold there, are drawn to buy or cover on the pullback. The breakeven-seller story describes what happens after a downside break, so it fails here. Round numbers attract attention but do not form a wall. Volume that has already traded does not leave orders behind.
  54. The correct answer is A (A strategy earns excess returns, rivals copy it, profits erode, and participants adapt toward new approaches.): The Adaptive Markets Hypothesis treats markets as an evolving population: a profitable strategy attracts imitators, competition shrinks returns, and participants then adapt toward new approaches. A permanent efficient state is the Efficient Markets view. Persistent biases describe a behavioral view of lasting mispricing. Abandoning the approach with no change leaves out the evolution the hypothesis stresses.
  55. The correct answer is B (Liquidity and many active participants, so prices reflect broad independent buying and selling.): Technical analysis assumes prices reflect the free interaction of many buyers and sellers, which needs liquidity, enough participants and reliable data. In this microcap a few small trades move price, so patterns reflect noise. Regulation, history length and low volatility all matter somewhere else, but none supplies the broad participation that chart signals rely on.
  56. The correct answer is A (Predicted gain is 3.4%, though only about 64% of ETF variation traces to the index.): Plugging X = 2.0 into the equation gives 0.4 + 1.5 x 2.0 = 3.4%, and r squared is 0.80 x 0.80 = 0.64, so 64% of the variation is explained. The 3.0% answer forgets the intercept, and 3.8% multiplies the intercept by 2. Reporting 80% treats r as the explained share, but only r squared gives that, leaving about 36% unexplained.
  57. The correct answer is B (Treat it as a likely secondary reaction and hold the core position, watching for new highs): Dow described primary movements as lasting a year or more, secondary reactions lasting weeks to months and often retracing a third to two thirds of the prior advance, and minor movements lasting under about three weeks. A five week decline retracing about a third fits a secondary reaction, so holding while watching for a new high or a lower low is sensible. It is too long to be minor, and it is not enough to call a primary reversal.
  58. The correct answer is B (It is the credit spread, and a discount price puts yield to maturity above the coupon.): The extra yield a corporate bond pays over a comparable Treasury is the credit spread. A bond priced below par offers a yield above its coupon because the buyer also earns the gain back to $1,000. Here the current yield is $50 divided by $950, about 5.3%, which is above the 5% coupon, and the yield to maturity is higher still.
  59. The correct answer is C (The disposition effect, which cuts winners short and lets losers run, reversing sound risk control): Selling a winner early while holding a loser in order to avoid realizing a loss is the disposition effect. It is the opposite of cutting losses and letting profits run. House money effect describes taking more risk after gains, not selling them, and the trader's arithmetic is wrong, since 18 percent down is a larger drop than 12 percent is a gain.
  60. The correct answer is B (A reading of 66 is an outlier, since it lies more than one IQR above Q3.): The fences are Q1 minus 18, which is 22, and Q3 plus 18, which is 70. A value of 66 is only 14 above Q3, but the rule requires 18, so it is not an outlier, which makes the statement about 66 false. The readings of 69 and 20 and the upper fence of 70 are all described correctly.
  61. The correct answer is A (The 500-stock mean is the population parameter, while the 30-stock mean is only a sample estimate.): A parameter describes an entire population and a statistic describes a sample drawn from it. The index holds 500 stocks and the first analyst measured all of them, so that average is the population parameter. The second analyst's 30 stocks are a sample, so the average is a statistic estimating it. A short time window does not turn a full population into a sample, and a sample does not become its own population.
  62. The correct answer is D (Rank stocks on past returns, buy the top group, hold for a set period, then re-rank.): Momentum tests first sort stocks by returns over a formation period, then buy the winners and hold them for a fixed holding period before repeating the sort. Buying the losers is a reversal test. Ranking on the same period that is then measured uses information not available at purchase. A variable holding rule is not a standard test design.
  63. The correct answer is D (Defenders say the pattern may be risk compensation, and anomalies often fade once widely traded): An anomaly is a pattern the pricing models cannot explain, but defenders of the EMH reply that it may be compensation for risk the model missed, and many anomalies fade once they are widely known and traded. Persistence cannot be assumed from behavior alone. Data mining is a real worry but needs evidence, and no three country rule exists.
  64. The correct answer is A (Survivorship bias, because only companies lasting to the present appear and the failures are hidden.): Survivorship bias arises when a database holds only the securities that still exist, so failed companies that a real investor would have owned are missing and average results look better than they were. Look-ahead bias is a real testing flaw, but nothing in the stem suggests future data were used. Unadjusted prices and bar length distort other things, not the missing losers.
  65. The correct answer is A (An event study, which replaces the earlier test of how quickly prices adjust to public information): Fama's revision renamed the three forms of testing as return predictability, event studies, and tests for private information. Measuring how prices respond to a public announcement is an event study, and it replaces the old semi-strong form tests. Return predictability asks whether past variables forecast returns, and private information tests examine whether insiders profit. The joint hypothesis is a problem facing every test, not a category.
  66. The correct answer is B (Investors are rational, maximize expected utility, and process available information correctly): The standard model assumes rational agents who maximize expected utility and use information correctly. Risk neutrality is a special case that ignores risk aversion. Settling for satisfactory choices is bounded rationality, a behavioral idea. Persistent drift from fundamentals conflicts with the standard model's view that rational trading corrects prices.
  67. The correct answer is A (Frame outcomes against a reference point, apply the value function and weights, then pick the best.): Prospect theory has an editing phase that frames outcomes as gains or losses relative to a reference point, followed by an evaluation phase that applies the value function and decision weights. The other sequences follow expected utility, set the reference point afterward, or code outcomes after choosing.
  68. The correct answer is C (Trades meant to mislead others about price or demand, not reflect real investment views, are manipulation): Standard II(B) covers transactions that distort price or volume with intent to mislead market participants, even when each order is legal. The colleague wants to prop up quarter end value, not act on a view of fair value. The rumor idea, front running and material nonpublic information are separate concepts, so each tempts but misses the intent to create a false price signal.
  69. The correct answer is B (Information is material if its release would probably move the price, even if uncertain): Materiality does not require certainty: information that would probably affect the price or an investor's decision is material. Breach of duty is a legal test for liability, not for materiality. The source of the tip does not decide materiality, and the Standard sets no percentage threshold. Each wrong choice accepts the trader's idea that a likely event can be treated as unimportant.
  70. The correct answer is B (Favor stocks with rising ratio lines versus the index and treat P as a funding source.): Relative strength analysis ranks securities by performance against the index, and the ratio line shows it. Stock P has risen in absolute terms but its ratio line has fallen for three months, so it is lagging and is a candidate to fund purchases of stocks with rising ratio lines. A large absolute gain does not prove leadership. Betting on laggards catching up opposes the idea that relative trends persist, and waiting for price to break support ignores the evidence already in the ratio line.
  71. The correct answer is D (OHLC bar chart, with the left tick marking the open and the right tick the close): An OHLC bar draws a vertical line from the low to the high, with a tick to the left for the open and a tick to the right for the close. A candlestick shows the same four prices but fills or hollows a real body. A line chart connects closes only, and a point-and-figure chart plots Xs and Os without a time axis.
  72. The correct answer is B (A general conclusion drawn from a few observations, with no larger sample or new data.): Four crossovers on one stock is far too small a sample to support a general rule, so the flaw is drawing a broad conclusion from a few observations without a larger sample or new data. Data mining requires a search across many rules, which she did not describe. Look-ahead bias needs information that was unavailable at the signal date, and survivorship bias needs a database that omits failed securities. Neither appears in her argument, though each is a real testing error.
  73. The correct answer is B (That the averages discount everything, since the price absorbed the news within minutes of its release.): The tenet that the averages discount everything holds that prices already reflect all known information and expectations, so a news shock is absorbed almost at once. Confirmation concerns whether the two averages reach matching highs or lows, volume is a supporting check, and persistence concerns the length of a trend. None of those explains the instant reaction to the warning.
  74. The correct answer is D (It has retraced 61.8 percent, a deep retracement that raises doubt about the downtrend): The decline is 1.3000 minus 1.1000, or 0.2000. The bounce is 1.2236 minus 1.1000, or 0.1236, and 0.1236 divided by 0.2000 equals 0.618. A 61.8 percent retracement is deep, and moving past it is read as a sign the trend is weakening. A 38.2 percent bounce would stop near 1.1764, and 50 percent is not a Fibonacci ratio. A 161.8 percent figure is an extension, which would require a move above the old high.
  75. The correct answer is C (Compute true range, average the first 14 as a seed, then smooth bars with Wilder's formula.): The standard sequence uses the greatest of the three true range measures, takes a simple average of the first 14 values as the starting ATR, then updates with prior ATR times 13 plus current true range, all divided by 14. Using high minus low drops the gap component that matters for this contract. A rolling simple average is a common variant but is not Wilder's method, and skipping the seed leaves the smoothing without a starting value.
  76. The correct answer is D (It shows positive relative strength, because the ratio line rises while the benchmark declines.): The ratio line divides the stock by the benchmark, so it rises whenever the stock performs better than the index, even if the index is falling. A rising ratio against a declining benchmark therefore shows positive relative strength. The ratio can rise while the benchmark falls, so waiting for the benchmark to turn adds nothing. An absolute trend filter such as a 200-day average answers a different question about the stock's own trend.
  77. The correct answer is D (The base currency, the U.S. dollar, weakened against the Canadian dollar, so the position lost value.): In a pair quoted as base/quote, the price states how much of the quote currency buys one unit of the base. For USD/CAD, the U.S. dollar is the base and the Canadian dollar is the quote. A fall from 1.3600 to 1.3400 means one U.S. dollar buys fewer Canadian dollars, so the base weakened and a long position loses. Swapping the currencies or reading a lower number as strength produces the incorrect conclusions.
  78. The correct answer is C (Volatility tends to revert toward its average, so a larger price move is likely ahead.): Volatility cycles alternate between compression and expansion and tend to move back toward their long-run average. A reading far below the two-year average suggests that expansion is more likely than a further decline, though not its direction. Low volatility says nothing about direction and does not imply high option prices.
  79. The correct answer is C (Bearish divergence in both RSI and OBV warns that momentum is fading, so tighten the stop.): Price made a higher high while both RSI (66 versus 74) and OBV made lower highs, a bearish divergence from two independent measures. Divergence warns that the advance is losing support but does not time a top, so tightening the stop is the measured response. Reading RSI below 70 as confirmation ignores the divergence. Exiting everything treats divergence as a timing tool, and adding ignores the warning.
  80. The correct answer is A (She must act for her employer's benefit but not at the expense of clients): Standard IV(A) asks for loyalty to the employer, but not at the expense of clients, and the Code places client interests above the member's and the employer's. Following an instruction to recommend an unsuitable fund would breach her client duties. Merely mentioning cheaper funds or obtaining a signed consent does not make an unsuitable recommendation suitable.
  81. The correct answer is B (Short about 130,000 of the ETF, since the slope says V moves 1.30 times.): The slope is the sensitivity of V's return to the ETF's return, so a 100,000 position in V behaves like 130,000 of the ETF. Using r (88,000) or r squared (about 77,000) mixes up the fit of the line with its steepness. An equal dollar hedge of 100,000 ignores that V moves more than the ETF, and some risk remains because r squared is only about 0.77.
  82. The correct answer is C (House money effect: recent gains feel less valuable and are risked freely.): Treating recent winnings as the market's money and not her own, then raising risk, is the house money effect. Loss aversion would reduce risk, the disposition effect concerns when positions are sold, and regret aversion concerns avoiding decisions that may later feel wrong.
  83. The correct answer is D (The arithmetic mean overstates growth; the geometric mean, about negative 13.4 percent, reflects the compounded loss.): The fund went from 1.00 to 1.50 to 0.75, a cumulative loss of 25 percent. The geometric mean is the square root of 0.75 minus 1, about negative 13.4 percent per year, which describes compounding correctly. The arithmetic mean of zero ignores that the second loss applies to a larger base, and negative 25 percent is the total, not the yearly rate.
  84. The correct answer is B (Confirmation bias, favoring evidence that supports an existing belief while discounting evidence that contradicts it): He is selecting and weighting information to support what he already holds, which is confirmation bias. Conservatism is the closest look-alike because both preserve a prior view, but conservatism means under-reacting to new data, not choosing which data to read. Hindsight bias concerns past outcomes, and illusion of control concerns beliefs about influence, neither of which is described.
  85. The correct answer is B (From negative 2.35 percent to positive 2.45 percent, the mean plus or minus two standard deviations.): The Empirical Rule puts about 68, 95, and 99.7 percent of values within one, two, and three standard deviations of the mean. Two standard deviations is 2.4 percent, so the range is 0.05 minus 2.4 equals negative 2.35 and 0.05 plus 2.4 equals 2.45. The one and three deviation ranges belong to the 68 and 99.7 percent coverage levels. Centering on zero is tempting but drops the stated mean.
  86. The correct answer is A (Delta changes as price moves, and gamma measures that change, so 0.30 holds near today's price.): Delta is the expected change in option price per dollar move in the stock, but it is not constant. Gamma measures how delta changes as the stock moves, so 0.30 is a good estimate only near today's price and the gain will usually accelerate for a call as it moves into the money. Vega measures sensitivity to implied volatility and theta measures time decay. Neither describes the change in delta from price movement.
  87. The correct answer is D (Momentum, where recent strong performers tend to keep outperforming over the following months.): Ranking on trailing returns and buying the leaders is the momentum strategy, whose profits contradict the weak form of the hypothesis because past prices predict future ones. The value, size, and January effects are separate anomalies built on different sorting variables: valuation ratios, market capitalization, and the calendar. Candidates mix them up because all four are cited together as evidence against efficiency.
  88. The correct answer is A (Summation: observed price movement is the combined total of every cycle acting at once.): Hurst's principle of summation says that price is the sum of cycles of different lengths, which is why the combined line looks irregular. Harmonicity concerns the numerical relationship between neighboring cycle lengths, nominality concerns typical cycle periods, and translation concerns where peaks fall within a cycle. All three are real principles but none states that cycles add together.
  89. The correct answer is A (Primary trend for the long movement and secondary reaction for the intermediate correction): In Dow Theory the primary trend is the main movement lasting a year or more, the secondary reaction is the intermediate correction lasting weeks to months, and the minor trend is the short-term fluctuation within them. Swapping those labels reverses their meaning.
  90. The correct answer is B (The market's expected 30-day volatility of the S&P 500, implied by the prices of SPX options.): The VIX is calculated from S&P 500 index option prices and expresses the volatility the market expects over the next 30 days, so it is forward looking. Realized standard deviation is historical volatility, an average range is a different statistic, and the ratio of put to call volume is a separate sentiment gauge.
  91. The correct answer is D (The list contains only survivors, so failed and delisted companies are missing and results are overstated): Using only the companies in the index today leaves out firms dropped, merged away or bankrupt, which makes the past look better than it was. That is survivorship bias. Overfitting and trading costs are real concerns but are not what the setup shows. Adjusted prices handle splits and dividends and are a standard input, not a source of look ahead bias.
  92. The correct answer is B (Volatility has contracted, and low volatility tends to be followed by a sharp expansion): Bollinger Bands widen and narrow with the standard deviation of price, so narrow bands mean contracted volatility. Bollinger observed that quiet periods tend to give way to volatile ones, but the squeeze does not say whether the breakout will be up or down.
  93. The correct answer is C (Promote the integrity and viability of the global capital markets for the ultimate benefit of society): One of the six Code components is to promote the integrity and viability of the global capital markets for society's benefit. Confidentiality is a real duty but sits in Standard III(E) rather than in the Code list. The employer-first statement reverses the Code, which puts clients and the profession first, and supervisor sign-off is not a Code component.
  94. The correct answer is D (Both statements give the same odds, so the reaction reflects framing): A 90 percent chance of keeping principal and a 10 percent chance of loss are the same fact, so a different reaction reflects framing. Anchoring, overconfidence and mental accounting are real biases, but none of them is triggered by two wordings of identical odds. The analyst is wrong to read the preference as a real difference in risk.
  95. The correct answer is C (Treat it as a runaway gap, a sign the trend is likely to continue.): A runaway gap forms in the middle of a strong move and is usually followed by more of the same trend. An exhaustion gap comes near the end of a move and is often followed by stalling, and a breakaway gap leaves a base at the start of a trend. Common gaps form within a range and tend to fill.
  96. The correct answer is D (Expected utility theory has investors judge outcomes as gains and losses measured from a reference point): Judging outcomes as gains and losses from a reference point is prospect theory, a behavioral idea, while expected utility theory looks at final wealth. The other three are accurate: traditional models rely on arbitrage, framing effects are behavioral, and traditional theory treats money as fungible across mental accounts.
  97. The correct answer is C (It confuses absolute trend with relative trend: K lagged the index, so its ratio line fell.): Relative strength compares a security with a benchmark. K rose 6 percent while the index rose 10 percent, so K lagged and its ratio line fell even though K is in an absolute uptrend. The analyst confuses absolute trend with relative trend. A quarterly comparison is perfectly usable. A dividend difference of that size would not close a four point gap, and a broad index is a legitimate benchmark, though a sector comparison can add a second view.
  98. The correct answer is A (A double top breakout, a buy signal because the X column exceeds the earlier X high): A double top breakout occurs when an X column rises above the high of the preceding X column, and it is a buy signal. The chart shows only two X columns near the level, so a triple top breakout or a catapult, which build on three tops, is not what is drawn. A double top that stalls at an equal high would be a sell, but this column climbed past the earlier high.
  99. The correct answer is D (Waiting for both averages to confirm screens out false moves but misses the early trend.): Confirmation by two averages reduces whipsaws, but it also means the signal comes after a good share of the move, which is the standard criticism of lag. Dow theory rests on closing prices, so using intraday highs adds noise rather than safety. The theory has no volume doubling or retest requirement before a signal.
  100. The correct answer is C (The theory says prices reflect available information, not later-revealed value): The EMH claims prices reflect available information so investors cannot consistently earn excess risk adjusted returns, not that prices equal later revealed value. A bubble that cannot be reliably exploited in advance is not decisive evidence against it. The sample size point, the weak form claim and quick arbitrage each tempt but miss that the investor confused available information with later value.
  101. The correct answer is D (Open interest equals the number of contracts traded during the day, including those opened and closed.): The false statement describes volume. Open interest is the number of outstanding contracts not yet closed or delivered, and it rises only when new positions are opened. The other three statements are correct, including the data challenge created by splicing contract months into one continuous chart.
  102. The correct answer is A (Loss aversion is losses outweighing equal gains; risk aversion prefers a sure amount to a gamble.): Loss aversion says a loss is felt more strongly than an equal gain, which explains rejecting the fair coin flip. Risk aversion is the preference for a sure outcome over a gamble with the same expected value, and prospect theory says it holds in gains while risk seeking appears in losses. The two ideas overlap in behavior but are different concepts.
  103. The correct answer is B (A gain of about 300 dollars, found as 0.40 times 1.50 times 500 shares of exposure.): Delta estimates the option's price change per 1 dollar move in the stock. The per share change is 0.40 times 1.50, or 0.60 dollars. Five contracts cover 500 shares, so the position changes by about 300 dollars. Treating delta as one overstates the gain, counting one contract understates it, and dividing by delta inverts the relationship.
  104. The correct answer is B (Treat the spike as a liquidity effect and rely on the ETF chart instead): In a thin market one large order can create a spike that looks like a signal but only reflects a lack of buyers or sellers. Liquidity is a requirement for chart reading to be reliable, and the heavily traded ETF meets it. Reading the spike as a breakout ignores that problem. A shorter interval adds bars but not participants, and adjusting for splits and dividends corrects data, not a spike caused by thin trading.
  105. The correct answer is A (Distribution: informed holders sell into strong demand, so markdown likely follows.): Wyckoff's distribution phase follows a markup and occurs when strong hands sell to a public that is eager to buy, which shows up as heavy volume without further price progress. Accumulation is the equivalent base at lows. Markup features rising prices, and markdown requires price to be falling, not stalling.
  106. The correct answer is A (The price falls about 4.0 percent, to roughly 96, as yields and prices move oppositely.): Approximate price change is minus modified duration times yield change: -8.0 x 0.50% = -4.0%, so the price moves from 100 to about 96. A rising yield means a falling price, so the rise answer is reversed. The 0.5% answer ignores duration, and 8.0% applies the duration to a 1.00 point move rather than the actual half point.
  107. The correct answer is C (Anchoring and adjustment, where the estimate starts from last year's figure and is adjusted too little.): The forecast starts at the prior figure of 2.40 and moves only 0.15 toward the 3.10 guidance, which is the signature of anchoring and adjustment, an information processing bias. Framing would involve a change in presentation, availability would involve recent vivid events, and mental accounting would involve separating funds into categories. None of those is described in the facts.
  108. The correct answer is D (A 20-week cycle, with two of them fitting inside each dominant 40-week cycle): Harmonicity says neighboring cycles are related by a factor of about two. Dividing 40 weeks by two gives a 20-week cycle, and two of them make up one dominant cycle. A 10-week cycle would be two levels down. An 80-week cycle is the next longer cycle, not the next shorter, and 13 weeks uses a ratio of three, which Hurst did not give as the neighboring relation.
  109. The correct answer is C (The 70 percent win rate guarantees positive expectancy, so the system will be profitable.): Expectancy equals the win rate times the average win minus the loss rate times the average loss: 140 minus 180, or negative 40 dollars. A high win rate does not make a system profitable when the average loss is three times the average win. At 80 percent the figure becomes 160 minus 120, or positive 40, so the other statements are true.
  110. The correct answer is A (At 2 to 1, traders must win over half of trades to break even): Breakeven win rate equals risk divided by total of risk plus reward. At 2 to 1 that is 1/3, about 33 percent, not more than half, so the statement about 2 to 1 is false. At 3 to 1 the figure is 1/4, which matches the statement about 3 to 1. ATR stops widen with volatility, and a 3 percent filter does trade fewer false signals for later entries.
  111. The correct answer is A (Volume is the primary confirmation, so heavy volume can validate a trend the averages disagree on): Dow made the two averages confirming each other the primary test, and volume only a secondary check, so volume cannot validate a move the averages do not confirm. The other statements are consistent with the theory: secondary reactions typically retrace a portion of the primary move, a line can stand in for a reaction, and signals use closing prices.
  112. The correct answer is C (A bearish reversal signal on heavy volume, so reduce exposure or tighten the stop.): A bearish engulfing pattern at the end of an advance shows sellers overwhelming buyers, and record volume adds weight to the signal. The sensible response is to cut risk and look for confirmation. Calling it absorption or neutral ignores where it formed, and a larger body is bullish only when the second candle is white after a decline.
  113. The correct answer is C (A futures contract on crude oil, standardized, exchange traded, margined, and settled daily until expiration.): Standardization, an exchange, margin, and daily mark-to-market are the defining features of a futures contract. A forward is customized and settled once at maturity. An oil company ETF holds equities, which the technician ruled out. An option buyer pays a premium and has no daily settlement obligation, so it differs in structure even though it also expires.
  114. The correct answer is A (The horizontal axis marks equal intervals of time, so each column represents one trading day.): A point and figure chart has no time axis. Columns appear only when price changes enough, so one column can span many days or a single day. The other three claims are correct: a 30 point reversal is needed to start a new column, the count method derives targets from the number of columns, and moves smaller than a box leave no mark. Each looks unusual compared with time based charts, but each is true.
  115. The correct answer is C (38.2 percent, since the pullback of 7.64 points is 0.382 of the 20-point advance.): The advance is 60 minus 40, or 20 points, and the pullback is 60 minus 52.36, or 7.64 points. Dividing gives 0.382. The 61.8 percent figure comes from (52.36 - 40) / 20, which measures what is left of the advance. A 23.6 percent pullback would end at 55.28 and a 50 percent pullback at 50.
  116. The correct answer is A (Extreme returns occur more often than a normal curve implies, so standard-deviation stops understate tail risk): The chart shows a distribution with fatter tails than a normal curve, so moves beyond three standard deviations are more frequent than the model assumed. Stops sized from normal-curve standard deviations therefore understate tail risk. Right skew would shift weight to one side, not fatten both tails or explain repeated deep losses. Clustering describes timing, not the shape of the distribution, and repeated large losses are evidence of fat tails, not coincidence.
  117. The correct answer is A (The geometric mean, because compounding links the years and this rate reproduces the ending value.): Wealth builds by multiplying (1 + return) each year, so the single rate that reproduces ending wealth is the geometric mean. With returns as dispersed as -30% and +45%, the arithmetic mean overstates compounded growth. The median and mode describe typical or common years and do not tie back to ending wealth.
  118. The correct answer is B (One dealer sets the prices instead of many competing participants, so prices ignore supply and demand.): Technical analysis assumes that prices come from free price discovery, where many buyers and sellers compete. A single bank setting quotes breaks that assumption, and more frequent trading would not repair it. Thin trading is a liquidity problem, which is real but is not the shortcoming that the stem says persists even with frequent trades.
  119. The correct answer is D (Japanese rice traders developed candlesticks in the 1700s; Nison introduced them to the West in 1991.): Candlestick charting traces to 18th century Japanese rice trading, with Munehisa Homma often credited for early work, and Nison's 1991 book brought it to the West. Dow's work is separate and centered on the two averages. The Chinese origin and the 1971 date are close enough to sound right but do not match the record.
  120. The correct answer is B (Confirmation bias: he seeks and favors evidence supporting his view while discounting the divergence.): Reading only supportive research and waving away disconfirming volume evidence is the signature of confirmation bias. Conservatism is a defensible neighbor, because both are belief perseverance biases, but he raises his target on one headline, which is not slow updating. The stem gives no hint about similarity to past setups or a belief in control.
  121. The correct answer is C ($6,000, which is 20 percent of the first-year management fee of $30,000): The referral fee is 20 percent of $30,000, which is $6,000. The $24,000 figure is the share the manager retains. The $9,000 figure applies a 30 percent rate instead of the stated 20 percent. Dividing by 20 percent inflates the fee by a factor of five instead of reducing it. Standard VI(C) requires the adviser to disclose the arrangement before the client signs.
  122. The correct answer is C (About 6.9 percent in either direction, one standard deviation over a month.): The square root of 12 is about 3.46, and 24.0 / 3.46 is about 6.9. Annualized volatility scales with the square root of time, so dividing by 12 gives only 2.0, and dividing by the square root of 252 (about 15.9) gives 1.5, a one-day figure. Using 24 directly treats the annual number as a monthly one.
  123. The correct answer is B (The weak form holds, but the strong form is rejected because private information pays): The weak form says past prices give no edge, and the study found none. The strong form says even private information is in prices, and insider profits contradict that. The study says nothing about public fundamental analysis, so the semi-strong form is not tested by this evidence. Proposed by Eugene Fama, the three forms are nested by information set.
  124. The correct answer is C (The z-score is 2.0, found by subtracting the mean and dividing by the standard deviation.): A z-score is the value minus the mean, divided by the standard deviation. Here 4.5 minus 0.5 equals 4.0, and 4.0 divided by 2.0 equals 2.0. Leaving out the mean gives 2.25, stopping before the division gives 4.0, and using the variance of 4.0 as the divisor gives 1.0. Only 2.0 follows the definition.
  125. The correct answer is B (Both confirm on a break below the lowest trough, but the triple top shows three peaks): Both patterns are reversals that confirm only when price breaks below the lowest low between the peaks, and the difference is the number of failed attempts, two against three. Confirmation never comes at a peak, because a peak could still be broken. Both are reversal patterns, and both measure the target from the height of the pattern projected from the broken support.
  126. The correct answer is A (Dow left editorials, Hamilton extended them, and Rhea later organized the ideas into a systematic theory.): Dow built the averages and wrote editorials but never produced a complete treatise. Hamilton extended the ideas, and Rhea later organized them into a systematic theory. A reversed order of contributors, a swap of Dow and Rhea, or a finished book by Dow each tempts a student who half remembers the names, yet each gives the wrong person the wrong job, so the analyst's claim still fails.
  127. The correct answer is C (Note the extremes, wait for a trendline break, then act with a defined stop.): Sentiment extremes mark conditions where a turn becomes likely, but timing is poor, so contrarians wait for price evidence such as a channel break before acting, and set a stop to define the risk. Shorting at once ignores timing, waiting for neutrality discards the contrarian edge, and buying the bullish extreme joins the crowd at the point of greatest risk.
  128. The correct answer is B (Traders expect larger swings ahead than recent trading showed, though the figure gives no direction.): Historical volatility measures how much prices actually varied in a past window, while implied volatility is the market's forward expectation backed out of option prices. An implied figure well above the historical one, ahead of earnings, shows traders expect larger moves, in either direction. Implied volatility carries no directional forecast. The two measures need not match, because one looks back and the other looks ahead.
  129. The correct answer is C (On-balance volume is a momentum oscillator that stays bounded between 0 and 100, like RSI.): On balance volume is a cumulative running total of volume added on up days and subtracted on down days, so it is unbounded and is not an oscillator limited to 0 to 100. The other three statements are standard: the histogram plots the MACD line minus its signal line, RSI is built from average gains and losses and stays bounded, and a high stochastic reading places the close near the top of its range.
  130. The correct answer is B (Non-confirmation, because the Transportation Average failed to exceed its prior high): Dow required both averages to exceed their prior highs for a signal to be confirmed. The Industrial Average rose 300 points above its high, but the Transportation Average closed 170 points short of its own, so the move is not confirmed. Confirmation needs both averages. Non-confirmation is a warning, not proof of a reversal, and the size of percentage differences does not define a secondary trend.
  131. The correct answer is B (Volume must confirm the trend, and it is expanding on declines rather than advances): In a healthy uptrend, volume should expand on advances and contract on pullbacks. Lower volume on the new high with heavier volume on the pullback fails the volume tenet and counts as a warning. The discounting tenet does not tell the analyst to ignore volume. Trend persistence says a trend lasts until a definite reversal, but a volume warning is evidence to watch. The second average in confirmation is the Transportation Average, not volume.
  132. The correct answer is D (Unadjusted prices, since they show the levels that actually traded at the time): Adjusted data divides earlier prices by the split ratio, which suits trend and return studies, but traders at the time saw 100, not 50, so unadjusted prices are the right choice for round-number behavior. Volume is adjusted the opposite way, multiplied by 2, so it does not stay unchanged. Unadjusted data does not handle dividends, so the claim that it handles dividends mixes up the two data types.
  133. The correct answer is B (The distance is subtracted from the neckline, since a top projects a decline): The standard measuring rule takes the vertical distance from the head to the neckline and projects it from the neckline in the direction of the break. For a top, that direction is down, so adding the distance gives a level above the pattern and contradicts the bearish break. Starting at the head peak, using the left shoulder low or waiting for a pullback are common confusions, but none of them is part of the usual rule.
  134. The correct answer is B (Harmonicity relates neighboring cycles by small whole numbers; nominality means similar lengths appear across many markets.): Harmonicity is the relationship between neighboring cycles, usually a ratio of two or three, while nominality is the observation that cycles of similar approximate lengths are found in many markets. Summation covers cycles adding together and translation covers where a peak sits within a cycle. The other choices borrow the wrong name, swap the definitions or attach the wrong feature to nominality.
  135. The correct answer is A (A covered call, collecting premium in exchange for capping gains on the shares above 90.): She owns 500 shares and sold a call against them, which is a covered call. She keeps the premium if the stock stays below 90, and her gain on the shares is capped above 90. A collar needs a purchased put for downside protection, which she does not hold. A cash secured put sells a put, not a call. A short straddle sells both a call and a put with no shares involved.
  136. The correct answer is C (Conservatism, holding to a prior view and under-reacting to new information.): Conservatism is a belief perseverance bias in which a person clings to an initial view and revises it too little when new evidence arrives. Availability bias concerns which information comes to mind first, illusion of control concerns perceived influence over results, and self-attribution concerns who gets credit for outcomes. Each is a real bias that sounds related, but none describes a small nudge to a target after sharp forecast cuts.
  137. The correct answer is D (Any test of efficiency also tests the pricing model behind expected returns, which may be wrong.): To call a return abnormal a researcher needs a model of normal expected returns, so any efficiency test is a joint test of efficiency and that model. A 3 percent gap over CAPM could mean prices are inefficient or that CAPM misstates risk. Data mining is a fair caution, and CAPM is often criticized, but the stated flaws are different problems, and CAPM prices systematic risk rather than total risk.
  138. The correct answer is C (Adjacent terms approach 0.618, the reciprocal of 1.618; terms two apart approach its square, 0.382.): The ratio of a Fibonacci number to the next one approaches 0.618, which is the reciprocal of the Golden Ratio of 1.618. Skipping one term gives 0.618 squared, or 0.382, and skipping two gives 0.236. The ratios therefore come from the arithmetic of the sequence and not from fitting past price swings.
  139. The correct answer is D (Candlesticks, because they hold the same four prices and shade the open-to-close body visibly): OHLC bars and candlesticks contain exactly the same four prices. Candlesticks make the open-to-close relationship obvious through the shaded body, which is what the trader wants to scan quickly. Bars do not hold extra data, a close-only line omits the open and range, and a candle body shows price, not volume.
  140. The correct answer is C (Define the event, set an expected-return model, compute abnormal returns, then test them against zero.): The model of normal returns must be fixed before measuring abnormal returns, otherwise the benchmark can be bent to fit the answer. The event comes first because abnormal returns are measured around it, and the significance test comes last. Testing raw returns against zero ignores compensation for risk, and a model chosen after the fact makes the efficiency conclusion meaningless.
  141. The correct answer is B (ADX measures trend strength regardless of direction, so 12 suggests a weak or absent trend.): ADX is derived from the spread between the DI lines but expresses only how strong a trend is, not which way it points. A low ADX such as 12 signals little trend even though +DI exceeds -DI. The direction comes from the relationship of +DI and -DI. The 70 and 30 zones belong to RSI, not ADX.
  142. The correct answer is C (A secondary trend, or wave, and minor trends, or ripples): A decline lasting about three weeks to three months that retraces roughly one third to two thirds of the prior advance is a secondary trend, called a wave. The small swings lasting under about three weeks are minor trends, called ripples. The primary trend, the tide, normally lasts more than a year. Labeling the decline primary or minor misses its duration, and calling a secondary movement a tide confuses the names.
  143. The correct answer is D (Anchoring and adjustment: estimates start at the purchase price and move too little.): Her estimates begin at the purchase price of 60 and move only a few points per report, which is the signature of anchoring and adjustment. Loss aversion, confirmation bias and regret avoidance are all real tendencies a loss holder may show, so each tempts, but none explains why the numeric estimate stays tied to the original price while downgrades keep arriving.

Practice Test 2 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is C (Sentiment is stretched optimistic, so the market is vulnerable, though timing is uncertain): Contrary opinion treats extreme optimism as a warning, because most buyers are already fully invested. A low put/call ratio means few puts are being bought, mutual fund cash near record lows means little buying power is left, and a 78% bullish poll shows a crowded view. Reading low cash as dry powder or a low put/call ratio as hedging gets both measures backward, and none of them gives precise timing.
  2. The correct answer is C (The total number of outstanding contracts that have not yet been closed out or delivered): Open interest is the number of contracts still outstanding that have not been offset or delivered. Each contract has one long and one short side, so it is counted once, not twice. Volume counts contracts traded in a session, which tempts because it appears beside open interest on a quote page. New contracts opened during the day change open interest only after closings are netted against them.
  3. The correct answer is D (It filters small fluctuations so supply and demand shifts stand out without time noise.): Plotting only full-box moves removes minor price changes and the passage of time, which leaves a clean picture of supply and demand. Standard point-and-figure charts carry no volume data and have no fixed time scale per column. The filtering is a threshold on price change, not a moving average, even though both reduce noise.
  4. The correct answer is B ($46.80, from the peer median multiple of 13 times forecast EPS of $3.60): The multiples sorted are 11, 12, 13, 14 and 18, so the median is 13 and 13 times $3.60 equals $46.80. The mean is 13.6, giving $48.96, but the policy and the outlier call for the median. Using 18 gives $64.80 and using 11 gives $39.60. A target built on an acquisition-premium multiple lacks a reasonable basis.
  5. The correct answer is C (Point C, the top where informed holders sell to eager late buyers.): The cycle runs from accumulation (A) through markup (B) and distribution (C) to markdown (D). Herding peaks near the top, where informed owners sell to the public. Accumulation is quiet buying by informed investors, markup is trend following, and markdown is the panic selling that follows distribution.
  6. The correct answer is A (Equal percentage changes take equal vertical distance, as investors judge returns.): On a logarithmic axis a move from 10 to 20 takes the same vertical space as a move from 40 to 80, because both are 100% gains. This matches how investors think about returns. The arithmetic scale gives equal dollar changes equal distance. A log axis does not convert data into returns or change the weight of recent data, it only changes how the axis spaces the same prices.
  7. The correct answer is C (Trading on nonpublic merger news could not earn abnormal returns, because prices already reflect inside information.): Semi-strong form says prices reflect all public information, so public filings and price history offer no consistent edge. Inside information is not public, so insiders could still earn abnormal returns under this form. Only the strong form claims that even nonpublic information is already in the price, which makes the merger statement the one that does not belong.
  8. The correct answer is B (Accumulation at the base, markup, distribution at the top, then markdown): A full cycle runs from accumulation at a base, to markup as the trend advances, to distribution at a top where supply takes over, and then to markdown. Buying at the end of accumulation and selling at the start of distribution fits that order. The other options start in the wrong place or swap adjacent phases.
  9. The correct answer is C (Accept that the feeling will persist and build the plan around it, since education rarely helps.): Emotional biases such as endowment and status quo come from feelings, not faulty reasoning, so more facts seldom correct them and the adviser is better off adapting the plan. Overriding the client violates her preferences, and waiting for a recovery to the inherited value anchors the decision to the very reference point driving the problem.
  10. The correct answer is D (State a hypothesis, define exact rules, test on history, then confirm on separate out-of-sample data.): The scientific method starts with a hypothesis, then turns it into exact rules, tests those rules on history, and finally confirms the result on data the rules had not seen. Reversing the first two steps lets the rules shape the story. Re-testing on the same history cannot confirm anything because the data already guided the refinements. Refining on the full history after the out-of-sample test contaminates the sample that was meant to stay clean.
  11. The correct answer is D (They show the open, high and low as well as the close, revealing range and conviction.): A line chart connects only closing prices, while bar and candlestick charts add the open, high and low, so each period reveals its range and where the close sits within it. Tick charts record each transaction, volume is a separate series plotted beneath price, and bars show the actual high and low rather than an average of them. Each wrong choice describes a different tool.
  12. The correct answer is A (Compute the 20-period SMA, measure its standard deviation, then add and subtract twice that figure): Bollinger Bands start with a 20-period simple moving average as the middle band, then use the standard deviation of the same 20 closes, and place the outer bands two standard deviations above and below the average. The standard deviation is what makes the bands widen with volatility. Keltner Channels use an exponential average and the average true range, a percentage envelope uses a fixed percent, and adding to the close skips the middle band.
  13.
