Using 60 percent adds $18 and gives $68, which is the share she did not close. Closing the full gap gives $80 with no anchoring. Subtracting 40 percent of the gap would give $38, and moving 60 percent of the gap downward gives $32.
  14. The correct answer is B (Either the market is inefficient or CAPM is a flawed benchmark, and the test cannot say): Every efficiency test is also a test of the model used for expected returns, which is the joint hypothesis problem. A 4 percent excess return over CAPM could mean mispricing or that CAPM misstates risk, so the study cannot separate the two. A long sample, a claim that CAPM is broken, or a weak form label each picks one side without evidence that rules out the other.
  15. The correct answer is C (Dragonfly doji: sellers drove price down, but buyers recovered it, hinting at a bottom.): The circled candle has its open, close and high together at the top of the range with a long lower shadow, which is a dragonfly doji. Sellers pushed price far lower during the session, yet buyers brought it back to the open, so after a decline it hints at a possible bottom that still needs confirmation. The gravestone is the mirror image with the long shadow above, the long-legged doji has long shadows on both sides, and a spinning top has a visible small real body.
  16. The correct answer is B (Leptokurtic, meaning excess kurtosis with a higher peak and fatter tails than a normal distribution.): A taller peak with more extreme observations than a normal curve is leptokurtic, meaning positive excess kurtosis. Matching the mean and standard deviation does not make the shape normal, because kurtosis describes the tails and peak separately. Skewness measures asymmetry, not tail weight. Heteroskedasticity describes variance changing across time, which can produce fat tails but is a different concept from the shape described.
  17. The correct answer is C (Refrain from trading or sharing, report what she heard to compliance, and follow its direction): With material nonpublic information, the analyst should not act on it or pass it on, and she should bring it to compliance so the firm can set up proper restrictions. Telling the portfolio manager spreads the information, and calling the issuer to confirm it adds a second leak. Trading for clients still uses the information improperly.
  18. The correct answer is B (Bullish engulfing, a potential bottom as buyers overpower sellers): A bullish engulfing pattern is a white real body that completely covers the preceding black real body after a decline, showing buyers overpowering sellers. A harami reverses that relationship, with the second body inside the first. A piercing line closes only above the midpoint of the prior body, and a tweezer bottom is defined by matching lows.
  19. The correct answer is A (A rally continuing on steadily shrinking volume confirms that the uptrend is growing stronger.): Volume should expand in the direction of the trend, so a rally that proceeds on shrinking volume is showing weaker participation, a warning rather than a confirmation. Rising prices on rising volume, suspicion of a light volume breakout and rising on balance volume with price are all standard confirmation ideas and true. The false statement is the one that calls shrinking volume a sign of strength.
  20. The correct answer is A (Since investors learn from past bubbles, blow-off tops cannot recur once a market has experienced one): Bubbles and crashes have recurred through history because the emotions and herding that drive them are part of human behavior, so a blow-off top can form again. The other three statements are standard: herding can stretch trends beyond fair value, anchoring can make prior price levels matter, and biases help produce the persistent trends technicians study. The false statement is the one claiming learning prevents recurrence.
  21. The correct answer is C (In an uptrend, each reaction low is lower than the last while highs keep rising.): An uptrend is defined by higher highs and higher lows, so reaction lows rise rather than fall; lower lows would break the definition. The remaining statements are standard teaching: Dow described primary trends lasting a year or more with secondary reactions inside, trendlines under rising lows mark support and a break warns of change, and trends are presumed to persist until signals say otherwise.
  22. The correct answer is B (Both lows are due in about 7 weeks, and summation suggests a deeper, more significant low.): The 20-week cycle has 7 weeks left (20 minus 13), and the 10-week cycle also has 7 weeks left (10 minus 3), so the two bottoms are due together. The 10-week period is half the 20-week period, which is the harmonic relationship. Under the principle of summation, cycles combine into the observed price movement, so coinciding lows tend to produce a more notable low.
  23. The correct answer is B (Wait for stronger volume or a follow-through close, because breakouts this near the apex often fail): A symmetrical triangle usually breaks out about two thirds to three quarters of the way to the apex, and a break close to the apex carries less force. Below-average volume adds to the doubt, so waiting for volume or follow-through is the best response. The prior uptrend gives only a mild upside bias, a short sale lacks any downside break, and a 1% close on light volume is not strong confirmation.
  24. The correct answer is A (Consider partial profits, but the uptrend stays intact until the lower channel line breaks): In a channel, the upper line is a natural place to trim, while the lower line is the one whose violation warns of a trend change. A touch of the upper line followed by a pullback is a reason to tighten or take partial profits, not a reversal. Adding would require a close above the line, and a bounce off a channel line confirms the channel rather than invalidating it.
  25. The correct answer is B (Rationality is only a modeling assumption, and documented behavior such as loss aversion departs from it.): Rationality is a modeling assumption, and documented behavior such as loss aversion and herding departs from it in repeated ways, which can leave patterns in prices. Offsetting errors, a narrow reading of utility theory, or the claim that behavioral finance adds nothing are arguments a student might make, yet none shows the analyst's inference from rational models to meaningless charts is unsound.
  26. The correct answer is C (Behavioral finance, since conservatism and slow reactions to news can make prices adjust gradually): Traditional economics assumes rational agents who incorporate news immediately, which leaves little room for a trend built from small news steps. Behavioral finance allows biases such as conservatism, which cause investors to under-react and prices to adjust gradually. A risk premium story is possible but does not fit the stated pattern. Expected utility theory does not predict that winners are sold quickly, and calling the trend bad data is circular.
  27. The correct answer is C (About 95 percent of days, since the range spans two standard deviations each side.): The Empirical Rule says about 68 percent of values fall within one standard deviation, 95 percent within two and 99.7 percent within three. A range of plus or minus 2 percent equals two standard deviations here. Half of that, 47.5 percent, covers only the positive side. The 68 and 99.7 figures belong to one and three deviations.
  28. The correct answer is B (Volume rises by one contract and open interest also rises by one contract): A trade between a new buyer and a new seller creates one new contract, so it adds one to volume and one to open interest. Open interest stays flat only when an existing holder passes a position to a new one, and it falls when both sides close. Volume always rises with each contract traded.
  29. The correct answer is C (Volatility cannot cluster, because random direction rules out any predictable pattern in the size of moves.): No serial correlation in changes means direction is hard to predict, and a martingale makes today's price the best forecast. It says nothing about the size of moves, and volatility can cluster in a series whose direction is unpredictable. The conclusion that volatility cannot cluster stretches randomness beyond what the test measured.

Trade two risks 6.00 to gain 12.00, a ratio of 2.0. Measuring reward from the stop inflates both ratios, dividing risk by reward inverts the ranking, and comparing raw reward ignores how much is risked.
  31. The correct answer is D (Prices reflect the collective knowledge and expectations of all participants, even for unknown events): Dow argued that the averages absorb everything known and expected by buyers and sellers, including events nobody can foresee, so price is the summary of all information. The barometer idea, the role of insiders, and the confirmation principle are all parts of Dow's thinking, which makes them tempting, but none of them is the reason the theory gives for discounting everything.
  32. The correct answer is B (About 6.9 percent, found by dividing 24 by the square root of 12.): VIX is an annualized figure, and volatility grows with the square root of time. For one month divide by the square root of 12, which is about 3.46, giving 24 divided by 3.46, or 6.9 percent. Dividing by 12 treats volatility as linear in time. Dividing by the square roots of 252 or 52 would give one day or one week estimates instead.
  33. The correct answer is B (A model tuned to earn the highest past return has the best chance of repeating it.): The false claim is that a model tuned to earn the highest past return has the best chance of repeating it. Tuning to the best past result usually fits noise, so performance tends to fall afterward. The other claims are true. Fewer parameters leave less room to fit noise, filling at the same close that triggered the signal uses unavailable information, and a signal can predict moves yet lose money after trading costs.

A hold at a Fibonacci level is only a favorable sign and never proof of a new high. The claims of invalidity, excessive depth, or the wrong tool are tempting but contradict what the chart shows.

Free price discovery is about controls on price formation, and reliable data is about the record itself. Too few participants is related and tempting, but liquidity is the most direct description of what the stem shows.

  37. The correct answer is A (Conservatism leads investors to react too strongly to news, dropping prior forecasts after one report.): Conservatism is the opposite of overreaction: investors cling to a prior view and update too slowly. The other three descriptions are standard. Confirmation seeks supporting evidence, representativeness uses similarity in place of base rates, and hindsight makes past events seem predictable.
  38. The correct answer is D (Series 1 shows excess kurtosis (fat tails); series 2 shows positive skew.): Kurtosis describes tail weight on both sides of the distribution, so frequent large moves in both directions mean fat tails in series 1. Skew describes asymmetry, and a long right tail with most values slightly negative is positive skew. The other pairings swap the terms or call a one-sided tail a general fat-tail condition.
  39. The correct answer is C (A buy signal, because recent prices are now rising faster than the longer-term 50 bar average): In the standard two moving average system, the shorter average crossing above the longer one is a buy signal, because recent prices are averaging higher than the longer history. Crossing below is the sell signal, and the shorter average is the one that must cross, not the longer. Extra filters such as a 200 bar average can be added, but they are not part of the basic rule, and a crossover is not read as exhaustion.
  40. The correct answer is B (The range is 1.05, from the low to the high; the open-to-close change is 0.45.): Range is the high minus the low: 26.10 minus 25.05 equals 1.05. The open-to-close change is the close minus the open: 25.85 minus 25.40 equals 0.45. Swapping them confuses the whole session span with the real body. The 0.70 figure measures from the open to the high, and 0.80 measures from the low to the close, so neither describes the range or the open-to-close change.
  41. The remedy is a database that includes delisted names so failures count. A longer period from the same database still lacks them. Many delisted firms ranked well before collapsing, so the top decile restriction does not help. An out of sample window drawn from the same survivor database carries the same bias.

Because both stocks have similar prices, a $4.20 figure against $1.10 shows the first stock's closes are more dispersed and therefore more volatile. The measure carries no direction, so it cannot show an uptrend. It is also not a daily range, which is an average true range idea. A candlestick adds the open, high and low: the range is 86 minus 77, or 9 points, while the real body is just 0.2, showing a violent session that ended almost unchanged, which signals indecision.   44. The correct answer is D (Implied volatility comes from past closing prices of the underlying, so it only looks backward.): Implied volatility is derived from current option prices using an option model, so it reflects the market's expectation about the future and is not computed from past closes. Historical volatility is the backward-looking measure. Clustering and the equity put skew are both standard observations.
  45. The correct answer is C (Herding, copying the crowd instead of own analysis, which can inflate a bubble): Buying because others are buying, with no independent analysis, is herding, and when it feeds on itself it can carry prices far above value and inflate a bubble. Overconfidence, extrapolation and regret aversion are real biases that may also appear in a rally, but the buyers here describe copying the crowd, so none of them names the behavior the stem reports.
  46. The correct answer is A (Volatility cycles: calm periods breed expansion, and a breakout with widening bands marks strength, not exhaustion.): Bollinger observed that volatility alternates between quiet and active phases, so a period of contraction often precedes expansion. The bands themselves do not give direction, but a breakout in which the bands widen shows that the new volatility is accompanied by price strength. A touch of the outer band is not an automatic sell signal, narrow bands show low volatility rather than a finished trend, and band width is calculated from price standard deviation, not from volume.
  47. The correct answer is A (Narrowing participation is a negative divergence, leaving the advance vulnerable despite new index highs.): A rising index with a falling advance decline line and growing new lows shows fewer stocks supporting the advance, a negative divergence that often precedes weakness. Breadth is a confirming tool, and expecting it to catch up ignores the warning. Expanding new lows at highs are a sign of deteriorating participation, not healthy rotation, and a divergence signals vulnerability but not the exact timing for a short sale.
  48. The correct answer is B (250 shares, since 500 dollars of risk divided by the 2 dollar stop distance gives size.): Fixed fractional position sizing risks a set percentage of equity on each trade. One percent of 50,000 is 500 dollars. The stop is 2 dollars below entry, so shares equal 500 divided by 2, or 250, and the maximum loss at the stop is 500 dollars. A round lot ignores the risk budget, and a percentage of price is not the stop distance.
  49. The correct answer is C (Conservatism, where a prior view is updated too slowly when new information arrives.): The analyst does not reject the new data, she just moves her estimate too little, which defines conservatism. Confirmation bias would show up as seeking or favoring supportive reports. Representativeness is a classification shortcut and hindsight bias concerns memory of what was foreseen. The four are grouped as belief perseverance biases, which is why candidates confuse them.
  50. The middle candle shows buyers losing control, and the third shows sellers taking over. The morning star is the bullish mirror at the end of a decline. Dark cloud cover has only two candles and no star, and three black crows needs three consecutive long black candles.
  51. The return fell from 18 to 4 percent, a drop of 14 points or 14 divided by 18, about 78 percent, and 4 percent trails buy and hold at 9. A positive result is not an edge when it lags the benchmark, and the drop is classic overfitting.
  52. The correct answer is B (A long sideways range after a decline fits accumulation, the base phase, not a markdown.): Wyckoff's four phases are accumulation, markup, distribution and markdown. A prolonged sideways range after a decline is the base, accumulation, where supply is being absorbed. Markdown is an active downtrend, not a flat range. Distribution forms near highs after an advance, so it does not fit a base after a decline. Her pullback term is correct for downside breaks, so it is not the flaw.
  53. The correct answer is A (Close at 116.20, beyond both the 3.6-point and the 3.0-point thresholds): The 3 percent filter is 0.03 times 120, or 3.6 points, so the close must be below 116.40. The ATR filter is 1.5 times 2.0, or 3.0 points, so the close must be below 117.00. Only 116.20 meets both; 116.80 meets the ATR filter only, and the other two closes meet neither.
  54. The correct answer is A (Ask a colleague to build the strongest bearish case and list evidence refuting her thesis.): Belief perseverance is countered by seeking disconfirming evidence, and a colleague building the strongest bearish case does exactly that. Rereading the memo strengthens the thesis, halving the position manages risk but leaves the belief untouched, and dismissing two reports as too few is conservatism, the habit she needs to correct. Each sounds prudent yet none tests the thesis.
  55. The correct answer is B (Adjust returns for risk, test on out-of-sample data, then subtract trading costs before calling it exploitable.): An apparent anomaly may reflect extra risk, chance, or costs. The researcher should first adjust for risk, then test on data not used to find the pattern, and finally deduct realistic trading costs. Tuning parameters to the sample, reusing the same data, and ignoring risk all invite false positives and overstate the profit that a real investor could capture.

Confirmation bias would involve ignoring or discounting the new data, not partially absorbing it. Representativeness concerns judging by resemblance to a stereotype, and hindsight bias concerns distorted memory of past predictions, neither of which is described.
  57. The correct answer is C (Equal vertical distances show equal percentage changes, so the early 300% gain is not understated.): On a logarithmic axis, a move from 10 to 40 and a move from 70 to 280 would span the same height because each is a 300% gain. The arithmetic scale draws 10 to 40 and 70 to 100 at equal height, since each is 30 points, which understates the 300% gain against the 43% gain. The log axis changes only the price spacing, not time or the data. Equal dollar changes belong to the arithmetic scale.
  58. The correct answer is B (The second crossover is a buy signal because the shorter average fell below the longer.): Under the two average rule an upward cross of the shorter average is a buy signal and a downward cross is a sell signal, so the second crossover is bearish. The other conclusions are sound: the first crossover is a buy, the slower average turns after the faster one, and both crossovers can fail in sideways trading when price oscillates around the averages.
  59. The correct answer is D (Violated support has turned into resistance, so the stall near 1.2010 is the expected role reversal.): Three closes below 1.2000 on heavy volume violated the support. By the polarity principle, broken support becomes resistance, and traders who bought above it are eager to exit near breakeven, which produces the stall. Role reversal works in both directions. A level that has been tested often keeps its importance rather than disappearing.
  60. The correct answer is D (The hypothesis came after seeing the results, so the test proves little; this is data mining.): She tried 40 combinations, chose the best, and only then wrote the hypothesis, so the result is data mining and proves little. With 40 trials a strong result appears by chance, and a single five percent significance threshold ignores that the best of many was selected. More markets or a longer history would help any result, but they do not fix the core flaw that the hypothesis followed the data.
  61. The correct answer is C (Changes cannot be forecast from past changes, yet cumulative paths can look like trends.): A random walk means each change is independent of the past, so the next change cannot be forecast from earlier ones. Because random increments accumulate, the arcsine law says the path often spends long periods on one side of its starting level, which can look like a trend. Mean reversion and momentum both imply predictability, which a random walk rules out.
  62. The correct answer is D (Distribution now, then a markdown phase, and later a new accumulation base): A sideways range at the end of a long markup, on heavy volume with failing rallies, is distribution. Wyckoff's cycle then moves to markdown, and the decline ends in a new accumulation base. Calling the range accumulation confuses a top with a bottom. Starting at markdown skips the current phase, and placing accumulation before markdown reverses the cycle order.
  63. The correct answer is B (The split halved the price per share without changing company value, so prices need adjusting first): A 2-for-1 split cuts the share price in half and doubles the share count, leaving the company's value unchanged. Without adjusting the earlier prices, the chart shows a false 50 percent plunge. Market capitalization does not change, gap labels need clean data, and a dividend adjustment is a different correction.
  64. The correct answer is A (Volume is failing to confirm the advance, a caution but not a reversal signal by itself.): Dow expected volume to expand in the direction of the primary trend, so falling volume on advances and rising volume on pullbacks is a warning. The averages confirm each other, so the trend remains in force until a definite reversal signal appears in the averages. Volume is secondary evidence in the theory, neither a trigger on its own nor irrelevant.
  65. The correct answer is A (Each player should lead about half the time, so a lopsided lead signals a biased coin.): The arcsine law says the share of time one side leads is most likely near the extremes, not near one half, so a lopsided lead is ordinary in a fair game. The claim of equal leading time and a biased coin is therefore false. The other statements correctly note that long runs occur by chance and that trends can appear in random data.
  66. The correct answer is C (Public participation, usually the longest phase, so join with a stop under the reaction low): Improving news, steady gains, and trend followers joining are the public participation phase, which is usually the longest and where the largest gain occurs, so joining with a stop below the latest reaction low is sensible. Accumulation features poor news, distribution features heavy speculation, and calling the phase the shortest reverses the usual teaching.
  67. The correct answer is C (Futures for open interest, and an exchange-traded fund for a single sector chart): Futures report open interest, the count of contracts outstanding, which helps show whether new money is entering a move. An exchange-traded fund trades like a stock and holds a whole sector, so one chart represents the group. A mutual fund prices only once a day, swapping the two roles fails, and spot currency pairs have no single open interest figure while a broad index is not one sector.
  68. The 60 percent figure is the portion retained (3,600 / 6,000), 6.7 percent is 2,400 / 36,000, and 8 percent is 2,400 / 30,000, which measure against the wrong base.
  69. The other three are true: terms are formed by adding the previous two, 34/21 is close to 1.619, and 0.618 squared is 0.382, which also equals 1 minus 0.618.
  70. The correct answer is B (The curve is inverted, and the wider spread shows investors demanding more credit-risk compensation.): When the 2-year yield of 4.9 percent exceeds the 10-year yield of 4.0 percent, short rates sit above long rates, which is an inverted curve. A credit spread is the extra yield over Treasuries that investors require for default risk, so widening from 1.2 to 2.4 points signals rising concern, and corporate prices fall relative to Treasuries. A normal curve slopes upward, and a flat curve shows near-equal yields rather than two yields that happen to be above 4 percent.
  71.
