It is a warning about the strength of the advance, not a timing signal for the exact top. Bullish divergence is the mirror image at lows, and a lower RSI peak does not mean buyers have reserves left.
  72. The correct answer is C (The line rises to 41,080 by adding the net advance of 580 issues.): The advance-decline line is a running total of daily net advances. Net advances are 1,820 minus 1,240, or 580, and 40,500 plus 580 equals 41,080. Subtracting gives 39,920, adding only advancers gives 42,320, and adding every issue traded, 3,060, gives 43,560. Each wrong value ignores the net difference.
  73. The correct answer is B (Either markets are inefficient or the CAPM mismeasures risk; the test cannot say which.): Any test of efficiency needs a model of expected returns, so an abnormal return can mean inefficiency or a flawed model. That is the joint hypothesis problem. The result does not prove inefficiency or validate the CAPM, and risk adjustment is a required part of such tests, not an invalid one.
  74. The correct answer is D (A secondary movement, or wave, which is a correction inside the larger primary trend.): Dow classified a primary movement as the tide lasting a year or more, a secondary movement as a wave or correction lasting from about three weeks to several months, and minor movements as ripples. A three week decline retracing about half of the advance is a secondary movement inside the primary uptrend. A new bear tide would require a break of the prior lows, not a pause in the advance.
  75. The correct answer is B (Disposition effect: selling winners too soon and holding losers too long, driven by loss aversion.): The pattern of selling the gainer and clinging to the loser until it breaks even is the disposition effect. It stems from loss aversion and regret avoidance, because a loss only feels real once sold. The house money effect would predict holding or adding to the winner after gains. Loss aversion is the underlying motive, but it does not by itself describe the asymmetric selling that defines this behavior.
  76. The correct answer is C (A heavy-volume neckline breakout matters more for the inverse pattern than for the top): In standard teaching, the neckline break of a head and shoulders top can occur on modest volume, since prices can fall under their own weight. The breakout of an inverse pattern needs a clear expansion in volume to show real demand. Contracting volume on either break does not confirm anything, and volume is part of how both patterns are judged.
  77. The correct answer is A (There is a strong inverse relationship, so the two series tend to move in opposite directions.): Pearson's r ranges from negative 1 to positive 1. Its size shows strength and its sign shows direction, so negative 0.90 means a strong inverse linear relationship. Scatter around the line would show up as a value near zero. The sign tells direction, not cause. R squared is 0.81 and can never be negative, because it is a squared value that measures the share of variation explained.
  78. The correct answer is A (Negative 25 dollars, so she declines even though the expected monetary value is positive 25.): The subjective value is 0.5 times 150 minus 0.5 times 2 times 100, which is 75 minus 100, or negative 25. The plain expected value is 75 minus 50, or positive 25, so a loss averse manager rejects a bet an expected value maximizer accepts. Dropping the probabilities gives negative 50 and counting only the gain gives 75, neither a complete weighting.
  79. The correct answer is B (It captures the gap between sessions, so volatility while the market was closed is counted.): Wilder's true range is the greatest of high minus low, the absolute high minus prior close, and the absolute low minus prior close. Here the three values are 1, 5 and 4, so true range is 5 rather than the misleading 1. Smoothing is the job of the average true range, and percentage scaling is a separate normalization. Volume plays no part in the calculation.
  80. The correct answer is D (Misrepresentation under Standard I(C), because presenting another's work as her own is plagiarism): Standard I(C) specifically covers plagiarism, which means using others' work as one's own without credit. Misconduct is a broader Standard and tempts because the act is dishonest, but the specific Standard is the better fit. Independence concerns bias from outside influence, and a copyright issue is a legal side effect rather than the core of the Standard.
  81. The correct answer is A (The ADX filter blocks the entry, because trend strength is below the required reading of 25.): The trigger fires because the close is above the 50-day average, and the $42 price clears the $10 minimum. The ADX reading of 19 fails the filter, which requires 25 or more, so the signal is suppressed. A filter screens signals out; it does not change the position size.
  82. The correct answer is B (About 10.1 percent, found from the geometric mean of the three yearly returns): Growth over several years compounds, so the right measure is the geometric mean: 1.40 times 0.90 times 1.06 equals 1.3356, and the cube root is about 1.101, or 10.1 percent. The arithmetic average of 12.0 percent overstates it, the 33.6 percent figure is cumulative rather than annual, and the median of 6.0 percent ignores the other years. Claiming 12 percent annual growth would mislead readers.
  83. The correct answer is C (Sellers drove price sharply lower, but buyers absorbed the selling and recovered most of it.): The long lower shadow shows sellers pushed price far down, while the small body near the high shows buyers took control and recovered most of the loss by the close. After a decline this signals that selling pressure is being absorbed. A rally rejected near the high describes a shooting star, a bounce on thin trade does not give a body near the high, and opening at the low would leave no lower shadow.
  84. The correct answer is B (A secondary reaction, a countertrend move inside a primary uptrend that has not ended): Dow Theory splits movements into primary, secondary and minor. A decline lasting from a few weeks to a few months that retraces roughly one third to two thirds of the advance is a normal secondary reaction. The primary uptrend continues until lows and highs stop rising. A minor movement lasts only days to a few weeks, and retracement size or length alone does not end a primary trend.
  85. The correct answer is A (Endowment bias, in which he values the shares more highly simply because he owns them): Refusing to sell at a price he would not pay to buy is the classic endowment effect: ownership itself raises the value he places on the asset. Status quo bias is the close look-alike but it involves inertia rather than a higher valuation. Nothing in the stem mentions a loss or blame, and emotional biases like these are harder to correct with information because they come from feelings, not errors of reasoning.
  86. The correct answer is A (A double top breakout, a buy signal because the new column exceeds both peaks at 50): With two X columns peaking at 50, a later X column that reaches 51 has exceeded both tops, which is the double top breakout buy signal. A triple top needs three equal peaks. The O column to 44 was only the reaction that set up the move. One box beyond the prior top is all a breakout needs.
  87. The correct answer is C (After a decisive break, former support tends to turn into resistance, so the reversal is rejection.): By the polarity principle, support that gives way on a decisive, heavy-volume close tends to become resistance when price returns. The reversal at 90.10 fits that script. The level keeps meaning because it flips role. Light volume on a rally to the level is common and does not cancel a heavy-volume break, and horizontal levels reverse roles as well as trendlines do.
  88. The correct answer is C (Bollinger uses standard deviation around a simple average; Keltner uses ATR around an exponential one.): Bollinger Bands set the bands from the standard deviation of price around a simple moving average, typically 20 periods and 2 deviations. Keltner Channels use an exponential average of typical price with an ATR multiple. The swapped description reverses them, and the other options describe constructions neither one uses.
  89. The correct answer is D (Run the unchanged rule on a later period it was never fitted to.): A rule tuned over many trials can look good by chance, so the result on the fitting data proves little. Applying the unchanged rule to data it has not seen is the honest test of whether an edge exists. Re-optimizing on more history or adding filters until losses disappear both make overfitting worse, and trading at full size skips validation altogether.
  90. The correct answer is A (The first client shows loss aversion, and the second client shows the disposition effect.): Expected value is 0.5 times 150 minus 0.5 times 100, or plus 25, yet the first client declines because a 100 loss weighs more than a 150 gain, which is loss aversion. The second client sells a winner and holds a loser, the textbook disposition effect. House money effect would mean taking more risk after gains.
  91. The correct answer is A (Crowded speculator longs against heavy commercial hedging suggest a top is near; fade the trend.): Technicians watch the report for extremes in positioning. Large speculators tend to be trend followers and are crowded on one side near turning points, while commercial hedgers are managing business risk and are often on the opposite side. A record net long by speculators therefore raises the odds that buyers are exhausted. Hedger shorts offset physical holdings and are not directional bearish views, and the large speculator group is a key part of the report.
  92. The correct answer is B (A trade at 58, since three 2-point boxes below the 64 top is the reversal.): The reversal size equals the box size times the number of boxes: 2 points times 3 equals 6 points. A new O column begins when price trades 6 points below the top of the X column, so 64 minus 6 equals 58. A one-box decline of 2 points to 62 is below the threshold and is ignored. The 61 choice treats the 3 as points rather than boxes, and a full retracement to 52 is not required.
  93. The correct answer is C (Showing a composite that omits accounts closed after underperforming, with a footnote noting it): Leaving out accounts that closed after poor results flatters the record, so the composite is no longer fair or complete, and a footnote does not cure that. A clearly labeled example, a past-results statement and net-of-fee returns clearly stated are all consistent with the Standard. They tempt because they involve selecting or qualifying results, but each is stated openly.
  94. The correct answer is C (Prospect theory says people judge gains and losses from a reference point, not final wealth.): Judging final wealth is the expected utility view that prospect theory was built to challenge. Prospect theory says outcomes are coded as gains or losses against a reference point, often the purchase price, so the same dollar outcome can feel very different. Gains are weighted less than equal losses, not more, and the value function is curved, not linear.
  95. The correct answer is A (Abnormal returns depend on a pricing model, so the result may reflect a flawed model.): Every test of market efficiency also tests the model used to define normal returns, the joint hypothesis problem, so an abnormal return can mean inefficiency or a bad model. Price-to-book is public information and fits a semi-strong test. Efficiency predicts no persistent abnormal returns. Insider data belongs to strong-form tests.
  96. The correct answer is C (Breakaway gap, marking the start of a new trend out of a long consolidation area.): A gap that leaves a long consolidation on heavy volume and stays open is a breakaway gap, the signal that a new trend is starting. A runaway gap occurs after the trend is well underway, an exhaustion gap near its end, and a common gap inside a range with ordinary volume that tends to fill quickly.
  97. The correct answer is C (Charts are fractal because crowd behavior driven by fear and greed repeats at every time scale): Technicians describe price charts as fractal: the same shapes appear at different scales because the buying and selling psychology behind them is similar for short term and long term participants. Sharing source trades does not make shapes match, since weekly bars compress very different information. Algorithmic trading and volume levels do not explain the self similarity across scales.
  98. The correct answer is D (Hindsight bias, the tendency to see past outcomes as having been predictable.): Hindsight bias makes an outcome look as if it had been predictable once it is known. His purchase of more shares before the drop shows he did not act as though he knew. The other biases describe evidence selection, judgment by resemblance and overrated influence, none of which is a claim about having predicted the past.
  99. The correct answer is B (Bollinger width tracks closing-price deviation, reacting sharply to one extreme close, while Keltner uses smoothed ATR.): Bollinger Bands use a simple moving average with width set by the standard deviation of closing prices, which jumps after one extreme close. Keltner Channels use an exponential average with width set by a multiple of ATR, itself a smoothed average built on True Range. The reverse assignments describe the wrong tool in each case. The closing-price-only claim is also wrong, because True Range includes gaps and the full bar range.
  100. The correct answer is A (Self-control bias, favoring present spending over long-term goals he knows matter): He understands the goal, yet repeatedly chooses immediate spending, which is self-control bias. The catch-up risk taking is a consequence, not the root cause. Status quo bias would involve inertia without the admitted knowledge and temptation. Overconfidence explains the aggressive request but not the spending lapses. Because this is an emotional bias, an advisor would adapt, for example with automatic contributions.
  101. The correct answer is C (Same shape; after a decline it is a hammer, after a rally a hanging man.): A hammer and a hanging man share one shape, so the preceding trend defines them. After a decline the long lower shadow shows buyers rejecting lower prices, which is bullish. After a rally the same shape warns that sellers are testing the advance, which is bearish. Swapping the names reverses both meanings, gap rules are not part of the definition, and ignoring the prior trend erases the only difference between the two.
  102. The correct answer is B (Price falls to about $853, and current yield rises to about 4.7% on the lower price.): The coupon of $40 for 10 years discounted at 6% is worth about $294, and the $1,000 face value discounted at 6% is worth about $558, so the price is near $853. Current yield is $40 divided by $853, about 4.7%. Prices move opposite to yields, and the relationship is convex rather than a straight multiple, so a 2 point rise costs well over 8%.
  103. The correct answer is A (Weak and semi-strong forms hold, while the strong form fails for private information): No gain from past price patterns fits the weak form, and no gain from published earnings news fits the semi-strong form. Profit from unpublished merger news means private information is not fully in prices, so the strong form fails. Saying public news is not in prices contradicts the earnings finding. Using past prices and finding no profit supports the weak form, and a profit available only from private information does not make every form fail.
  104. The correct answer is D (A sudden overnight shift in sentiment leaves buyers paying far above the prior close): A gap forms when the balance of supply and demand shifts abruptly between sessions, so the first trade occurs far from the prior close because no one will transact in between. Thin liquidity and auction mechanics may affect an open slightly but do not create a gap of 4.50. Selling by earlier holders would push the open down, not up to 52.50.
  105. The correct answer is C (Index X rises about 9 percent, Index Y under 1 percent, since X weights by price): In a price-weighted index, the 200 stock dominates: the sum goes from 220 to 240, about 9.1 percent. In a cap-weighted index, stock P is only 10 of 110 billion, so its 10 percent gain adds 1 billion, about 0.9 percent. Equal weighting would give about 5 percent. The index never moves by the full 10 percent unless the stock is the entire index.
  106. The correct answer is C (A single non-confirmation is a warning; reversal needs later evidence, such as breaking prior lows.): Under Dow Theory a failure of the averages to confirm each other is a caution flag, not a completed reversal. The trend is presumed to continue until a definite reversal signal, such as both averages breaking below earlier lows. Closing prices are the most important prices, and both averages matter, so the analyst acted too soon.
  107. The correct answer is B (Place the stop from the chart, find risk per share, divide the 500 budget by it.): One percent of 50,000 is a 500 dollar risk budget. The chart sets the stop, so the risk per share is 40 minus 38, or 2 dollars, and 500 divided by 2 gives 250 shares. Sizing by account value or by price ignores the stop distance, and choosing the stop after buying lets the position decide the risk.
  108. The correct answer is A (A rising wedge: both lines slope up and converge, which usually resolves downward despite rising price.): An ascending triangle has a flat top, so a rising upper line rules it out. A flag slopes against the prior trend, and a channel has parallel lines. Two rising lines that converge describe a rising wedge, which tends to break down because each push higher gains less ground.
  109. The correct answer is B (Accumulation is ending and public participation is beginning, confirmed by both averages exceeding prior highs.): Months of basing on low volume match accumulation. A rally in which both averages clear prior reaction highs on rising volume, with improving business news, is how Dow described the start of public participation. Waiting for fully optimistic news is tempting, but by then the second phase is well along. The other answers confuse the order of the phases.
  110. The correct answer is C (Illusion of control, believing personal involvement and effort can affect outcomes one does not influence): The trader thinks his activity changes outcomes it cannot change, which is the illusion of control. People fall into it when they feel involved, make choices, and put in effort. Overconfidence concerns overrating knowledge, and representativeness concerns resemblance. Hindsight bias concerns past results, so each is a near miss rather than the stated belief.
  111. The correct answer is D (25 percent, the decline from the 80,000 peak to the later 60,000 low): Maximum drawdown is the largest peak-to-trough decline as a percentage of the peak. Here the equity falls 20,000 from 80,000, which is 25 percent. Dividing by the trough gives 33 percent, which is the wrong base. The 60 percent figure is a gain, and the account never fell to 50,000 after the peak.
  112. The correct answer is B (Momentum measures speed of price change, which can slow while price rises.): RSI compares average gains with average losses, so it measures how fast price is moving. Price can still make new highs while that speed is fading, producing a bearish divergence and an early warning. RSI is not built from volume, its overbought level is 70, and a divergence is a warning rather than proof that a reversal already happened.
  113. The correct answer is A (Disclose the holding to her employer and in the report so readers can judge her objectivity): Standard VI(A) requires full and fair disclosure of ownership that could reasonably impair independence, to both clients and the employer. Selling is not required, and telling compliance alone leaves the readers without the information they need. Waiting to trade belongs to the priority of transactions rule, which addresses timing rather than disclosure.
  114. The correct answer is C (The rule leaves 0.3 percent outside, so losses are rare yet possible, particularly with fat tails.): The Empirical Rule says about 99.7 percent of values lie within three standard deviations, which leaves about 0.3 percent outside. A move beyond the range is therefore unlikely, not impossible, and real returns often have fatter tails than a normal curve. The rule works for any approximately normal data with any mean, including returns. Two deviations, not three, cover 95 percent, so the claim that three deviations cover 95 percent mixes up the bands.
  115. The correct answer is B (Chart analysis of past prices could still beat the market, since only fundamentals are reflected): The description is the semi-strong form, and it covers past prices as public information, so chart analysis should not beat the market, which makes the claim that only fundamentals are reflected the false conclusion. The other three conclusions are accurate: past price rules fail, the strong form would also remove insider gains, and semi-strong efficiency includes the weak form.
  116. The correct answer is C (State a testable hypothesis with rules defined in advance, then test it on historical data): The scientific method moves from observation to a precisely stated hypothesis and then a test with rules fixed in advance. Collecting only supporting examples is confirmation bias, live trading is a costly first test, and publishing before testing skips the step that separates a real edge from a coincidence.
  117. The correct answer is C (Volatility generally rises in steady advances and falls in sharp declines, so put skew favors calls.): Volatility typically rises in falling markets and shrinks in calm advances, so the claim that it rises in steady rallies and falls in sharp declines is reversed, and it also gives a wrong reason for put skew. Put skew exists because investors pay up for downside protection. Volatility does move in cycles of calm and turbulence. Historical volatility is backward-looking and implied volatility is the market's forward-looking estimate.
  118. The correct answer is A (M's ratio line to the S&P 500 is falling, so its relative strength is weak): Relative strength compares the stock with the benchmark. With M up 12 percent and the index up 20 percent, M divided by the index declined, so M lagged. An absolute uptrend and weak relative strength can coexist. Ratio lines can be read over any period, and there is no half-of-the-index rule.
  119. The correct answer is C (The daily range runs from open to close, making it identical to the candlestick body): The range runs from the session low to the session high, while the open to close distance is the real body in candlestick terms, so equating them is the false statement. The other three statements are standard: the low is the minimum traded price and may equal the open, a close above the open gives a white or hollow body, and sessions with equal closes can have very different ranges.
  120. The correct answer is D (The energy ETF, because its ratio rose while the tech ETF lagged its benchmark despite gaining.): Relative strength compares performance to a benchmark. The energy ETF is down 6 percent against a benchmark down 14 percent, a ratio of 0.94 divided by 0.86, or about plus 9 percent. The tech ETF at 1.04 divided by 1.12 is about minus 7 percent, so it lagged despite rising. Absolute return is a separate measure, and relative strength is meaningful even in falling markets. A falling ratio is weakness, not an automatic catch-up setup.
  121. The correct answer is D (A bullish engulfing pattern, because the white body wraps the prior black body.): The second candle opens below the prior close of 50.00 and closes at 52.50, above the prior open of 52.00, so its body fully engulfs the black body. That is a bullish engulfing pattern after a decline. A piercing line must close below the prior open, a harami has the second body inside the first, and a morning star needs three candles.
  122. The correct answer is A (Extreme volume on a parabolic final leg often marks a blow-off, so it can signal exhaustion): Volume normally rises in the direction of the trend, but a very steep, accelerating final leg on the heaviest volume of the whole run is the typical blow-off top, a sign of exhaustion. That does not prove an immediate decline, only that the risk is higher. Volume on advances still carries information, and contracting volume would signal weakness, not strength.
  123. The correct answer is C (Herding, where investors imitate the crowd, producing trends and exaggerated swings that appear on charts.): Herding is the tendency to follow what others do, and it helps explain why trends overshoot and why bubbles and crashes form. Technicians argue that repeated crowd behavior leaves recognizable footprints on charts. Overconfidence, extrapolation and regret aversion are genuine biases and can touch charts, but none matches buying simply because others are buying.
  124. The correct answer is B (Volume is diverging from price, warning that the advance lacks participation, not yet a climax): Rising prices on shrinking volume is a divergence: fewer participants support each new high, so confirmation is missing. A climax is a burst of unusually heavy volume at an extreme, not a gradual fade. A selling climax occurs at the end of declines, and volume is a standard confirmation tool for trends.
  125. The correct answer is D (Tighten stops, but wait for a close below the neckline before confirming the top.): A head and shoulders top is confirmed only when price closes below the neckline, so the manager should tighten risk without acting on an unfinished pattern. Lighter volume on the right shoulder supports the bearish read rather than refuting it. The measured target is subtracted from the neckline, not added to it, and it is applied only after the break.
  126. The correct answer is C (Profit-taking without new selling pressure, so a flag is likely and the advance should resume): A sharp advance followed by a brief, shallow consolidation sloping against the trend on declining volume is a flag, a continuation pattern in which traders take profit without heavy selling. Real distribution normally comes with heavier volume and a wider range. A descending triangle needs a flat lower boundary, and a head and shoulders top needs a head and shoulders, neither of which is described.
  127. The correct answer is A (Spot currency has no central exchange volume, so tick volume or futures volume must stand in.): Spot currencies trade over the counter, so no single exchange reports total volume. Tick volume, which counts price changes, and volume from currency futures are the usual proxies. Open interest exists only for futures and options, so it cannot stand in for volume on a spot chart. Dropping volume altogether is tempting but gives up information that useful proxies still provide.
  128. The correct answer is A (The range is $3.30 and the change from the open to the close is +$1.50.): The range is the high minus the low: 44.80 minus 41.50 = $3.30. The change from open to close is 43.60 minus 42.10 = +$1.50. Close minus low gives $2.10, which measures the recovery from the low, not the open-to-close move. High minus open gives $2.70, which measures only the upward excursion. A negative sign would imply the close was under the open, which it was not.
  129. The correct answer is B (An exponential moving average, which weights recent closes heavily and fades old closes gradually): Only the exponential average satisfies both conditions: heavier weight on recent closes and no sudden drop of old data, since old closes fade gradually. The linearly weighted average also favors recent closes but removes the oldest close completely at the window edge. A longer simple average reacts more slowly, and displacing an average in time does not change its weights.
  130. The correct answer is B (Multiply the 20-point range by each ratio and subtract the result from the swing high): A retracement measures a partial move back against the prior advance, so the ratios are applied to the size of the move, here 60 minus 40, or 20 points. Each product is subtracted from the swing high, giving levels such as 60 minus 4.72 for 23.6 percent. Applying ratios to the price itself ignores the move size, and adding to the high describes an extension target above the old high. A single quotient of high over low is not a retracement level at all.
  131. The correct answer is D (Fewer participants are willing to buy at higher prices, so the uptrend is losing conviction): Volume should expand in the direction of the trend, so successively smaller volume on each rally leg shows fewer participants at higher prices and weaker conviction. Thin volume on pullbacks is the normal pattern, which tempts the confirmation reading but does not fit rally legs. Scarce supply is not shown by thin volume, and a divergence warns without proving that a reversal has already happened.
  132. The correct answer is D (A rising ratio line proves the ETF's own price is rising in absolute terms as well): A ratio line only compares two series, so it can rise when the ETF falls less than the benchmark. That makes the claim of absolute price gain the false one. The remaining three statements are standard teaching: outperformance can occur in a falling market, top-down ranking runs from asset class to sector to security, and relative strength often leads. Each of the three is tempting to doubt but is accurate here.
  133. The correct answer is A (A martingale, which fixes the expected next price without requiring independent, identically distributed changes.): A martingale says only that the best forecast of the next price is the current price. A random walk is stricter because it requires independent, identically distributed changes. Mean reversion predicts movement toward an average, and the arcsine law concerns the long stretches a random series spends above or below its start.
  134. The correct answer is A (Bearish divergence warns that momentum is fading, so tighten stops and await price confirmation.): When price makes a higher high but RSI makes a lower high, momentum is not confirming the advance. This is a bearish divergence, which is a warning, not a sell signal by itself, so waiting for price confirmation and tightening stops fits. Adding to longs ignores the warning. The bullish divergence label is reversed, and a lower peak at a high price is not an oversold reading.
  135. The correct answer is C (Move one column to the right and plot O's from 58 down through 54): A three-box reversal with a box size of 2 requires a 6-point fall from the last X box: 60 down to 54. That qualifies, so plotting moves to the next column, and the O column begins one box below the prior top, at 58, then runs down to 54. Staying in the column, starting at 60, or waiting for 52 all break the standard rules.
  136. The correct answer is C (Price is above the upper Bollinger Band yet inside the Keltner Channel, so no breakout shows.): A close at 85 is above the upper Bollinger Band at 84 but below the upper Keltner Channel at 86, so the two envelopes disagree. Bollinger Bands use standard deviation and Keltner Channels use an EMA with ATR, so the channel is wider here because ATR captures ranges and gaps. The disagreement is information about the size of the move, not a reason to throw out the Bollinger reading, and the close is not outside both.
  137. The correct answer is D (Minor movements are the most reliable guide for judging the direction of the primary trend.): Dow regarded minor movements, lasting from a few days to under three weeks, as the least reliable and often misleading, so calling them the most reliable guide to the primary trend is the false statement. The other three are standard teaching: minor movements are weak guides, secondary reactions in a bear market are rallies, and they last weeks to months.
  138. The correct answer is A (40,800, because the line adds advancing minus declining issues.): The advance-decline line is a running total of each session's net breadth, which is advancing issues minus declining issues. Here 1,900 minus 1,100 equals 800, and 40,000 plus 800 gives 40,800. Adding only the advancers gives 41,900, adding all issues gives 43,000, and reversing the subtraction gives 39,200.
  139. The correct answer is A (Plot nothing, because price has not yet fallen to the 45 box.): A three-box reversal needs price to reach the box three below the top of the column, which here is 45, before a column of Os begins at 47. A box fills only when price trades to or through its value, and 45.40 has not reached 45. Rounding to 45 or accepting two boxes both ignore the reversal rule, and Os are not mixed into a column of Xs.
  140. The correct answer is C (Test for a new X, test for reversal, then start an O column one box lower): Plotting follows a fixed order: first check whether the price fills a new box in the current direction, and if not, check whether it has moved the full reversal amount the other way. With a three-box reversal, the move qualifies, so the plotter moves one column right and starts the O column one box below the top X. Xs never run downward, the new column does not share the old top row, and there is no time interval to wait for.
  141. The correct answer is C (Buy on the close above 36, projecting a target near 42 from the 6 point height): A double bottom is confirmed when price closes above the peak between the lows, here 36, and the target is that peak plus the pattern height of 6 points, or about 42. Buying at the second low acts before confirmation, and a close below 30 would be a failure, not a bullish setup. Doubling the height to reach 48 overstates the standard measuring rule.
  142. The correct answer is B (Double top confirmed by the close below 62, with a minimum objective near 54): The two peaks at 70 with a valley at 62 form a double top, which is confirmed only when price closes below the valley. The height is 70 minus 62, or 8, so the minimum objective is 62 minus 8, or 54. Confirming at the second peak, doubling the height, or calling it a continuation misapplies the rules.
  143. The correct answer is A (Correlation alone does not prove causation, and r squared of 0.49 means 49 percent shared variation.): Two series can move together because of a common driver or by coincidence, so a correlation coefficient never proves one causes the other. The share of variation in one series accounted for by the other is r squared, which is 0.70 times 0.70, or 0.49. Using r directly as the explained share overstates it. The slope plays no role in r squared, and no 0.90 cutoff exists that makes a 0.70 correlation unusable.

Practice Test 3 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (A double top; the confirmed breakdown projects about eight points of decline below 72, near 64.): Two peaks at the same level with an intervening low at 72 form a double top, not a head and shoulders, which needs a higher middle peak. The pattern height is 80 minus 72, or eight points, and the target is measured down from the 72 breakdown level, giving about 64. Lighter volume on the second peak is typical and does not invalidate the pattern. Measuring from the peak understates the target.
  2. The correct answer is C (Split adjustment changes percentage changes between bars, so adjusted history misstates returns): A proper split adjustment rescales earlier prices by the split factor, which leaves every percentage change between bars intact, so the claim that it misstates returns is false. The other three are true: survivor only samples omit acquired or failed firms, the bar interval changes signal counts, and dividend adjusted prices differ from raw quotes yet preserve the return holders received.
  3. The correct answer is A (300 shares, because the 750 dollar risk divided by the 2.50 dollar stop distance equals 300.): Dollar risk is 1.5 percent of 50,000, which is 750 dollars. The risk per share is the entry minus the stop, 40.00 minus 37.50, or 2.50. Shares equal 750 divided by 2.50, which is 300. The account divided by price ignores the stop entirely, and dividing risk by the entry price ignores the stop distance. Using 1 percent risk gives 200 shares, which is the wrong risk rate.
  4. The correct answer is D (Buying power looks exhausted, so the market is vulnerable to a pullback or a top.): When funds hold little cash and the survey crowd is bullish, most participants are already invested, which leaves few new buyers to push prices higher. That is the contrarian warning. Treating low cash as confirmation reads it like momentum, and the two readings point the same way rather than offsetting.
  5. The correct answer is D (Leaving a 3 for 1 split unadjusted keeps percentage changes correct, so indicators are unaffected.): Without a split adjustment a 3 for 1 split shows as a drop of about two thirds in one bar, which distorts percentage changes and indicators, so the statement that leaving it unadjusted keeps percentage changes correct is false. The other three are true: split adjustment divides earlier prices, dropping delisted stocks overstates results, and unadjusted futures rolls create false jumps.
  6. The correct answer is A (Chart 1 is logarithmic, since steady percentage growth plots as a straight line there.): Steady percentage growth plots as a straight line on a logarithmic scale and as an upward-curving line on an arithmetic one, so the straight-line panel, Chart 1, is the log scale. The scales do not draw the series alike. Equal dollar moves taking equal vertical distances describes an arithmetic axis, and naming Chart 2 gives the right reason with the wrong panel.
  7. The correct answer is B (The uptrend is presumed to continue because both averages confirm, with volume only a caution): Both averages confirmed with new closing highs, and Dow treated volume as a secondary check, so weak volume is a warning but not a definite reversal signal. Volume does not override the averages, volume does not have to set a new high for confirmation, and a secondary reaction is a price movement, not a volume pattern, and does not end a primary trend.
  8. The correct answer is A (Specify a return model, measure abnormal returns, test against zero, and caveat the model): Every efficiency test is joint: it needs a model of expected returns to define what abnormal means, so a rejection could mean the market is inefficient or the model is wrong. The sequence therefore starts with the model, then abnormal returns, then the test, and ends with that caveat. Fama later grouped such tests into return predictability, event studies, and tests for private information.
  9. The correct answer is A (Monday's close was only 2.1% above resistance, so the 3% filter was first met Tuesday.): The 3% rule asks for a close at least 3% beyond the broken level, which here means 41.20 or higher. Monday's close of 40.84 is 2.1% above 40.00, so it falls short. Tuesday's 41.30 is 3.25% above, so confirmation arrives then. The other answers either drop the threshold or invent extra requirements that the rule does not contain.
  10. The correct answer is D (Momentum, which contradicts the weak form because past price data alone helped predict returns): The manager ranks stocks only on past twelve-month returns, which is momentum, and profiting from past prices contradicts the weak form, and because the semi-strong and strong forms include the weak form, they are contradicted too. The value effect sorts on price relative to earnings or book value, a public fundamental ratio. The size effect sorts on company capitalization, and the January effect depends on the calendar month, not on a rolling past-return ranking.
  11. The correct answer is B (Primary is the tide, secondary is the waves, and minor is the ripples): Dow likened the primary trend to the tide, secondary reactions to waves, and minor day to day fluctuations to ripples. A rising tide can carry waves that retreat without reversing the tide, which is why secondary reactions do not end a primary trend. Each other pairing keeps the three sea terms but places at least two at the wrong level of duration, which tempts a student who recalls the images but not the order.
  12. The correct answer is C (Its signals arrive near exact tops and bottoms, which is why it is praised for timing): Because a reversal needs confirmation from both averages and a definite break, Dow Theory signals come well after tops and bottoms, so praising them for near exact timing is the false statement. The other three are standard points: critics question the averages' relevance, confirmation delays signals while filtering noise, and judging a line formation is subjective.
  13. The correct answer is D (About 68 percent, because the range spans one standard deviation on each side of the mean.): The mean minus one standard deviation is 0.1 minus 1.5, or negative 1.4, and the mean plus one standard deviation is 0.1 plus 1.5, or 1.6. The Empirical Rule puts about 68 percent of a normal distribution within one standard deviation of the mean. The 95 percent figure belongs to two standard deviations, and 99.7 percent belongs to three. The 34 percent figure is the share on one side of the mean only, but this range covers both sides.
  14. The correct answer is D (March 12, 2027, ten years after the record date, because the stricter regulatory period governs): Standard I(A) requires following the stricter of law and the Standards' recommendations, and Standard V(C) requires retaining records for the period required. The regulator's 10 years beats the firm's 7, so March 12, 2017 plus 10 years gives March 12, 2027. Seven years gives 2024, five years gives 2022, and counting from year end would push the date to December 31, 2027.
  15. The correct answer is C (Hold, move the stop just below the doji's low, and let the next close show direction.): A doji shows buyers and sellers in balance. After a long advance and at resistance it is a warning, not a confirmed top. Selling or shorting before confirmation acts too soon, and adding treats indecision as strength. Keeping the position with a closer stop below the doji's low limits risk while the next session reveals who wins.
  16. The correct answer is B (1.20, measured from Monday's high straight up to Tuesday's session low): A gap is the empty space between the previous bar's high and the new bar's low when price jumps up. Here that is 49.80 minus 48.60, which equals 1.20. The values 1.50 (50.10 minus 48.60), 1.70 (50.10 minus 48.40) and 1.40 (49.80 minus 48.40) each mix an open or a close into the measurement and overstate the space where no trading occurred.
  17. The correct answer is A (The dealer structure does not make patterns unreliable; supply, demand and trader behavior still shape price.): The first claim is right: EUR is the base currency, and a higher quote means more dollars per euro, so the euro strengthened. The flaw is the second claim, since patterns come from supply, demand and psychology, not from a central exchange, though volume data is limited. The base and quote reversals are wrong, and EUR/USD is a major pair, not a cross pair.
  18. The correct answer is B (An exhaustion gap, a late signal that the advance may be ending and buyers are spent): The gap comes at the end of a long advance, on heavy volume followed by failure to continue, which is the textbook exhaustion gap. A runaway gap sits near the middle of a move and is followed by continued progress. A breakaway gap leaves a base at the start, and a common gap forms in a quiet range, so neither fits a gap late in an eleven month climb.
  19. The correct answer is A (Herding: investors copy one another and trade together, pushing prices past fair value): Herding explains persistence: when participants imitate each other, buying or selling pressure feeds on itself and carries prices past fair value, creating the trends chartists study. A steady fundamental drift is the efficient market view, and anchoring or loss aversion can slow adjustment, but they describe lagging or sluggish prices rather than moves well beyond what fundamentals alone would justify.
  20. The correct answer is D (Survivorship bias, because companies that failed or were dropped are missing from the sample.): Choosing today's index members quietly removes every company that went bankrupt, was acquired at a loss, or was demoted, so the surviving group looks better than the real investable set. That is survivorship bias. Overfitting is a real concern given 14 tuned parameters, but the stem asks about the flaw tied to using only current members, which is the sample selection.
  21. The correct answer is D (Buyers pushed price up but sellers drove it back near the open, hinting bulls are tiring.): The candle has a small body near the low and a long upper shadow after an advance, the shape of a shooting star. It shows bulls pushed price up and bears took it back, a warning of weakening demand. The hammer is the mirror image at a decline, the doji reflects balance, and a marubozu reflects one-sided control.
  22. The correct answer is C (One long run is weak evidence, since random walks make long one-sided stretches): The arcsine law says a random walk tends to spend long periods on one side of its start, with few crossings, so a nine month run can occur by chance. One path is weak evidence; a proper test looks for correlation between successive returns across many observations. Equal time on each side is the opposite of the arcsine result, and a martingale says the expected next price equals the current one, not that gains continue.
  23. The correct answer is C (13, 21, 34, 55, with each term divided by the one before it approaching 1.618): Each term is the sum of the previous two, giving 5 + 8 = 13, then 21, 34 and 55. Dividing a term by the one before it, such as 55 divided by 34, approaches the golden ratio of 1.618. The reciprocal, a term over the following one, approaches 0.618, 1.382 is a Fibonacci extension level, and 2 appears only as an early quotient, so each of those limits is wrong for consecutive terms.
  24. The correct answer is A (3 to 1, since the 6 point reward is three times the 2 point risk): Reward is the distance from entry to target, 46 minus 40, or 6 points. Risk is the distance from entry to stop, 40 minus 38, or 2 points, so the ratio is 3 to 1. Inverting the fraction gives 1 to 3, measuring reward from the stop gives 8 over 2 or 4 to 1, and dividing 46 by 38 gives about 1.2, none of which uses the right distances.
  25. The correct answer is C (Dividing 55, 34 and 21 by 89 gives 0.618, 0.382, 0.236, so her labels shift): 55/89 is 0.618, 34/89 is 0.382, and 21/89 is 0.236, each rounded to three places. So dividing a term by the next term gives 0.618, by the term two places up gives 0.382, and by the term three places up gives 0.236. Her labels are each shifted by one step. Reversing the list is wrong, the 1.618 figure comes from dividing a term by the one before it, and the ratios do follow from the sequence.
  26. The correct answer is A (Volume counts contracts traded in the session; open interest counts contracts still open at the close.): Volume is the number of contracts that changed hands during the period, usually drawn as bars under the price chart. Open interest is the number of contracts that remain outstanding and not yet closed or delivered, often drawn as a line. Open interest rises by 3,000 only when new positions are opened. Swapping the definitions is the common mix-up, and open interest counts each contract once, not as a running total of volume.
  27. The correct answer is B (A line chart connecting daily closes also displays each day's high and low.): A line chart that connects closing prices shows only the close, so it omits the open, the high and the low. An OHLC bar shows all four prices with ticks for the open and close, and a candlestick shows them with a real body and shadows. The other three statements are accurate.
  28. The correct answer is A (Pessimism is extreme, which contrarians treat as a possible bullish turning point, not a sell signal): Contrarians view sentiment extremes as warnings: a high put/call ratio and few bulls signal widespread fear, which tends to occur near bottoms. Following the crowd would be the opposite of the method. Put/call and surveys are used as timing aids, not dismissed as lagging or meaningless.
  29. The correct answer is B (Bullish engulfing, since day two's real body covers day one's body after a decline.): Day one's real body spans 50.10 to 52.00, and day two's body spans 49.70 to 52.60, so the second body fully covers the first. A piercing line would close above the midpoint of the first body but below its open, whereas this close of 52.60 is above the open of 52.00. A harami needs the second body inside the first, and a morning star needs three candles.
  30. The correct answer is C (Survivorship bias and overfitting probably inflate it, so it needs out-of-sample tests with delisted stocks.): Testing only on stocks that are in the index today leaves out firms that failed or were removed, which is survivorship bias. Tuning six parameters on the same data invites overfitting. A longer test does not cure either problem, and it does not make survivorship bias smaller. Look-ahead bias involves using information that was not yet available, which is a different flaw. The remedy is testing on fresh data with failed companies included.
  31. The correct answer is C (It lags, because confirmation by both averages arrives after much of the move): The complaint is about timing: waiting for both averages to confirm is what makes the theory late, so lag is the criticism. Subjectivity is a real criticism too and is tempting, but it concerns classification of swings, not lateness. Dow treated volume as a secondary confirming tool rather than ignoring it, and the theory sets no fixed percentage targets for its phases.
  32. The correct answer is A (Liquidity, free price discovery, enough participants, reliable data): Frequent trades that absorb size describe liquidity, prices formed by open interaction of buyers and sellers describe free price discovery, many independent traders describe enough participants, and accurate records describe reliable data. Her checklist runs in exactly that order. The other lists use the same four names but swap one adjacent pair, so each looks right until the order of her checks is read carefully.
  33. The correct answer is D (58.54, found by subtracting 38.2 percent of the 30 point swing from 70): The swing is 70 - 40 = 30. A 38.2 percent retracement gives back 0.382 x 30 = 11.46, so the level is 70 - 11.46 = 58.54. Measuring up from the low gives 40 + 11.46 = 51.46, which is the 61.8 percent level. Adding above the high gives 81.46, an extension, and 0.382 x 70 = 26.74 gives 43.26, which applies the ratio to price rather than to the swing.
  34. The correct answer is A (Arithmetic gaps widen from 10 to 20 to 40 units, while logarithmic gaps stay equal): An arithmetic axis measures equal dollar distances, so the moves of 10, 20 and 40 dollars appear as growing gaps. A logarithmic axis measures equal percentage moves, so three doublings look identical. That is why technicians prefer log scales for long or steep trends.
  35. The correct answer is A (Perceived value of -$100, so he likely declines despite a positive expected value of +$100): The expected value is 0.5(600) - 0.5(400) = +$100. With the loss weighted by 2, the perceived value is 0.5(600) - 0.5(2)(400) = 300 - 400 = -$100. He therefore turns down a bet that is favorable in dollars. Using the coefficient on the gain gives +$400, and omitting probabilities gives 600 - 800 = -$200.
  36. The correct answer is B (The record is not risk adjusted, so the extra return may compensate for higher risk): Raw outperformance does not show an abnormal risk-adjusted return: a fund with a different risk profile than its benchmark may simply earn more for bearing more risk, which is the joint hypothesis problem. Outperformance does not require inside information, does not refute every form, and the weak form concerns past prices, not earnings data.
  37. The correct answer is A (Daily reset compounds each day's leveraged return, so multi-period results drift from twice the index.): Leveraged funds reset exposure daily. In the example the fund gains 20 percent, then loses 18.18 percent, so 1.20 times 0.8182 equals 0.982, a loss of about 1.8 percent while the index is flat. This path dependence grows in choppy markets. Fees and futures roll costs exist, but they do not create the effect, and the ETF versus ETN distinction concerns issuer credit risk, not compounding.
  38. The correct answer is B (Implied volatility fell because earnings uncertainty resolved; it gauges expected size of moves, not direction.): Implied volatility rises into a known event because the range of outcomes is wide and drops once the event passes, whatever the direction. It is a two-sided measure of expected move size, not a forecast of up or down. It is derived from option prices and differs from historical volatility, and the pre-event 48 reflected genuine uncertainty rather than error.
  39. The correct answer is D (68, found by subtracting the 12 point head height from the neckline): The head height is 92 minus the neckline of 80, or 12 points. For a top, that distance is projected down from the neckline, so the target is 80 minus 12, or 68. Adding the height gives 104, the right shoulder gives 74, and doubling gives 56, each a nameable error.
  40. The correct answer is B (Distribution occurs near a market bottom, when informed buyers quietly accumulate shares): Distribution happens near a market top, when informed holders sell to an eager public, and accumulation is the phase near a bottom. The statement that puts distribution at a bottom is therefore false. A throwback after an upside breakout and a pullback after a downside breakdown are correctly defined, and markup is the advancing phase between accumulation and distribution.
  41. The correct answer is B (The strategy uses public analyst information, so it tests the semi-strong form, over a short sample.): The weak form concerns past price and volume data, while analyst upgrades are public information, which makes the claim a semi-strong test. Five years is also too short to separate skill from luck, and the benchmark itself is a model. Weak efficiency does not say nobody outperforms, upgrades are not private, and fundamental data belong to the semi-strong form.
  42. The correct answer is C (A signal, because it flags a condition without specifying position size, exits, or risk controls.): A signal identifies a condition of interest, here an oversold reading. A strategy adds position sizing, exits, and risk rules, and a model is a formal quantitative structure. A filter screens out trades from another rule, which this entry condition does not do.
  43. The correct answer is D (Endowment bias, valuing the shares above market worth because she owns them): The manager prizes the shares because she owns them and because of their family history, which is the endowment effect. Status quo bias is tempting because she does nothing, but it describes inertia rather than attachment to these specific shares. Regret aversion centers on dreading a bad outcome from acting, and loss aversion needs an actual loss, and neither is described. Emotional biases like this are hard to remove, so an advisor would adapt the plan around the client.
  44. The correct answer is D (A split-adjusted series, where earlier prices are divided by the split ratio.): A split changes the number of shares, not the company's value, so older prices are divided by the split ratio, here 2, making the 120 close equal 60 and removing the artificial drop. Dividend adjustment handles cash payouts, a continuous series solves a futures expiry problem, and a log scale changes how moves look without correcting the underlying data.
  45. The correct answer is B (Wait for a close below the neckline, then subtract the 13-point head height from 55.): The pattern is only confirmed when price closes decisively below the neckline near 55, so waiting for that close comes first. The minimum target is the vertical distance from the head (68) to the neckline (55), which is 13 points, subtracted from the breakout level: 55 minus 13 equals 42. Selling at the failed right shoulder is tempting but acts before confirmation, and the head-to-neckline distance, not the shoulder height of 7 points, sets the target. Subtracting 13 from the 68 peak gives 55, which is just the neckline and not a target at all.
  46. The correct answer is C (The lack of confirmation warns of weakness, but the uptrend stands until a definite reversal): Dow Theory has several tenets that interact. Averages must confirm each other, so the transport lag is a caution. Volume should expand with the trend, but volume cannot replace confirmation between the averages. The trend is presumed to persist until a definite reversal signal appears, so a single divergence is a warning and not an end. Saying the market discounts everything does not make a divergence meaningless.
  47. The correct answer is A (2.70, the distance from today's high to yesterday's close, the largest of the three measures.): Wilder's true range is the greatest of high minus low (1.50), the absolute high minus prior close (|52.40 - 49.70| = 2.70), and the absolute low minus prior close (|50.90 - 49.70| = 1.20). The largest is 2.70. The 1.50 answer ignores the gap up, 1.20 is the smallest gap measure, and 2.10 is a close to close change that is not one of the three measures.
  48. The correct answer is B (A down candle with a 21-point range, though it closed 3 points above Monday's close.): A candle is classified by close versus open: 1,265 is below the 1,270 open, so it is a down candle even though the close beat Monday's close of 1,262 by 3 points. The range is the high minus the low: 1,276 minus 1,255 equals 21. The up-candle choice mixes close-to-close change with the candle's own direction. The 5-point figure is only the real body (1,270 minus 1,265), and the 6-point figure is just the upper shadow.
  49. The correct answer is B (Extension levels such as 1.618 mark expected pullback depths inside the swing, not targets beyond it): Retracement levels measure pullbacks inside the prior swing, while extensions project targets beyond it, so saying extensions mark pullback depths is the reversal that makes the statement false. The others are true: 90 minus 0.382 times 50 is about 70.9, 90 minus 0.618 times 50 is about 59.1, and extensions project beyond the swing high.
  50. The correct answer is D (The exponential average weights recent closes more, so it reacts faster): A simple average weights all ten closes equally, so it carries the most lag of the common types, while the exponential average gives the newest close a larger factor and reacts sooner. The analyst has reversed the weighting. Each tempting alternative either invents a lookback difference, a second smoothing step, or a wrong speed ranking that standard construction does not support.
  51. The correct answer is D (Treat it as resistance, since broken support often reverses role and a buy faces supply): After support is violated, it commonly becomes resistance. The light-volume rally back to it means buyers lack strong conviction, so sellers who bought at the old level may exit near breakeven. Buying at C faces that supply. Ignoring the break, or treating the level as erased, discards the role reversal principle.
  52. The correct answer is B (Delta rises to about 0.58, since gamma is the change in delta per dollar move.): Gamma measures how much delta changes when the underlying moves one dollar. A $1.00 rise adds about 0.08 to the delta of 0.50, giving roughly 0.58, so the call becomes more sensitive as it moves into the money. Time decay is theta and sensitivity to implied volatility is vega. Gamma is actually largest for options near the strike.
  53. The correct answer is B (Gratitude and reciprocity can bias judgment without the recipient noticing, undermining the objectivity clients rely on): Standard I(B) rests on the recognition that people feel obliged to those who give them things, and this bias can operate even when the person believes she is objective. The Standards set no fixed dollar limit. Publishing a rating first does not remove the obligation she will feel. Her sincere intent does not remove the bias, which is the reason the offer threatens independence.
  54. The correct answer is C (White candle, 4 point body, 2 point upper shadow, and 3 point lower shadow): The close of 106 is above the open of 102, so the candle is white, and the real body is 106 - 102 = 4 points. The upper shadow runs from the body top at 106 to the high at 108, which is 2 points, and the lower shadow runs from the open at 102 down to the low at 99, which is 3 points. Swapping the shadows, using the full 9 point range, or a black candle each fail one of these measurements.
  55. The correct answer is B (Base is the British pound, quote is the US dollar, and the spread is 2 pips.): The first currency in GBP/USD is the base and the second is the quote. Most pairs are quoted to four decimals, and a pip is the fourth decimal, 0.0001. The gap between 1.2650 and 1.2652 is 0.0002, which is 2 pips.
  56. The correct answer is C (A dragonfly doji has a long upper shadow, with open, low and close at the low.): A dragonfly doji has its open, high and close at about the same level near the top and a long lower shadow, so describing a long upper shadow with everything at the low is wrong. The other statements are accurate. Long-legged doji show wide ranges with little net change, spinning tops show small bodies with indecision, and a marubozu shows a dominant side.
  57. The correct answer is A (Candlesticks began in Japanese rice trading in the 1700s and reached the West through Nison.): Candlestick charting grew from rice trading in Japan in the 1700s, long before Dow's writings around 1900, and it reached Western analysts through Steve Nison in the 1990s. The wrong options keep a familiar name or place but swap the source person, move the date past Dow, or reverse the direction of transmission, so the timeline fails.
  58. The correct answer is C (Fat-tailed real returns produce fewer moves beyond three standard deviations than a normal model predicts.): Fat tails (excess kurtosis) mean extreme moves occur more often than a normal curve predicts, so the stated claim is backwards. The 95% range is the mean plus or minus two standard deviations, 0.05 plus or minus 2.0, and positive skew does mean a longer right tail. The law of large numbers describes the sample mean approaching the true mean.
  59. The ratio is 5.50 divided by 2.50, or 2.2, which still meets the 2 to 1 minimum. Using the planned entry gives 3, and adjusting only one side gives 2.4 or 2.75, which understate the effect of slippage.
  60. The correct answer is B (A bull flag, which usually resolves with a breakout in the direction of the prior advance): Parallel boundaries sloping against a sharp prior move, with volume shrinking, define a flag, and in an advance it usually resolves upward. A pennant has converging lines and a wedge has lines that converge while both slope the same way. A rectangle has horizontal boundaries, so none of these fits the parallel sloping channel.
  61. The correct answer is A (Overfitting, also called curve fitting, because the rule was tuned to random noise in past data.): Overfitting means tuning a rule so closely to past data that it captures noise rather than a real pattern, and each added setting is another dial to turn. Poor results on new data are the usual result. Look-ahead bias uses information that was not available at the decision time. Survivorship bias concerns missing failed securities. Walk-forward testing sets rules on one segment and checks them on later unseen data.
  62. The correct answer is B (Divide pre-split prices by three so the series is continuous and shows no false decline.): A split changes the share count and price but not value, so history must be adjusted to remove the artificial gap. Dividing earlier prices by the split ratio makes Monday read 30, and earlier volume is multiplied by three. Multiplying prices would be the fix for a reverse split. Leaving raw data in place would show a false 67 percent crash and distort the moving average.
  63. The correct answer is D (2.80, the distance from today's high of 51.00 down to yesterday's close of 48.20.): True Range is the greatest of three values: high minus low, the absolute value of high minus prior close, and the absolute value of low minus prior close. Here those are 51.00 minus 49.70 equals 1.30, 51.00 minus 48.20 equals 2.80, and 49.70 minus 48.20 equals 1.50. The largest is 2.80. The opening gap of 1.90 is not one of Wilder's three measures, though it hints at why the gap must be counted.
  64. The correct answer is D (The spread narrowed from 1.4 to 1.2 points, so credit risk eased despite higher yields): The credit spread is the corporate yield minus the Treasury yield. Now it is 5.4 minus 4.2, or 1.2 points, down from 1.4 last quarter, so perceived credit risk eased. The corporate yield rose only because the Treasury yield rose by 0.3 points. A higher yield is not a wider spread.
  65. The correct answer is A (Seven, the charterholders only; the others may state their exam progress factually): Only the 7 who have been awarded the charter hold the designation. Passing exam levels without completing the other requirements is not holding the designation, though those candidates may truthfully state which exams they passed. Adding in the Level III passers gives 11, adding every exam passer gives 16, and adding the five Level II passers to the charterholders gives 12, which also has no basis in the Standard.
  66. The correct answer is B (About +1.00 dollar, and gamma, because delta itself changes as the stock moves.): Delta estimates premium change per one dollar in the stock, so 0.50 x 2.00 = +1.00 dollar. Gamma is the rate of change of delta, so the estimate drifts on larger moves. Theta measures time decay and vega measures sensitivity to volatility, and a delta of 0.50 is not a one for one link or a 0.50 dollar flat change.
  67. The correct answer is C (C, A, B: C's ratio rose about 4%, A's was flat, and B's fell about 9%.): Stock A rose 15% as the index rose 15%, so its ratio of 0.0100 is unchanged. Stock B rose 5% against 15%, so its ratio fell from 0.0200 to about 0.0183, down roughly 9%. Stock C fell 6% while the index fell 10%, so its ratio rose from 0.01250 to about 0.01306, up roughly 4%. Ranking by ratio change puts C first, A second and B last.
  68. The correct answer is C (The t-statistic is 4.5, so the return is significant, though a faulty benchmark could explain it.): The t-statistic is 1.8 divided by 0.4, or 4.5, which is highly significant. Efficiency tests always use a benchmark model, so a significant abnormal return means either the market is inefficient or the market model is wrong, the joint hypothesis problem. Dividing the other way gives 0.22, which is the wrong ratio, and earnings announcements are public information, which makes this a semi-strong test.
  69. The correct answer is D (A value function concave for gains and convex for losses gives risk aversion, then risk seeking.): Both gambles have expected values of 416 and minus 416, so she gives up expected value in the gain case and accepts a worse expected outcome in the loss case. Prospect theory explains this with an S-shaped value function, concave in gains and convex in losses, measured from a reference point. Expected utility over final wealth cannot produce risk seeking for losses.
  70. The correct answer is D (0.382, since 34 divided by 89 equals 0.3820): Dividing 34 by 89 gives 0.3820. A term divided by the term one place right gives 55/89 = 0.6180, and three places right gives 21/89 = 0.2360. The Golden Ratio of 1.618 comes from dividing a term by the one before it, such as 89/55 = 1.618. The two place quotient is therefore the 0.382 retracement ratio.
  71. The correct answer is C (Just below the former resistance, since a close beneath it shows reversal failed): The idea behind the trade is that old resistance now acts as support. A close back beneath the level shows that idea failed, so the stop belongs just under the level. A stop exactly on it risks being hit by normal testing, a stop far below the prior swing low risks too much, and a moving average has no connection to the broken level.
  72. The correct answer is A (The target is measured down from the neckline break, so 75 minus 10 gives 65.): The usual measuring rule takes the vertical distance from the head to the neckline, which is 10 points, and projects it from the neckline break in the direction of the new trend. For a top the projection goes down, giving 65, not up to the head's level. Measuring from the head double counts, doubling overstates, and reversal patterns do have targets.
  73. The correct answer is B (Hindsight bias, seeing a past event as having been predictable after the outcome is known): Claiming the reversal was obvious, when her September notes show otherwise, is hindsight bias: after an outcome is known it feels foreseeable. Overconfidence concerns the accuracy of forecasts made in advance, illusion of control concerns believed influence, and conservatism concerns slow updating of views. None of those involves rewriting memory of what was predictable.
  74. The correct answer is A (A sharp rise, a short pause on light volume, then a breakout on rising volume.): A bull flag starts with a steep flagpole, then a short, slightly downward-sloping consolidation on lighter volume, and ends with an upside breakout on rising volume. The slope runs against the trend, and the duration is short, typically a few weeks. Consolidations lasting many months are bases, not flags.
  75. The correct answer is A (Including the factors important to the analysis and recommendation, along with the process's limitations): Standard V(B) requires members to disclose the basic format and principles of the process and to include the important factors behind a recommendation, including limitations. The report leaves out the single assumption that drives the result. Diligence, record keeping and compensation disclosure are separate duties, and the stem does not say any of them failed.
  76. The correct answer is D (A price objective of 68, the breakout level plus the formation's 8-point height): The widest height is 60 minus 52, or 8 points. For an upside breakout, the measuring rule adds that height to the breakout level: 60 plus 8 equals 68. Half the height gives 64, subtracting gives 52, and doubling gives 76, none of which follow the rule.
  77. The correct answer is B (The results are inflated by look-ahead bias, since the entry price was unknowable at signal time.): A signal that fires at the close cannot be filled at that day's low, because the low is only known once the day is over. Using information that was not available at decision time is look-ahead bias, and it flatters both the return and the smoothness of the curve. One lookback parameter is not by itself overfitting, and nothing in the setup mentions delisted stocks, so survivorship is speculation here. A smooth curve built on an impossible fill proves nothing about the rule.
  78. The correct answer is A (Momentum is weakening, a bearish divergence, so tighten the stop and watch for confirmation): A new price high with a lower indicator peak is a bearish divergence: price is reaching further while momentum is not. That is a warning of fading strength, so tightening the stop while waiting for price to confirm fits best. A divergence is not proof of a top, and the idea that the lower reading confirms the high ignores that the peak actually fell.
  79. The correct answer is C (A few participants can move the price, so it reflects single orders, not many judgments.): Technical analysis assumes prices come from many buyers and sellers acting on their own views, in a market with liquidity and free price discovery. With few participants and a dominant holder, single orders can create moves that mean little. The volume-only answer is tempting because volume suffers too, but price patterns are damaged as well.
  80. The correct answer is D (About 2.5 percent of days, since 95 percent lie within two deviations and tails split evenly.): A loss larger than 800 dollars lies more than two standard deviations below the mean, since 800 divided by 400 is 2. About 95 percent of days fall within two deviations, so 5 percent lie outside, split evenly between gains and losses, giving about 2.5 percent on the loss side. The 5 percent answer counts both tails. The 16 percent and 0.15 percent answers belong to cutoffs of one and three deviations.
  81. The correct answer is C (It rises from 0.0500 to about 0.0545, showing outperformance even though both prices rose.): The ratio line is stock price divided by index. At the start it is 45 divided by 900, or 0.0500, and at the end 54 divided by 990, or 0.0545, a rise of about 9 percent. A rising ratio means the stock gained more than the benchmark, since 20 percent beat 10 percent. Both prices rising does not make relative strength flat.
  82. The correct answer is B (A close outside a band is no reversal signal, as strong trends can walk the band.): Bollinger Bands default to a 20-period average and 2 standard deviations, and Bollinger himself warned that tags of a band are not signals on their own. In a strong trend price can keep closing near or outside the band, called a band walk. Treating a statistical extreme as a certain reversal ignores that. The 3 deviation default is wrong, and a close outside a band is not a continuation signal either.
  83. The correct answer is D (Hold the position, raise the stop below the doji's low, and watch the next close): A gravestone doji after an advance warns of a top, but a single candle needs confirmation from the next session. Holding with a tighter stop under the doji's low limits the risk while waiting for that confirmation. Selling everything acts as though confirmation had arrived, and adding ignores the warning. No candlestick rule measures a price target from the length of a shadow.
  84. The correct answer is A (30 percent, which is 1,800 points divided by the 6,000 point total advance.): The signal came 13,800 minus 12,000, or 1,800 points above the low. The total advance was 18,000 minus 12,000, or 6,000 points. The share already used is 1,800 divided by 6,000, or 30 percent. The other values divide by the wrong base or report the remaining share. This lag is a common criticism of Dow Theory.
  85. The correct answer is B (They are anomalies; defenders reply they may be unmodeled risk compensation that fades once widely traded): Momentum and size are named anomalies that standard pricing models cannot explain. An anomaly does not prove inefficiency, because the pricing model may have missed a risk, and many anomalies shrink once widely traded. Claiming proof, blaming costs alone, or calling decades of data noise each overreaches or contradicts the evidence the stem describes.
  86. The correct answer is A (Status quo bias, a preference for leaving things as they are): Status quo bias is a preference for leaving things unchanged, and her stated reason is that change feels uncomfortable even though the holdings no longer fit her goals. The endowment effect would be about valuing the stocks more because she owns them, and she did not say that. Regret aversion would involve fear of being wrong, and self-control bias concerns immediate satisfaction over long term goals. Emotional biases like these are harder to correct than cognitive errors.
  87. The correct answer is D (Post margin and buy, settle gains and losses daily, then sell an offsetting contract before expiry): Futures require a margin deposit rather than full payment, and the account is marked to market each day. A trader who wants no delivery closes the position by taking the opposite side before expiry. Paying full value describes a cash purchase, settling only at expiration ignores daily marking, and delivering at expiration is the path for those who do want the commodity.
  88. The correct answer is C (Availability bias leads investors to weight long run base rates over vivid, recent events.): Availability bias works the other way: vivid or easily recalled events get too much weight and base rates are underused, so the statement is false. The other three statements correctly describe anchoring and adjustment, framing and mental accounting, which is why they tempt a reader looking for an error but are accurate.
  89. The correct answer is C (An anomaly may fade once published or fail to cover costs, so it cannot disprove efficiency.): An anomaly found in historical data may fade once published or may fail to cover costs and risk, so it cannot disprove efficiency alone. Granting the skeptic's claim, noting the small stock concentration, or stacking more anomalies each sounds reasonable, yet none addresses the flaw that a pattern is not a certain, costless profit and that tests are joint with a model.
  90. The correct answer is A (A downtrend with lower highs and lower lows; the rally to 65 is a secondary reaction.): Peaks of 80, 68 and 65 are each lower than the last, and troughs of 60 and 57 are also descending, which satisfies the definition of a downtrend. The rally to 65 is a secondary reaction within that trend. Comparing a rally high with an earlier trough is not a valid test, and the lower highs and lows rule out a range.
  91. The correct answer is B (Outstanding contracts not yet closed out or delivered, each long matched to a short.): Open interest is the count of contracts still open, and every long position has a matching short. Volume is the 90,000 contracts traded in the session and is a different figure. The number of bushels delivered and the number of traders are not what open interest counts, and a rise from 410,000 to 435,000 means more contracts are open.
  92. The correct answer is B (A high put/call ratio shows traders are buying mostly calls, and contrarians treat it as bearish.): A high put/call ratio means put volume is large relative to call volume, which shows pessimism, and contrarians usually read extreme pessimism as bullish. The statement therefore has both the meaning and the contrarian reading backwards. The other three statements correctly describe the COT groups, the VIX and mutual fund cash.
  93. The correct answer is C (A secondary reaction, lasting six weeks and retracing about 37 percent of the advance.): The advance is 3,000 points and the decline is 33,000 minus 31,900, or 1,100 points, which is 36.7 percent of the advance. A six-week decline is longer than three weeks, so it is not minor. A primary reversal would require a break below the prior reaction low. The 63 percent figure comes from (31,900 - 30,000) / 3,000, which is the portion of the advance still intact.
  94.
