The correct answer is B (Use a split and dividend adjusted series so earlier prices are rescaled and the drop vanishes.): A split halts nothing in value, yet unadjusted prices show a false gap that moving averages treat as a real crash. Adjusting earlier prices for splits and dividends gives a continuous series. Repairing one bar leaves the older prices on the wrong scale, weekly bars only hide the gap in one place, and ignoring nearby signals leaves the averages themselves distorted for weeks.
  95. The correct answer is A (A close below $77.60, which is 3 percent under the trendline value.): The 3 percent filter requires a close beyond the trendline by 3 percent of its value. For $80.00 that is 0.03 x 80.00 = $2.40, so the confirming close is below 80.00 minus 2.40 = $77.60. Adding the amount gives $82.40, the wrong side. Using three dollars gives $77.00, and a decimal slip of 0.3 percent gives $79.76, a much weaker test.
  96. The correct answer is D (6 percent, since 15,000 shares divided by 250,000 shares equals 0.06): The order is 15,000 / 250,000 = 0.06, or 6 percent of an average day. That is large enough to move a thinly traded stock and spoil the free price discovery the method relies on. Dividing the other way gives 16.7, which is not a percentage of volume. A decimal slip gives 0.6 percent, and halving for the buy side has no basis in the arithmetic.
  97. The correct answer is A (A VIX spike precisely times a market bottom, so buyers can act on the first spike.): The VIX is built from S&P 500 option prices and is annualized with a 30 day horizon. It tends to rise in sharp declines as traders buy protection, and a low reading can persist for years. Spikes often accompany market bottoms, but they are not precise timing tools, since the VIX has stayed high for weeks while prices kept falling. Acting on the first spike alone is the statement that does not hold.
  98. The correct answer is C (A 50% retracement, a normal pullback within a continuing uptrend, not a reversal.): The advance covered 40 points and the pullback gave back 20, so the retracement is 20 divided by 40, or 50%. Pullbacks of about a third to two thirds are normal within a trend, especially when volume shrinks during the decline. Dividing by 80 or by 60 uses the wrong base, and a half retracement alone does not signal distribution.
  99. The correct answer is D (A martingale, because expected next price equals today's while variance may depend on the past): A martingale requires only that the expected next price equals the current price given the history, so clustered volatility is allowed. A random walk is stricter and requires changes that are independent and identically distributed, so volatility clustering breaks it even when returns are uncorrelated. Predictable size is not predictable direction. The arcsine law is about how long a random walk stays on one side of its starting level.
  100. The correct answer is C (Body from open to close, shadows to the extremes, then color by close versus open.): A candlestick body spans open to close, shadows extend to the session high and low, and the body color is set by comparing the close with the open. Using the prior close is how gaps are described on bar charts, not how candle color is set. The high versus low comparison has no direction, and swapping the body and shadows distorts the shape.
  101. The correct answer is B (Each U.S. dollar, the base currency, now buys more Canadian dollars, so the long position gained.): In USD/CAD the U.S. dollar is the base and the Canadian dollar is the quote, so the price states how many Canadian dollars one U.S. dollar buys. A rise from 1.3600 to 1.3700 means the base currency gained, and a long position profits. A higher quote means the quote currency is weaker, not stronger. Because the U.S. dollar is on one side, the pair is not a cross pair.
  102. The correct answer is C (Volume is not confirming, since it grew on the decline and shrank on the rally): Dow expected volume to expand in the direction of the primary trend. Here volume is heavier on the decline and lighter on the rally, which is a warning. A warning is not a reversal signal, since prices still need to show a definite reversal. Low volume on a rally is not bullish, and the volume tenet applies to the market as a whole, not only to the transports.
  103. The correct answer is D (The stock is outperforming the benchmark, which shows relative strength even though the market is weak.): A ratio line divides the asset's price by the benchmark's price, so it rises whenever the asset does better than the benchmark. That can happen when the asset falls less than the index, so absolute gains are not needed. Rising ratio means outperformance, not underperformance. The ratio line carries no volatility information and only compares the two price paths.
  104. The correct answer is D (A hanging man forms after a decline and signals that sellers are exhausted.): A hanging man has the hammer shape but appears after an advance and warns of a top, so describing it after a decline with sellers exhausted is false. The shooting star, piercing line and tweezer top are each described correctly in the other statements. The statement that confuses the hanging man with the hammer is the exception.
  105. The correct answer is A (Rising open interest in a falling market usually means new shorts are added, supporting the downtrend.): When open interest rises while prices fall, new contracts are being opened and the new shorts are driving prices lower, which supports the downtrend. His claim that the decline must reverse because buyers entered ignores that every new contract also has a seller. Short covering would reduce open interest, not raise it. Each contract has two sides, but the direction of open interest still carries information, and volume is a separate confirmation.
  106. The correct answer is B (Secondary reactions, which retrace part of the primary advance as traders take profits and correct excesses.): Dow compared the market to the tide, waves and ripples. The tide is the primary trend, the waves are secondary reactions lasting roughly three weeks to three months, and the ripples are minor daily moves. Declines of a few weeks to a few months inside a rising series of peaks and troughs fit the waves, which are corrections rather than reversals.
  107. The correct answer is B (Trapped buyers sell at breakeven when price returns, which supplies that level.): After support fails, buyers who bought near that level are underwater and plan to exit at breakeven when price returns, which creates supply and turns the old support into resistance. Short covering would create demand, round number clustering is real but does not explain polarity, and a lack of volume describes a symptom rather than the psychology behind the reversal of roles.
  108. The correct answer is D (She anchored on the old forecast and adjusted too little, so should rebuild from new data.): Anchoring and adjustment starts from an initial value and moves too little toward the evidence. Here the forecast moved only 0.40 of a 1.20 gap. The remedy is to estimate afresh from the new data rather than from the old number. Framing, availability and mental accounting describe different mistakes.
  109. The correct answer is C (Adding the letters CFA after his name on new business cards and in his email signature): Only someone awarded the charter may use the CFA designation, so placing the letters after his name implies a credential he does not yet hold. Stating factually that he passed an exam level, describing the Program, or saying he has finished all three levels without claiming the designation are all acceptable. The wrong choices tempt because they also mention the CFA, but they make accurate statements.
  110. The correct answer is C (Theta from passing time and vega from falling implied volatility, while delta contributed nothing.): The stock stayed at 100, so delta contributed nothing. The premium fell for two reasons: twelve days remained, so theta eroded time value, and implied volatility dropped from 35 to 27 percent, so vega cut the premium. Gamma describes how delta changes and does not explain a loss here. Vega affects short dated options too, though less than long dated ones, so it cannot be ignored.
  111. The correct answer is D (Double bottom, a reversal pattern implying higher prices after a close above the peak.): A double bottom has two lows at nearly the same level separated by a rally, and it is complete when price closes above the peak between them. It follows a decline and implies a reversal upward. A double top is the mirror image, a rectangle is a continuation pattern, and a rounding bottom lacks distinct twin lows.
  112. The correct answer is A (Loss aversion, overconfidence, status quo bias, and endowment bias.): Loss aversion, overconfidence, status quo, endowment, regret aversion and self-control are emotional biases. Anchoring is an information processing bias, and confirmation, hindsight and representativeness are belief perseverance biases, so each wrong list contains at least one cognitive item.
  113. The correct answer is A (Retracements are measured back from the high, so 0.382 gives 120 minus 15.3, about 104.7.): A retracement gives back part of the range measured from the high: 120 minus 0.382 times 40 is about 104.7. Adding the ratio to the low, as she did, produced 95.3, which is not the level. Applying the ratio to the whole price, subtracting from the low, or switching to 0.5 each uses a wrong base or ratio and misses the required 104.7.
  114. The correct answer is B (Expected price stays at $80, with a rough 95 percent band of $60 to $100): A martingale has no expected change, so the expected price is $80. The standard deviation grows with the square root of time: 2 × √25 = 2 × 5 = $10, so two standard deviations are $20 and the band is $60 to $100. The $76 to $84 band uses one day, $66 to $94 uses √50 = 7.07, and $30 to $130 adds 25 × $2 = $50 linearly.
  115. The correct answer is D (A weak positive linear correlation, so the oil change explains little of the stock's change.): A positive sign means the variables tend to rise together, and 0.15 is close to zero, so the link is weak and r squared is only about 0.02. Direction and strength are separate, so the upward tilt does not make the correlation strong. Scatter lowers the size of r, not its sign, and a perfect correlation needs all points on a line.
  116. The correct answer is A (Early quotients such as 1 and 2 are far from 1.618; the ratio only approaches it.): Each Fibonacci number is the sum of the two before it, and a term divided by its predecessor approaches 1.618 as terms grow. The first quotients, 1, 2 and 1.5, are nowhere near it, so the claim that the first quotient equals 1.618 fails. The quotients do swing around the limit, but they converge, and the next term gives 0.618, not two places later, which is why the other critiques fail.
  117. The correct answer is B (Volatility expectations are low and traders feel complacent, a calm phase that often precedes a rise.): A VIX in the low teens signals very low expected volatility, which is read as complacency. Volatility cycles suggest quiet phases are eventually followed by active ones, but a low VIX can persist for years in a calm bull market. It is not a precise top signal and it gives no timetable. Rising put demand would push the VIX up, so that reading is reversed.
  118. The correct answer is C (Logarithmic scale, because it shows equal percentage changes as equal vertical distances over long periods): Moves from 5 to 10 and from 40 to 80 are both 100 percent gains, and only a logarithmic scale draws them with the same height. An arithmetic scale spaces equal point changes equally, so it exaggerates later moves in a long advance. Log scaling does not require any change to the volume axis.
  119. The correct answer is C (State the hypothesis, define rules and data, test on history, then evaluate results before live use.): The scientific method begins with a clear hypothesis, then defines exactly how it will be tested, runs the test on data, and evaluates the evidence before anyone commits capital. Fitting a story to the best result afterward is data mining, which tends to produce false discoveries. Trading live first puts money at risk before evidence exists, and using history afterward only to confirm a decision already made reverses the logic of testing.
  120. The correct answer is A (The spread widened from 1.5 to 2.5 points; corporate prices fell as investors demanded more yield.): The credit spread is the yield difference between a risky bond and a Treasury of the same maturity. It grew by one point because the corporate yield rose while the Treasury yield stayed fixed. Bond prices move opposite to yields, so corporate prices dropped. Yield curve shape compares maturities, not credit quality, and an unchanged Treasury yield means its price did not move.
  121. The correct answer is A (The compound rate is the geometric mean, about minus 3.5% a year, leaving near $90,000.): Growth factors are 1.50, 0.50 and 1.20, and their product is 0.90, so $100,000 becomes $90,000. The cube root of 0.90 is about 0.9655, a compound rate near minus 3.5% per year. The arithmetic mean overstates multi-period growth when returns swing widely, and the median ignores two of the three results.
  122. The correct answer is C (Signals need both averages to confirm a trend, so they arrive after much of the move.): Dow Theory waits for both averages to confirm each other and for a definite break of earlier highs or lows. That demand for confirmation keeps false signals low but means a trend is recognized only after much of it has already happened, which is the lag critics point to. The theory uses closing prices, not intraday prices, and volume is a supporting check rather than a timed requirement.
  123. The correct answer is B (2.0 percent of shares outstanding, because 20,000 divided by 1,000,000 equals 0.02): The stated basis is shares outstanding, so 20,000 divided by 1,000,000 is 0.02, or 2.0 percent. Using the free float of 400,000 gives 5.0 percent, a figure that answers a different question. A decimal slip gives 0.2 percent. Standard VI(A) needs the stated figure to be accurate, since readers use it to judge how large her personal interest is.
  124. The correct answer is B (A public blockchain shows transactions and holdings, adding on-chain measures that complement price and volume studies.): Around-the-clock trading and sentiment-driven price action describe both bitcoin and major currency pairs, so they cannot be the distinguishing feature. The dealer network is a currency-market structure. A public blockchain exposes wallet balances and transfers, which gives on-chain data that has no counterpart in currency markets and supports analysis beyond price and volume.
  125. The correct answer is D (The averages must confirm each other, so a signal from one average alone is not sufficient.): Dow required both the Industrial and Transportation averages to give the same signal. A break in the Industrials alone is unconfirmed. The other tenets are misapplied: discounting does not cancel a break, Dow favored closing prices over intraday extremes, and volume confirms a trend when it expands in the direction of the trend.
  126. The correct answer is C (The 30-day volatility of the S&P 500 that index option prices imply, a forward-looking expectation.): The VIX is built from S&P 500 index option prices and states the volatility those prices imply over the next 30 days. Realized swings over the past weeks are historical volatility, which the student confused with it. The VIX gives size of expected moves, not direction, and it covers index options, not all stock options.
  127. The correct answer is A (The averages discount everything, so known news is reflected in prices as soon as it appears): Her claim that the news is already priced in is the tenet that the averages discount everything. Confirmation, volume, and trend persistence are real Dow tenets and they might apply to how to treat the decline, but none of them says news is already reflected. The detail that she talks about news, not about a second average or volume, points to discounting.
  128. The correct answer is A (The house money effect: after a gain a loss feels smaller, so risk tolerance rises): Treating prior gains as house money lowers sensitivity to losses and raises risk taking, which fits her words about the market's money. The disposition effect concerns when positions are sold, overconfidence would require her to credit skill, and she explicitly does not. Regret aversion could explain a purchase, but it does not match her reasoning about whose money is at risk.
  129. The correct answer is D (Use an intermediate length near 50 days, balancing lag against false signals, plus a confirming close.): Shorter averages react faster but produce more whipsaws, while longer ones filter noise but lag, so an intermediate length with a confirmation rule balances the two. A shorter exponential average adds noise, and adding a longer average adds more lag. Wilder smoothing is slower than an ordinary exponential of the same length, so it would not speed the signal.
  130. The correct answer is C (Falling open interest shows shorts covering, not new buyers entering, so fresh money is absent.): In a healthy advance, open interest rises as new money opens positions. A rising price with falling open interest means positions are being closed, mostly shorts buying back, so no new demand is arriving. New shorts would raise open interest, and open interest carries information beyond volume, since it counts open positions.
  131. The correct answer is C (Fama's revised categories keep the original labels weak form, semi-strong form, and strong form tests): In 1991 Fama renamed the categories as tests for return predictability, event studies, and tests for private information, so the claim that the original three labels were kept is false. The other statements are accurate: a flawed pricing model can create an apparent abnormal return, strong form became private information tests, and event studies remained.
  132. The correct answer is A (Decline the service and hold a low-cost index fund, because public filings are already priced.): Semi-strong efficiency says published information is already in prices, so scanning public filings should not earn abnormal returns after costs, and an index fund fits that belief. Less efficient small stocks, a speed advantage, and charting each sound sensible, but the first ignores her belief, the second assumes slow price adjustment, and the third forgets that past prices are public too.
  133. The correct answer is C (20 percent above her DCF value, from anchoring on the 40 IPO price.): The target exceeds the DCF value by 36 divided by 30 minus 1, which is 20 percent. Her estimate was pulled toward the IPO price of 40 and not moved enough toward the value her model produced, which is anchoring and adjustment. The 29 percent figure divides by the market price of 28, and the 10 percent figure compares 36 to 40, so neither answers the question asked.
  134. The correct answer is D (The disposition effect, selling winners early while holding losers to avoid locking in a loss): Selling winners quickly while riding losers is the disposition effect. Loss aversion is the underlying preference that drives it, so it is tempting, but it names the feeling rather than the observable behavior of realizing gains and deferring losses. Mental accounting actually keeps positions in separate accounts, not one pool, and the house money effect describes bigger bets after gains, which would not explain selling those gains within days.
  135. The correct answer is D (VWAP is 50.65, and her 50.80 fill was above it, so she paid more.): VWAP equals total dollar value divided by total shares: 100,000 plus 151,500 plus 255,000 is 506,500, divided by 10,000 shares gives 50.65. A buyer who paid 50.80 paid 0.15 more than the average, which is a worse fill. A lower price would be the better result for a buyer, and dividing by block count rather than shares ignores the volume weighting.
  136. The correct answer is C (Weeks one to six: new long money entering; week seven: long liquidation, no new short commitment.): Rising price with rising open interest means new money is entering, which confirms the advance. In week seven open interest fell, so contracts were being closed, which fits longs liquidating rather than shorts opening. New short selling would have raised open interest. Covering would reduce it during a rally, which does not match the first phase.
  137. The correct answer is B (R squared is 0.64, so 64% of the variation fits the line, not proof of cause.): R squared is the square of Pearson's r, so 0.80 times 0.80 equals 0.64. That share of the variation is accounted for by the fitted line, and 36% is not. Correlation measures how closely two series move together, not which one drives the other, so a shared driver such as global demand could explain both.
  138. The correct answer is C (Skip the analyst and hold a diversified low cost index fund, since public data is priced.): If prices already reflect all public information, paid analysis of reports and releases cannot add excess return after costs, so a diversified index fund is the logical choice. Semi-strong efficiency also covers past prices, so charts add nothing. Small stocks, speed, and charts each tempt, but each contradicts the belief the manager accepts.
  139. The correct answer is D (Crowd behavior forms similar structures at every interval, though longer intervals carry greater significance.): The fractal idea says crowd psychology produces similar shapes on all time scales, so pattern rules transfer. A longer interval involves more participants and more time, so its pattern carries greater significance and a larger projection. Equal weight for all intervals, denial that short charts follow the rules, and the claim that slow patterns are less reliable each break that principle.
  140. The correct answer is A (S-shaped around a reference point, concave for gains, convex for losses, and steeper for losses): Prospect theory measures outcomes as gains and losses from a reference point. Its value function is concave for gains, convex for losses, and steeper on the loss side, which reflects loss aversion. A curve that is concave everywhere describes the classic utility picture. The reversed curvature and straight lines both lose the diminishing sensitivity and the steeper loss slope.
  141. The correct answer is D (A martingale, because the expected next price equals today's price though changes are dependent.): A martingale requires only that the expected future price equal the current price given the information available. A random walk is stricter, since changes must be independent and identically distributed, and clustering of volatility violates that. Predictable volatility does not make the direction of the next price change predictable.
  142. The correct answer is A (Yesterday's change says nothing about the next change, so extrapolating it offers no edge): A random walk means each change is independent of the past, so recent moves, whether up or down, say nothing about the next one. It does not force reversion to a starting level, and long streaks happen by chance. A trader who expects a trend or a reversal from the last move is assuming a dependence the model rules out.
  143. The correct answer is D (Set the stop, compute 3 risk and 6 reward, then judge the 2-to-1 ratio.): The stop is placed before entry so the risk is known: 40 minus 37 equals 3 points. The reward is the target minus the entry, 46 minus 40, equal to 6 points, so the ratio is 6 divided by 3, which is 2 to 1 and just meets the minimum. Inverting the figures gives 1 to 2, and setting the stop after entry removes the discipline of defined risk. Measuring reward from the stop gives 9 points, but that is not what the trade earns from the entry.

Practice Test 4 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is A (The day's range is 0.80, the difference between the close and the open): The range is the high minus the low, or 1.60, and the close minus the open of 0.80 is the real body, so the range statement is false. The close of 52.90 is above the open of 52.10 and above the range midpoint of 52.60. A bar chart does show all four prices, while a line chart of closes shows just the close.
  2. The correct answer is D (The higher-low pattern has failed, and the broken 53 level may now act as resistance.): An uptrend is a sequence of higher highs and higher lows. A close at 51 undercuts the most recent low at 53, so the pattern has failed and the trend is in question. Under the polarity principle, support that is broken tends to become resistance. Staying above 46 ignores the latest low, and a single lower low raises doubt rather than confirming a new downtrend.
  3. The correct answer is B (The median shifts toward an extreme outlier more than the mean, since it uses every value.): The false statement says the median shifts toward an extreme outlier more than the mean, and that it uses every value. The mean uses every value, so it moves more with outliers, while the median depends only on the middle rank and resists them. The other statements are true: a few large gains can pull the mean above the median, the mode can describe categories, and the geometric mean not exceeds the arithmetic mean for positive values.
  4. The correct answer is B (Bollinger Bands widen with standard deviation of closes, Keltner Channels with ATR around an exponential average.): Bollinger Bands are set a multiple of the standard deviation of closes from a simple moving average, so they react to dispersion of closing prices. Keltner Channels are set a multiple of ATR from an exponential average, so they react to the bar ranges, including gaps. The two behave differently because they measure volatility differently.
  5. The correct answer is A (Tighten the stop and watch for confirmation, since the long upper shadow shows rejection.): The long upper shadow shows buyers pushed price up but sellers returned it to the open, a warning of weakening demand after an advance. A single candle still needs confirmation, so tightening the stop protects profit without overreacting. Adding because the body is technically white ignores the rejection, and selling everything treats the warning as proof. Keeping the old stop ignores the information the high provides.
  6. The correct answer is D (Commercials are the informed group, and record small-speculator longs are a contrary bearish warning.): Technicians watch commercial hedgers as the group with the best information about their own market and treat small speculators as the crowd to fade at extremes. A record small-speculator long, with commercials heavily short, is a bearish configuration. Reading a hedger short as bullish, or swapping the groups, reverses the logic. Hedgers are active and informative, so ignoring them is also wrong.
  7. The correct answer is A (No, because the Transports did not close above their prior peak close of 15,260.): Dow regarded the closing price as the most important price and required both averages to close above prior peaks to confirm a bull trend. The Transports closed at 15,190, below 15,260, so there is no confirmation yet. The intraday spike to 15,310 does not count. Dow did not demand that both highs occur on the same day, only that they confirm each other within a reasonable time.
  8. The correct answer is A (The opening price is treated as the day's most important price because it carries overnight news.): Dow named the closing price as the most important price of the day because it reflects the market's settled judgment after intraday noise, so the opening price statement is the false one. Distribution by informed holders, non confirmation between the averages, and volume behavior are standard Dow teaching, so each tempts as the exception but is consistent with the theory.
  9. The correct answer is D (Independent changes mean a streak does not make a reversal more likely.): If daily changes are independent, past streaks carry no information about the next change, so a stock is not due for a reversal. A random walk includes neither mean reversion nor momentum, and streaks of 12 in 14 can arise by chance. A streak alone cannot show the series is not a random walk.
  10. The correct answer is D (A bearish engulfing pattern has a small black body fully inside the prior white body.): In a bearish engulfing pattern the black body of the second day engulfs the prior white body, so it is the larger body that wraps the smaller one. A black body fully inside a white body is a bearish harami, which signals indecision. The shooting star, piercing line and tweezer top descriptions are all standard.
  11. The correct answer is C (Real body of 4 points and a full range of 11 points.): The real body is the distance between the open and close: 216 minus 212 equals 4. The range runs from the low to the high: 219 minus 208 equals 11. Measuring from the open to the high gives 7, which is neither measure, and swapping the two measures reverses their meaning.
  12. The correct answer is C (Use the stochastic oscillator, a momentum tool that often turns ahead of price.): Momentum oscillators such as the stochastic measure the speed of price change and often turn before price itself does, which makes them leading tools. Moving average crossovers are built from past prices, so they confirm a trend only after it has started. A longer average lags even more, and the ADX measures strength of a trend, not its direction.
  13. The correct answer is B (Measure excess return, adjust for risk, deduct trading costs, then test on another period.): An anomaly needs an excess return to be measured first, then adjusted for risk because a higher return can simply pay for higher risk. Trading costs come off next, since a rule that disappears after costs offers no real opportunity, and a test on other data checks that the result is not a quirk of one sample. Costs cannot be deducted from a return that has not yet been measured.
  14. The correct answer is C (Falling open interest means positions were being closed, so the rally reflects covering, not new buying): Open interest counts open contracts, with each long matched to a short. When it falls, traders are closing positions rather than opening new ones, so a rising price on falling open interest suggests short covering. Open interest is not volume, it counts both sides, and it updates daily.
  15. The correct answer is A (Confirm the close below the neckline, then subtract 0.0400 to reach 1.1600.): The measuring rule takes the vertical distance from the head to the neckline, here 1.2400 minus 1.2000 = 0.0400, and subtracts it from the neckline breakout level, 1.2000 minus 0.0400 = 1.1600. Projecting upward reverses the pattern, halving the height has no standard basis, and projecting from the head puts the target at the neckline itself, which price has already reached.
  16. The correct answer is A (Herding is investors imitating the crowd; a bubble is the resulting price far above value): Herding describes behavior: investors follow others instead of their own analysis. A bubble describes an outcome: prices rise far above value that fundamentals can justify. Herding can fuel a bubble, and it also appears when investors rush to sell, so neither term is tied to direction or investor type.
  17. The correct answer is B (The note is unsecured bank debt that carries the bank's credit risk; the fund holds assets.): An ETN is a promise by the issuing bank to pay the index return, so its value depends on the bank's creditworthiness as well as the index. An ETF holds the underlying securities or contracts in a fund structure, which keeps the assets separate from the sponsor. A downgrade of the bank therefore hurts the note holder and does not hurt the fund holder in the same way.
  18. The correct answer is B (Treat lagging breadth as a warning and trim exposure or tighten stops, since few stocks participate.): A record index close with a lagging cumulative advance decline line is a negative breadth divergence, which suggests few stocks carry the advance. Reducing exposure or tightening stops respects that warning without overreacting. Adding exposure ignores it, dismissing divergences removes a useful warning, and moving fully to cash assumes breadth can pinpoint a top, which it cannot.
  19. The correct answer is A (Relative strength, which compares performance against a benchmark instead of judging the asset's own trend.): Her statement compares the ETF with the market, which is relative strength. Momentum looks at the ETF alone. The RSI is a bounded oscillator built from the ETF's own gains and losses, and breadth counts advancing and declining issues across a whole market, so neither compares one asset with a benchmark.
  20. The correct answer is A (It resembles the January effect, whose edge may shrink once widely known, so test after costs): A 2.4 point January premium in small caps matches the documented January effect, a calendar anomaly tied to size. Anomalies tend to weaken after they are published and traded, and costs reduce what remains, so the sensible step is to test net results out of sample. One anomaly does not prove the strong form false, and it is not momentum.
  21. The correct answer is B (Add 1 to each return, multiply them together, take the cube root, then subtract 1.): Growth factors are 1.10, 1.20 and 0.95, whose product is 1.254. The cube root is about 1.078, so the geometric mean is about 7.8 percent. The arithmetic mean of 8.3 percent overstates compound growth. Skipping the add one step breaks the multiplication, and dividing by three replaces the needed root with an average.
  22. The correct answer is D (It stands at 24,800, a negative divergence with the index high that suggests narrowing participation.): Tuesday's net change is 1,800 minus 1,200, or plus 600, giving 25,600. Wednesday's net change is 1,100 minus 1,900, or minus 800, giving 24,800. The line fell while the index made a new high, a negative divergence that signals narrowing leadership. Adding the absolute values gives 26,400, skipping Wednesday leaves 25,600, and subtracting declines alone gives 21,900.
  23. The correct answer is B (A double top breakout needs an X column topping the prior X column by one box.): The buy signal appears when an X column rises one box above the top of the previous X column. Here the first top is 46 and the second reaches 47, so the signal is valid. The pullback to 43 is a legitimate three-box reversal from 46. Nothing in the pattern requires price to retrace to the starting base or to clear the old top by the full reversal amount.
  24. The correct answer is B (Unadjusted prices record the split as a real collapse, so history must be split-adjusted): A 2-for-1 split halves the quoted price without changing the value of the holding. Unadjusted data treats that halving as a genuine 50 percent loss, and the moving average then reads a collapse that never happened. Survivorship bias concerns which stocks are in the file, not their split history. A shorter interval would show the same gap, and reinvested dividends belong to a different adjustment.
  25. The correct answer is C (Volume counts contracts traded in the session, while open interest counts outstanding ones, up 12,000.): Volume is the number of contracts that changed hands in the period, including trades that open or close positions. Open interest is the total of contracts still open, counting each contract once, and it rose from 468,000 to 480,000, a net gain of 12,000. Volume of 215,000 therefore does not mean 215,000 new positions, since many trades closed or transferred existing ones.
  26. The correct answer is B (Survivorship bias disappears when testing only securities currently listed, since they have complete histories.): The false claim is that survivorship bias disappears when testing only securities that are currently listed. Currently listed securities are exactly the survivors, so a test limited to them leaves out failures and inflates results. The other statements are accurate: revised data creates look-ahead bias, a backtest adds trading mechanics beyond a signal test, and more tuned parameters relative to the data raise the chance of overfitting.
  27. The correct answer is B (OBV adds the full day's volume whenever the close is above that day's open.): OBV compares each close with the prior close, not with the same day's open, so the statement tying it to the open is false. It is a cumulative total whose direction matters more than the level, and divergence from price is one of its main uses. The other three describe the standard construction and interpretation.
  28. The correct answer is A (A close below the neckline is required, so shorting at the right shoulder acts too early.): A head and shoulders top is a reversal pattern that is only complete when price closes below the neckline drawn through the troughs. Until then the right shoulder may simply be a pause in an uptrend, so shorting early risks being wrong. The typical volume pattern is lighter volume on the right shoulder, and no retest of the head or extra shoulder is required.
  29. The correct answer is B (Under the semi-strong form, insiders trading on unannounced merger news should not earn abnormal returns.): The semi-strong form covers only public information, so insiders holding private merger news could still profit; only the strong form rules that out, which makes the statement about semi-strong insiders the one that does not fit. The weak, strong and semi-strong statements about chart rules, insiders and annual reports each match their form.
  30. The correct answer is D (6.25 percent, which is higher than the 5 percent at par): Current yield is the annual coupon divided by the price. The coupon is 5 per year, so 5 divided by 80 equals 0.0625, or 6.25 percent, up from 5 percent when the bond traded at par. Price and yield move in opposite directions. Keeping 5 percent ignores the price change, 5 times 80 over 100 gives 4 percent and moves the wrong way, and 80 divided by 5 is 16, an inverted ratio.
  31. The correct answer is A (A strong positive linear relationship appears, and the distant point deserves checking as a possible outlier.): Most points rise together from lower left to upper right, which indicates a positive linear relationship. A single point far from the cluster is a candidate outlier, and it can pull the correlation coefficient up or down, so it deserves a separate check. Correlation never establishes causation, whatever its size. One stray point does not change the sign of the pattern formed by 39 others, nor does it erase that pattern.
  32. The correct answer is C (Larger size makes a losing streak cut deeper, and deeper drawdowns need larger gains to recover.): Risk of ruin depends on the fraction of capital risked per trade, because losing streaks are a normal feature of any system. Doubling size turns the next streak into a much larger drawdown, and a drawdown of 20 percent needs a 25 percent gain to recover, not 20. The symmetric recovery idea is tempting but wrong, and market impact is negligible for a small retail order. Saying a streak is normal is true, yet that is exactly why size must stay fixed.
  33. The correct answer is A (Outcomes are judged from a reference point, with risk aversion in gains, risk seeking in losses.): Prospect theory measures outcomes as gains or losses from a reference point such as the purchase price. The value function is concave for gains, favoring locking them in, and convex for losses, favoring a gamble to avoid a sure loss. A reversed attitude, probability weighting, or mental accounting touches the topic but fails to give risk aversion in one case and risk seeking in the other.
  34. The correct answer is A (A hypothesis must be tested so it can be proven false, including all contradicting cases.): A testable hypothesis must be able to fail, so every case that contradicts it has to be counted. By keeping only the breakouts that worked she made the idea unfalsifiable. Sample size matters, but failed cases are not outliers to set aside. Requiring another indicator before counting a failure protects the hypothesis from evidence. Exact rules are good practice, yet they do not allow dropping failures that meet the rules.
  35. The correct answer is C (Accumulation may be underway, but no new primary uptrend is signaled until the Transports confirm): After a long decline with thin volume and a base, the setting matches accumulation, but Dow requires both averages to close beyond their prior highs, so the Transports must also confirm. The Industrials alone do not confirm a bull market. Public participation follows confirmation, and distribution describes a late bull market, not the turn after a decline.
  36. The correct answer is B (Suitability is judged client by client, considering risk tolerance and the whole portfolio): Suitability depends on each client's objectives, constraints and risk tolerance and how a holding fits the whole portfolio, so an equity mandate does not make every equity suitable. A firm wide approved list and a security rating are inputs, not the test. Reviewing only on client request also fails, because the manager must keep checking fit as circumstances change.
  37. The correct answer is B (The ratio line fell about 15 percent, so Stock X underperformed despite rising.): The stock gained 10 percent and the index gained 30 percent. The ratio line goes from 40 over 2,000, or 0.0200, to 44 over 2,600, or 0.0169, a fall of about 15 percent. A rising stock can still lose relative strength, and subtracting 10 from 30 does not give a ratio change.
  38. The correct answer is C (Accumulation, then markup, then distribution, with a markdown phase expected next.): The quiet base near lows is accumulation, the steady advance is markup, and the heavy sideways trade near highs is distribution, which normally precedes markdown. Reversing the cycle misreads the location of the base. Putting distribution before markup or markup before accumulation breaks the fixed order of the four phases.
  39. The correct answer is A (The value function is convex for losses, so a sure loss makes people risk seeking.): Prospect theory draws the value function convex in the loss region, so a sure loss is disliked more than a gamble with the same expected loss, which explains doubling down. Concave curvature for losses would predict selling, and that curvature belongs to the gain region. The function is steeper for losses than gains, not the reverse. Small probabilities are overweighted, not shrunk.
  40. The correct answer is B (A throwback, the return to a broken resistance level after an upside breakout): After an upside breakout, a return to the old resistance is called a throwback. After a downside breakdown, the return to the old support is a pullback, so that term fits the other direction. Here price held at the level and resumed rising, so the breakout did not fail, and the move is a retest of the level, not a Dow secondary reaction.
  41. The correct answer is D (Prospect theory explains the switch: risk aversion for gains and risk seeking for losses.): The investor avoids risk when facing gains and seeks it when facing losses, the fourfold pattern that follows from an S-shaped value function around a reference point. A single concave curve would predict risk aversion in both cases. Loss aversion is a steeper slope, not risk aversion in losses, and the reference point is central to the theory.
  42. The correct answer is D (Extreme pessimism is a contrarian buy setup, best acted on once price confirms a turn.): Contrary opinion treats extreme fear as a sign that most sellers have already sold, leaving little supply to push prices lower. A high put/call ratio, a spiking VIX and a very low bullish percentage all show fear, so together they form a contrarian buy setup, though the technician still waits for price to turn. Following the heavy put buying joins the crowd at its extreme, and a high VIX reflects fear, not complacency.
  43. The correct answer is A (An in-the-money put, with the 6.40 being the premium paid for the contract.): A right to sell is a put, and with the stock at 40 below the 45 strike it is in the money, with intrinsic value of 5.00. The 6.40 paid is the premium, which includes 1.40 of time value. A call gives the right to buy, an out-of-the-money put would need a stock above 45, and the premium and the 5.00 intrinsic value are different numbers.
  44. The correct answer is D (Find each bar's true range from three measures, average the first 14, then smooth later values.): Wilder's method takes the largest of three measures for each bar: the high minus low, the high minus the prior close, and the prior close minus the low. That captures overnight gaps. The first 14 true ranges are averaged to seed the value, and later values are smoothed from there. Using high minus low alone misses gaps. Smoothing before the first average has no starting value. Dividing by the close gives a percentage form, not ATR itself.
  45. The correct answer is D (It is a dragonfly doji, which after a decline hints at a bottom, not a top.): A doji has an open and close at nearly the same price, and the position of the shadow distinguishes the types. A long lower shadow with the open and close at the high is a dragonfly doji, and after a decline it suggests buyers rejected lower prices. The gravestone doji is its mirror image with a long upper shadow, and a spinning top has a small but visible body.
  46. The correct answer is C (Up candle with a 1.10 real body, a 0.60 upper shadow, and a 0.70 lower shadow.): The real body runs from the open to the close: 49.30 minus 48.20 equals 1.10, and the close is above the open, so it is an up candle. The upper shadow runs from the top of the body to the high: 49.90 minus 49.30 equals 0.60. The lower shadow runs from the bottom of the body to the low: 48.20 minus 47.50 equals 0.70. The 2.40 figure is the whole range, not the body.
  47. The correct answer is A (Its weight falls in the price-weighted index because its price halves; the cap-weighted weight is unchanged.): A price-weighted index weights each stock by its price, so halving the price from 120 to 60 cuts its weight, even though the index level is kept steady by a divisor adjustment. A cap-weighted index uses price times shares, and the split doubles shares while halving price, so market value and weight are unchanged. A split does not change the company's value, which rules out a fall in both indexes or a rise in both.
  48. The correct answer is C (Anchoring and adjustment, because the purchase price controls her target despite new information): Anchoring is relying too heavily on one number, usually the first one received, and adjusting too little from it. Her purchase price of 80 has no bearing on where the stock goes next, yet it sets her exit target. The disposition effect, holding losers, does explain why she does not sell now, but it does not explain the specific figure of 80. Availability and hindsight bias are not suggested by her statements.
  49. The correct answer is B (Logarithmic scales suit long periods or large ranges, because equal percentage moves take equal distances.): On a log axis equal percentage changes occupy equal vertical distance, so it is preferred for long periods or large price ranges such as 100 to 1,600. Unequal spacing for equal percentages describes an arithmetic axis. An arithmetic scale distorts long ranges because later dollar moves look bigger, and log scales matter little over short spans with narrow ranges.
  50. The correct answer is D (Specify a return model, measure abnormal returns, test significance, then weigh inefficiency against a flawed model.): Any test of efficiency is also a test of the asset pricing model used to define normal returns, so the model must be chosen first. Abnormal return is actual return minus the model's expected return, and only then can its significance be tested. A significant result means either the market is inefficient or the model is wrong, and the researcher cannot separate the two. Raw benchmark comparison ignores risk, and picking the model after seeing results is data mining.
  51. The correct answer is B (Participation is weak, so treat the breakout as unconfirmed until a follow-through or retest holds.): A breakout carries more weight when volume expands, because heavy participation shows conviction. With volume well below the weeks before, participation is weak, so the breakout is unconfirmed until follow through or a retest that holds. Light volume does not prove a trap, so an immediate short goes too far, and waving the reading away ignores a standard confirmation tool.
  52. The correct answer is A (Loss aversion is emotional and rooted in feeling, so information alone seldom removes it): Emotional biases arise from feelings and impulses rather than faulty reasoning or poor information, which makes them harder to correct with education. Better results usually come from adapting the process, for example with rules set in advance. Cognitive errors are the ones that respond best to more or better information, so the stated cure targets the wrong type of bias.
  53. The correct answer is D (Candlesticks trace to Japanese rice trading and reached Western traders widely in the early 1990s): Japanese rice trading, especially at the Dojima market in Osaka, is the origin of the methods later turned into the candlestick chart, and the trader most linked with it is Munehisa Homma. Candlesticks stayed largely unknown outside Japan until Steve Nison wrote about them for Western traders in the early 1990s. That does not show Western bar charts began there. Dow and Hamilton worked with stock averages, not rice or candlesticks.
  54. The correct answer is D (The pattern would carry the same bullish meaning if it appeared after a nine-week advance.): A bullish engulfing pattern is a bottom reversal and needs a decline before it. After an advance, the same shape has no bullish meaning. The other statements are correct: the larger body shows buyers overpowering sellers, a higher close next session adds confidence, and the second body must surround the first.
  55. The correct answer is C (Observe behavior, classify the bias as emotional or cognitive, then adapt to emotional and moderate cognitive.): Cognitive errors come from faulty reasoning and respond to information and education, so they can be moderated. Emotional biases are rooted in feelings and are hard to change, so the advisor adapts to them. This requires first classifying which kind each bias is, and the options that reverse the treatments or skip classification would apply the wrong remedy.
  56. The correct answer is D (Test the vendor's growth assumption and adjust or support it before publishing): Standard V(A) requires a reasonable and adequate basis supported by appropriate research, and a vendor's number she cannot support does not meet it. Attributing the figure, averaging two unsupported views, or accepting management guidance without testing it all skip the independent work. She should examine the assumption and either justify an adjusted forecast or delay the rating.
  57. The correct answer is A (Skew means call options carry higher implied volatility than puts at equal distances from the price.): For equity indexes skew usually means out of the money puts carry higher implied volatility than calls, so the statement that calls carry higher implied volatility is the false one. The others are true: implied volatility is forward looking and comes from option prices, volatility tends to mean revert after extreme readings, and different expirations carry different implied volatilities, which forms a term structure.
  58. The correct answer is A (Volume counts all trades, not new positions, and falling open interest shows positions were being closed): Volume counts every contract traded, whether it opens, closes, or transfers a position. A fall in open interest from 300,000 to 270,000 means net closing of 30,000 positions, so the claim of 90,000 new bullish positions fails. Reading the drop as new openings reverses its sign, and short covering shows closing, not fresh bullish commitment.
  59. The correct answer is D (Hamilton succeeded Dow as editor; Rhea wrote the 1932 book that organized the tenets.): William Peter Hamilton succeeded Dow as editor and applied the ideas in his editorials, and Robert Rhea organized them in his 1932 book The Dow Theory. S. A. Nelson is credited with first using the phrase Dow's Theory, and E. George Schaefer refined it later. Dow himself died thirty years before the 1932 book appeared.
  60. The correct answer is A (Report the geometric mean of 0 percent, because each pair of months multiplies to 1.0.): For returns that compound, the geometric mean is the correct average. Multiplying 1.25 by 0.80 gives 1.00, so every two-month pair is flat and the geometric mean is 0 percent. The arithmetic mean and median both give 2.5 percent but overstate growth, because the account never gained. Averaging the growth factors is the arithmetic mean in disguise, not the geometric mean.
  61. The correct answer is C (A good ratio gives no edge alone, and buying against the trend lowers the odds.): A reward to risk ratio describes payoff size, not the probability of success, so a 2.5 to 1 ratio gives no edge on its own. Buying a bounce in a downtrend goes against the prevailing direction and lowers the odds of reaching the target. Stop placement matters but is not the main flaw, and the ratio does not predict win rate.
  62. The correct answer is C (Eighteenth century Osaka rice traders, commonly credited to Munehisa Homma, tracked prices and emotion.): The usual account places candlestick origins in eighteenth century Japanese rice trading at Osaka, where traders linked price movement to market psychology, with Munehisa Homma commonly credited. Nison introduced the format to the West much later. A Western bar chart origin, a Western name as inventor, or a different commodity and motive each changes a key fact of that history.
  63. The correct answer is D (Do not rely on the slope, since the index explains little, and seek a better hedge): An r squared of 0.04 means the index explains only about four percent of the ETF's daily variation, so a hedge built on the slope would leave most of the risk unprotected. The slope may even be significant yet still useless for hedging. A low r squared does not by itself bias the slope, and halving the hedge does not fix the missing explanatory power. A better hedge instrument is the sound answer.
  64. The correct answer is C (Homma's rice trading, Dow's averages, Rhea's Dow Theory book, then Nison's candlestick book.): The Japanese rice traders, including Homma, charted prices in the 1700s. Dow published his stock averages in the late 1800s, Rhea organized his ideas in a 1932 book, and Nison introduced candlestick charting to Western analysts in 1991. Any order that places the Japanese traders after the American events confuses when candlestick methods were invented with when the West learned them.
  65. The correct answer is A (Investors update beliefs correctly and choose the option with the highest expected utility): Traditional finance assumes rational agents who process information correctly and maximize expected utility. Holding losers to avoid realizing them, anchoring on an initial value, and splitting money into mental accounts are findings of behavioral finance, each of which breaks the rational agent picture. They tempt a student who knows these ideas concern decisions but not which school claims them.
  66. The correct answer is B (Skewed right: a long tail of large gains pulls the mean above the median.): A right-skewed distribution has its tail stretched toward large positive values, and those values pull the mean above the median. Skew describes asymmetry, while kurtosis describes the weight of the tails, so a symmetric fat-tailed curve is a different shape. The Empirical Rule fits a normal curve, which this asymmetric chart is not.
  67. The correct answer is D (Upward momentum is slowing within the uptrend, so the cross is a caution, not a reversal.): A MACD cross below the signal line shows the gap between the 12 and 26-period averages is shrinking, which signals slowing momentum. While MACD stays above zero, the 12-period average is still above the 26-period average, so the trend is positive. The zero line cross, not the signal cross, marks the averages changing order.
  68. The correct answer is D (The first is the availability bias and the second is anchoring to the purchase price.): Availability means judging likelihood by how easily examples come to mind, and vivid crash coverage makes crashes seem frequent. The second investor treats his cost of 55 as a fixed reference and will not accept a price below it, which is anchoring. Reversing the labels is the common confusion between the two.
  69. The correct answer is C (The disposition effect, which is selling winners too soon while holding losers too long.): The disposition effect describes the tendency to realize gains early and postpone realizing losses, driven by loss aversion and the wish to avoid regret. Anchoring on the purchase price may play a part, but the full pattern of selling the winner while keeping the loser is the disposition effect. Herding and hindsight bias describe different behaviors.
  70. The correct answer is B (Test a larger sample in a separate period, since 12 trades cannot support a conclusion.): A sample of 12 trades is too small for the result to tell luck from skill, and the profits may come from one or two market episodes. The sound step is to test over more trades and on data not used to design the rule. Dropping losers and tuning the lengths both inflate the result.
  71. The correct answer is C (13 divided by 55 gives 0.236, and 21 divided by 55 gives 0.382): A term divided by the term three places later approaches 0.236 and by the term two places later approaches 0.382: 13 / 55 = 0.236 and 21 / 55 = 0.382. The pairing 21 / 55 and 34 / 55 gives 0.382 and 0.618, 13 / 34 and 21 / 34 give 0.382 and 0.618, and the inverted divisions give about 1.619 and 1.615.
  72. The correct answer is D (The 3,940 low and the lower high at 4,120 put the uptrend in doubt.): An uptrend requires each swing high and each swing low to exceed the previous one. The high rose from 4,100 to 4,160, but the next low at 3,940 fell below the earlier low at 3,950, and the latest peak at 4,120 is below 4,160. Both conditions have failed, so the uptrend is in doubt. A single lower low and lower high do not yet confirm a primary downtrend. The peaks all exceeding 4,100 does not rescue the pattern, since the comparison must be with the previous swing.
  73. The correct answer is B (Conservatism bias, under-weighting new information and updating too slowly): The key detail is that she reads everything and still revises almost nothing, which is conservatism: the original view stays dominant and new data is under-weighted. Confirmation bias would show up as selective searching, which the stem rules out. Anchoring and adjustment describes a numeric reference and belongs to information processing biases. Hindsight bias concerns claiming a past outcome was predictable.
  74. The correct answer is D (Implied volatility, the expected future volatility backed out of current option prices using a pricing model.): When option prices are fed into a pricing model and the volatility input is solved for, the result is the market's expected future volatility, called implied volatility. The 1 percent actual move is historical volatility, which looks backward. Skew describes how implied volatility changes across strikes and does not name the single figure. Average true range is built from price bars and uses no option prices.
  75. The correct answer is A (It is defined over gains and losses from a reference point, steeper for losses): The value function measures changes from a reference point and is steeper on the loss side, which is loss aversion. The final-wealth concave curve belongs to expected utility. An S-shape with equal slopes loses the asymmetry, and the convex-for-gains description reverses the real shape, which is concave for gains and convex for losses.
  76. The correct answer is C (Upper band 90.00 and lower 80.00; the close is 1.30 above the upper band.): The offset is 2 times 2.50, or 5.00, giving an upper band of 90.00 and a lower band of 80.00. A close at 91.30 is 1.30 above the upper band. Using one deviation gives 87.50 and 82.50, three deviations gives 92.50 and 77.50, and doubling the offset again gives 95.00 and 75.00.
  77. The correct answer is C (It shows subjectivity, and a close below the prior reaction low in both averages settles it): Two analysts classifying the same decline differently is the standard subjectivity criticism, and the theory's own rule resolves it: a close below the prior reaction low in both averages. Acting early gives up the confirmation that defines the theory. Volume is a secondary check, and Dow Theory relies on closing prices, not intraday lows.
  78. The correct answer is D (Set the stop level, find risk per share, then divide the 500 dollar budget by it): Position sizing starts with the stop, because the distance from entry to stop is the risk on each share. Dividing the 500 dollar risk budget, which is 1 percent of the account, by that risk per share gives the number of shares. Dividing the whole account by risk per share ignores the budget. Multiplying produces a meaningless figure. Dividing by the entry price sizes the position by cost, not by what is lost at the stop.
  79. The correct answer is C (The stock is outperforming the benchmark, even though the broader market is falling): The ratio line divides the stock by the benchmark, so a rising line means the stock is doing better than the index. With the index falling, the stock can outperform even if it only holds steady or falls less. Relative outperformance does not confirm an absolute uptrend, and a ratio of two series says nothing direct about where the benchmark itself is heading.
  80. The correct answer is B (William Peter Hamilton, who carried Dow's editorials forward as a coherent forecasting method): Hamilton took over as editor after Dow's death in 1902 and spent over two decades applying the averages to market forecasting, culminating in The Stock Market Barometer. Dow originated the ideas but did not write that book. Rhea later organized Hamilton's work into a formal statement in 1932, and Nelson was an early popularizer, so each fits a nearby role but not the one described.
  81. The correct answer is B (Liquidity is adequate, but a defended band limits free price discovery, so patterns are less reliable): Charting works best when prices are set freely by participants. Heavy trading satisfies liquidity, but a defended band restricts price discovery, so patterns inside it reflect policy as well as supply and demand. A band's reach is not limited to its edges, thousands of trades still mean many participants, and extra liquidity from defense does not restore free pricing.
  82. The correct answer is D (The ratio rises from 0.0200 to 0.0250, so the stock outperformed the index.): The ratio line is the asset divided by the benchmark. Start: 25 divided by 1,250 equals 0.0200. End: 30 divided by 1,200 equals 0.0250. A rising ratio means outperformance, which fits a 20 percent stock gain against a 4 percent index decline, a gap of 24 points. Dividing the index by the stock reverses the picture, and subtracting 4 instead of adding the decline gives 16, which is not the relative move.
  83. The correct answer is A (The house money effect, risking recent gains as if they were not his): Treating recent winnings as money that belongs to the market makes investors more willing to take risk, which is the house money effect. It comes from mental accounting, since the gain sits in a different mental account. The disposition effect concerns selling winners and keeping losers, and he sold nothing. Loss aversion would make him cautious, and the stem gives no sign of overconfidence about skill.
  84. The correct answer is B (Move one column right and plot Os from 51 down to 49): A three-box reversal from a top at 52 needs price to trade down to the 49 box. The low of 49.00 reaches that box's value, so the reversal is confirmed without waiting for a lower price. The new O column begins one box below the top X, at 51, and fills 51, 50 and 49. Starting at 52 overlaps the X column, and a lone O leaves the skipped boxes unrecorded. The bounce to 51 changes nothing recorded.
  85. The correct answer is A (Audited quarterly earnings reports must exist for the instrument before its chart can be used): Technical analysis works from price and volume, so it needs a liquid, freely priced market with many participants and trustworthy data. It does not require audited earnings reports, which is why currencies and commodities can be charted with no earnings at all. Liquidity, broad participation and reliable records are genuine requirements that may tempt a student to call them unnecessary.
  86. The correct answer is B (Profit is 1,000 US dollars, and the rise means the euro gained value against the dollar): In EUR/USD the euro is the base currency and the dollar is the quote currency. A rising quote means one euro buys more dollars, so the euro strengthened. The move of 0.0100 on 100,000 euros is 1,000 dollars, expressed in the quote currency. Reporting the profit in euros or reading the rise as dollar strength mixes up base and quote.
  87. The correct answer is B (Conservatism for the small revision, then hindsight bias for the claim of foresight): Revising from 3.00 to only 2.90 when the news justifies about 2.40 is a failure to update a belief enough, which is conservatism, a belief perseverance bias. Saying afterward that she knew it all along is hindsight bias. Anchoring is close to the first behavior but belongs to the information processing family. Illusion of control concerns believing one can influence outcomes, and representativeness concerns judging by resemblance to a category.
  88. The correct answer is C (It is a testable hypothesis, and next comes gathering price data to test against a benchmark.): A hypothesis is a specific claim that can be shown wrong, stated before looking at results. This statement names the group, the horizon, and the comparison, so it qualifies. The test and analysis come after it: collect data, run the test, and compare with a benchmark. Calling it a conclusion or backtest result skips the testing, and reshaping the claim to fit data invites data mining.
  89. The correct answer is A (A trend is presumed to continue until a definite reversal signal appears in the averages): The deciding detail is that neither average broke its last reaction low, so no definite reversal signal exists, and the trend is presumed to continue. Discounting is tempting because of the headline, but it describes how news enters prices, not why to hold. Confirmation and volume are real tenets, but neither is the reason to remain long here.
  90. The correct answer is B (Accumulation has ended and markup has begun, with heavy breakout volume confirming demand.): The eight-week range after a decline, with stronger volume on rallies than on dips, is the pattern of accumulation, where informed buyers absorb supply. A breakout above the range on heavy volume marks the start of the markup phase. Distribution occurs after an advance, not after a decline, and heavy breakout volume supports the move rather than refuting it.
  91. The correct answer is A (Bigger bets after a drawdown raise the risk of ruin if losses continue.): After eight 1% losses equity is about 0.99 to the eighth power, or 92.3% of the start, and a drawdown needs a larger percentage gain to recover. Doubling size to 2% raises the chance that a continued streak damages the account beyond repair. Past losses do not make the next win more likely, and size follows risk per trade rather than the profit target.
  92. The correct answer is C (He treats a paper loss as unreal until sold, which is the disposition effect, not discipline): Selling winners early and holding losers too long is the disposition effect, and it grows out of loss aversion and the wish to avoid realizing a loss. The 22 percent decline has already reduced his wealth whether or not he sells. Waiting for a return to even is a price based on his purchase cost rather than on the stock's prospects. The house money effect needs added risk taking with gains, and overconfidence does not explain the sell winner, hold loser pattern.
  93. The correct answer is D (The Fractal Markets Hypothesis says markets stay stable when all investors share the same short horizon): The Fractal Markets Hypothesis says markets are stable when investors with many different time horizons trade, because each group provides liquidity for the others. A crash occurs when everyone shifts to the same short horizon, so the claim is reversed. The other three claims are accurate descriptions of the Adaptive Markets Hypothesis, the January effect, and the defenders' risk explanation.
  94. The correct answer is B (A range bar chart, where each bar completes after price travels the set distance): A range bar completes when price travels the chosen distance, so its construction ignores elapsed time. Renko bricks also use a price move, not minutes. A tick chart is triggered by the number of trades, and a weekly bar is completed by the calendar. Only the range bar matches a fixed distance trigger described in the stem.
  95. The correct answer is A (A line chart displays the open, high and low of each period along with the close.): A line chart commonly joins closing prices, so it does not display the open, high, or low. The false statement credits it with information it hides. The other three are accurate: the OHLC bar spans the period range with a vertical line, candlestick body color reflects the close versus the open, and the line chart is built from closes.
  96. The correct answer is B (A peak after the midpoint is right translation, which implies a bearish larger trend.): The midpoint of a 40-week cycle is week 20, so a week 28 peak is right translation, which indicates a bullish bias, not a bearish one. Hurst's summation, harmonicity and synchronicity principles describe how cycles combine, relate by factors of two and share lows, so those three statements are sound. Only the bearish reading of right translation is wrong.
  97. The correct answer is D (Price reflects supply, demand and trader behavior, which charts record whatever the asset's underlying cash flows.): Technical analysis rests on the idea that price records supply, demand and trader behavior, whatever the asset's cash flows are. That logic applies to digital assets too. Liquidity does not make prices match fundamentals. Lacking earnings explains why other methods are harder, not why charts work. On chain data can supplement analysis but does not replace price and volume records.
  98. The correct answer is C (A value function concave for gains and convex for losses, measured from a reference point.): Prospect theory's value function is concave in the gain domain, which produces risk aversion, and convex in the loss domain, which produces risk seeking. Probability weighting is also part of the theory, but it drives how small chances are treated, not the flip in preference between gains and losses. A linear function would give no switch at all.
  99. The correct answer is D (Test the chosen parameters on data not used in the selection, such as a later period): Picking the best of 400 combinations on one data set invites overfitting, where the winner reflects noise. The sound check is out-of-sample testing on data the selection never saw. More searching or more indicators on the same history only increases the fit, and restricting the test to surviving stocks adds survivorship bias.
  100. The correct answer is A (A flag has parallel trendlines sloping against the prior move; a pennant has converging trendlines.): Both formations are short continuation patterns that follow a sharp move and are usually followed by a breakout in the prior direction. A flag is drawn with parallel trendlines that slope against the prior move, like a small channel, whereas a pennant has converging lines that form a tiny triangle. Both normally show declining volume, so volume does not separate them.
  101. The correct answer is C (The first trader shows hindsight bias, and the second trader shows confirmation bias.): Hindsight bias is believing after the fact that an outcome was predictable. Confirmation bias is seeking and favoring information that supports an existing belief. The swapped pairing reverses the definitions, and the illusion of control, representativeness and conservatism do not describe either remark.
  102. The correct answer is B (It is not a reversal; the trend is assumed to continue while the higher lows hold.): Dow assumed a trend stays in force until clear evidence shows it has reversed, and in an uptrend that evidence is a failure to keep making higher highs and higher lows, with confirmation. A dip that holds above the prior low is consistent with the trend, so abandoning it early is the mistake the tenet warns against.
  103. The correct answer is A (A rising relative strength ratio line means the stock's price is also rising in absolute terms.): A rising ratio line shows the stock is outperforming the benchmark, not that its price is rising. If the benchmark falls faster, the stock can drop in price and still lift the ratio. The other statements are true: relative strength often turns ahead of price, a stock can lead a lagging sector, and technicians commonly smooth the ratio line with a moving average.
  104. The correct answer is B (A bearish signal-line crossover, showing momentum has weakened relative to its own recent average.): MACD is the gap between a fast and a slow exponential average, and the signal line is a shorter average of MACD. When MACD crosses below its signal line, momentum has weakened relative to its recent average, which is a bearish signal-line crossover. A centerline crossover involves MACD crossing zero, a divergence compares MACD swings with price swings, and an overbought reading is not defined by a signal-line cross.
  105. The correct answer is C (Multiply growth factors 1.50 and 0.50, take the square root, subtract 1, about minus 13.4 percent): Compounded returns call for the geometric mean. The product of 1.50 and 0.50 is 0.75, the square root of 0.75 is about 0.866, and subtracting 1 gives about minus 13.4 percent, matching the loss in the account. The arithmetic average of 0 ignores compounding, and dividing or adding the factors gives unrelated results.
  106. The correct answer is B (Confirm the 54 box traded, move right one column, then plot Os from 58 to 54): With a two dollar box, three boxes down from 60 are 58, 56 and 54. The price of 54.50 sits in the 54 box, so the reversal is confirmed on the trade itself. The chartist then moves one column right and starts one box below the top X, at 58, filling down to 54. Recording inside the X column or starting at 60 breaks the column rules, and a close is not required.
  107. The correct answer is C (Keep the position but monitor it closely; the averages confirm and volume is an early caution.): Both averages confirmed, so the primary trend is presumed to continue until a definite reversal appears. Volume should expand with the trend, so weak volume on rallies is a caution, not a reversal signal. Holding with close monitoring respects both the confirmation tenet and the volume warning.
  108. The correct answer is D (The Adaptive Markets Hypothesis, which treats efficiency as changing over time.): Andrew Lo's Adaptive Markets Hypothesis applies evolutionary ideas: participants adapt, so efficiency varies with the environment. The Fractal Markets Hypothesis instead emphasizes liquidity from many investment horizons, and it breaks down when one horizon dominates. Behavioral finance is a broad field built on psychology. The random walk describes how prices move and does not address how efficiency evolves.
  109. The correct answer is B (A doji shows indecision and needs next-candle confirmation; alone it does not prove a reversal.): A doji shows buyers and sellers finishing near the same price, which signals indecision. After a rally it warns of fatigue but needs a lower close on the next candle to confirm. Shorting at the open acts before confirmation. A gap requirement and a long upper shadow requirement are invented conditions, and a doji alone does not mean sellers have taken control.
  110. The correct answer is A (Multiply each share price by shares outstanding, rank the market values, then assign groups using cutoffs.): Capitalization is price times shares outstanding, so P is worth 1.5 billion and Q is worth 18 billion, even though P has the higher share price. The values are ranked and then compared with the cutoffs to assign groups. Ranking by price would wrongly place P above Q. Dividing by volume measures something unrelated to size, and sector describes the industry, not the company's market value.
  111. The correct answer is D (Hindsight bias is overweighting new evidence and dropping a prior belief too quickly after a surprise.): Hindsight bias is the belief, after the fact, that an outcome was predictable, which is why she calls the crash obvious. It does not describe dropping beliefs too quickly, so that statement is false. The other three correctly describe slow updating, an inflated sense of influence and the general habit of keeping a thesis, so each tempts a reader hunting for the error.
  112. The correct answer is D (The multiple applies to each day's return and resets daily, so compounding breaks the annual multiple.): Leveraged funds target the multiple on each day's return and reset daily, so over a year compounding changes the result. In a volatile year a 10 percent index gain can leave a 3x fund far from 30 percent, sometimes below it. Fees and financing costs play a role but do not explain the gap. The reset period is daily, not monthly, and volatility drag affects long and inverse leveraged funds alike.
  113. The correct answer is B (Subjectivity, since the rules for lines and confirmation leave room for different interpretations.): Dow Theory was never written as a precise rule set, so confirmation and the identification of lines depend on judgment and two readers can reach different conclusions. That is the subjectivity criticism. Lag is a separate criticism about the delay in signals, and data mining and survivorship bias concern testing methods.
  114. The correct answer is D (Dow treated the intraday high as the key price because it showed peak buying strength.): Dow treated the closing price as the key price of the day, so the intraday high claim is the false one. The accumulation description is accurate and tempts a reader who expects bad news to mean no buying.
