Confirmation does not require identical dates, and the closing price statement is simply Dow's own rule, which a hasty reader may flip.
  115. The correct answer is B (Yes, the 3.4% range fits a line, and a close above 39,900 suggests an advance.): The range is 39,900 minus 38,600, or 1,300 points, which is 1,300 divided by 38,600, about 3.4%, within the 5% guideline. A breakout above the top suggests accumulation, but Dow required the other average to confirm. Treating 1,300 as 13% is a decimal slip, and breakouts from a line are the very signal the pattern gives.
  116. The correct answer is D (Adjacent term ratios oscillate around and converge toward 1.618, so one early ratio approximates it.): Each term is the sum of the two before it, and the ratio of a term to the previous one swings above and below 1.618 while getting closer as the terms grow. 13 over 8 is merely an early step. Dividing a term by the one after it gives about 0.618, and by the one two places later about 0.382.
  117. The correct answer is A (The credit spread, the extra yield investors demand for the corporate issuer's default risk.): The difference in yield between a corporate bond and a Treasury of similar maturity is the credit spread, and it pays the investor for credit and default risk. The yield curve slope compares maturities, the coupon is fixed by the bond's terms, and the current yield divides the coupon by price.
  118. The correct answer is A (Minor movement of 10 days, secondary movement of 9 weeks, primary movement of 22 months.): Dow described minor movements as lasting less than about three weeks, secondary movements as lasting three weeks to three months, and primary movements as lasting a year or longer. Ten days is minor, nine weeks is secondary, and 22 months is primary. The wrong options swap labels, or fail to give the longest swing the primary label, which makes the largest movement a correction.
  119. The correct answer is C (Treat it as confirmation of an established trend, accepting a later entry for fewer false signals.): Dow Theory is slow and requires judgment, so signals arrive after much of the move. Its strength is confirming that a primary trend is established, reducing false signals. It is not useless because it lags, and it does not mark precise tops or bottoms, nor does imprecision justify skipping confirmation.
  120. The correct answer is A (Buyers hold the edge, but the low ADX shows weak trend strength, so await confirmation): The +DI above -DI shows upward directional pressure, but ADX measures trend strength regardless of direction, and a reading of 17 that is falling shows a weak trend. A falling ADX does not mean sellers are winning. The DI spread still carries information, so the best step is to wait for ADX to rise.
  121. The correct answer is A (Dividing by the next higher number approaches 0.618, and by two places higher approaches 0.382): Take 34 divided by 55, about 0.618, and 21 divided by 55, about 0.382. Each step further along the sequence gives the next retracement ratio, so three places higher yields about 0.236. The student reversed the first two. Dividing the larger number by the smaller instead gives 1.618, which is the inverse, not the value requested.
  122. The correct answer is D (Anchoring and adjustment; build the estimate afresh from current comparables before consulting the old target.): Nudging an old target up a little is anchoring and adjustment, and the cure is an independent estimate from current comparables. Confirmation bias and representativeness are real biases but the stem shows neither. Conservatism sounds close because it also means slow updating, yet its suggested action of gradual steps repeats the very habit being corrected.
  123. The correct answer is D (Conservatism: she moved 0.10 of the 1.00 gap, which is 10 percent of the evidence): The gap between her prior view of 3.00 and the new evidence of 4.00 is 1.00. She moved 0.10, which is 10 percent of the way, so she under-reacted. Under-reacting to new information is conservatism. The 0.90 figure is the part left unmoved, the 3.3 percent rise (0.10 over 3.00) is small, and hindsight bias concerns memory for past predictions, not revisions.
  124. The correct answer is B (Professionalism, Integrity of Capital Markets, Duties to Clients, Duties to Employers): The Standards run Professionalism first, then Integrity of Capital Markets, Duties to Clients, Duties to Employers, Investment Analysis, Recommendations and Actions, Conflicts of Interest, and Responsibilities as a Member or Candidate. Each wrong sequence changes one or two positions among the first four, a common memory slip. The order is simply how the Standards are published.
  125. The correct answer is A (One is a dragonfly doji, two a gravestone doji; the gravestone is the stronger top warning): A dragonfly doji has a long lower shadow with the open, high and close together, and a gravestone doji has a long upper shadow with the open, low and close together. After a rally, the gravestone shows buyers pushing up and then losing everything, so it is the stronger warning of a top. A long-legged doji has long shadows on both sides, and a hammer or shooting star needs a visible small body rather than an open nearly equal to the close.
  126. The correct answer is B (Regret aversion, where choices are avoided because of the pain of possibly regretting the outcome.): Her stated reason is avoiding the regret of another poor decision, which defines regret aversion. Loss aversion and status quo bias are both defensible, since she stays in cash, but neither centers on the fear of self-blame. Hindsight bias concerns believing past events were predictable and does not explain why she avoids acting now.
  127. The correct answer is C (New bonds pay more, buyers demand a discount, so its price falls and its yield rises.): Prices and yields on bonds move in opposite directions. When comparable new bonds offer 5 percent, an existing 4 percent bond must be priced at a discount to attract buyers, and that lower price lifts its yield to maturity toward the market level. The coupon is fixed in dollars and never changes with price. Paying a premium would push the yield further below the market, which makes the bond even less attractive.
  128. The correct answer is C (The Industrials' high is unconfirmed because the Transports did not match it, so wait): Dow required both averages to confirm each other, and here only one made a new high, so the advance is unconfirmed. Non-confirmation is a warning, but the theory holds that a trend persists until a definite reversal signal appears, and none has. Volume should expand with the trend, so a lighter breakout adds doubt rather than confirmation. The discounting tenet says prices reflect all known information, which does not replace confirmation.
  129. The correct answer is D (She may share client information with a regulator only after the client has given written consent): The Standard allows disclosure when required by law and when the information concerns illegal activity, and it allows sharing with colleagues who need it to serve the client. It does not make written client consent a condition for those disclosures, so the consent statement is the incorrect one. The other choices are accurate even though confidentiality seems to forbid them.
  130. The correct answer is A (A close 3% beyond the broken level is needed, so a 2% close falls short.): The 3% rule is a breakout filter: price should close a set percentage beyond the trendline or level to reduce false signals. A 2% close falls short of the threshold, so the confirmation claim is premature. The rule works in both directions, uses closing prices, and measures from the broken level.
  131. The correct answer is C (Between minus 2.9 percent and plus 3.1 percent): About 95 percent of values fall within two standard deviations of the mean. Two times 1.5 is 3.0, so the range is 0.1 minus 3.0 equals minus 2.9 to 0.1 plus 3.0 equals 3.1. One deviation covers about 68 percent and three cover about 99.7 percent, and a symmetric interval around zero skips the mean.
  132. The correct answer is D (Bollinger Bands use standard deviation of price for width, while Keltner Channels use average true range.): Bollinger Bands place the outer bands a set number of standard deviations from a moving average, so their width reflects how widely closes have varied. Keltner Channels typically center on an exponential average and place the outer lines at a multiple of average true range. The two measures are not interchangeable, neither uses volume for the width, and Bollinger Bands are not fixed percentage envelopes.
  133. The correct answer is A (Buyers recovered an early dip and closed near the high, 3 points above the prior close.): On an OHLC bar the left tick is the open and the right tick is the close, so the stock opened at 52 and closed at 57, a 5-point gain from open to close. The low was 51 and the high 58, so the 57 close sits near the top of the 7-point range, and it is 3 points above the prior close of 54. The lower open shows an early dip, but it was recovered. A body that fills most of the range signals conviction rather than indecision, and the ticks are not reversed.
  134. The correct answer is D (Accept the bias as part of the client's makeup and adapt the recommendations around it.): Emotional biases stem from feelings rather than faulty reasoning, so education seldom removes them, and advisors usually adapt the plan to them, perhaps trimming the position gradually. Treating it as a cognitive error assumes information will fix it. Overriding the client risks losing trust, and waiting for a gain makes the client's attachment worse.
  135. The correct answer is B (The prior close lets the measure capture gaps between sessions, so overnight jumps are not missed.): The high minus low for this session is only 0.80, which would hide the 2.40 overnight jump from the prior close of 50.00. Wilder added the distances from the prior close to the high and the low so that gaps count, giving a True Range of 2.90 here. Smoothing happens later in the Average True Range, not in True Range. True Range uses no volume input, and it stays in price units rather than percentages.
  136. The correct answer is A (After an advance this shape is a hanging man, a topping warning needing bearish confirmation): The shape of a small body at the top with a long lower shadow is shared by the hammer and the hanging man, and the prior trend decides which it is. After seven weeks of advance it is a hanging man, a possible top that needs a lower next close to confirm. Calling it a hammer ignores the context. A shooting star has the long shadow on top, and a doji needs open and close nearly equal, which a visible body rules out.
  137. The correct answer is A (The 200-day average is the filter, the 10/40 crossover is the trigger, and sizing is separate.): A trigger rule is the event that calls for action, which here is the crossover. A filter decides whether the trigger is allowed to act, which is the job of the rising 200-day condition. Value rules compare price with a measure of worth and usually lean toward mean reversion, so neither average rule qualifies. Swapping trigger and filter is tempting because the long average looks more important, but it only screens the signal and creates no trade by itself.
  138. The correct answer is B (Rhea organized the tenets in 1932, and the second average is the Rail average): Dow died in 1902, so the 1932 book The Dow Theory was written by Robert Rhea, who organized the tenets into a clear list. Dow's two original averages were the Industrial Average and the Rail average, now called the Transportation Average. Hamilton wrote an earlier 1922 book, not the 1932 one. The Composite is a later blend of other averages, and the Utilities average was not part of Dow's original pair.
  139. The correct answer is D (Rhea wrote the 1932 book, Hamilton the 1922 book, and Nelson coined the phrase): Robert Rhea wrote The Dow Theory in 1932 and set out the tenets in a clear list. William Peter Hamilton, Dow's successor as editor of the Journal, wrote The Stock Market Barometer in 1922. S. A. Nelson wrote The ABC of Stock Speculation in 1903 and is credited with first using the phrase. Schaefer is linked to the mid-century revival of interest, and Dow died in 1902, so he could not have written the 1922 book.
  140. The correct answer is C (A secondary reaction inside the primary uptrend, so keep the bullish view until a definite reversal): Dow described secondary reactions as lasting from about three weeks to three months and retracing roughly one third to two thirds of the primary move. Five weeks and 38 percent fit that description exactly. Calling it a ripple ignores the five weeks, since minor movements last under three weeks. A retracement past one third is normal rather than a reversal, and a primary trend lasts a year or more, so three weeks says nothing about a new primary trend.
  141. The correct answer is A (Positive 0.6R per trade, an edge even though the system loses more often than it wins.): Expectancy equals win rate times average win minus loss rate times average loss, so 0.40 times 3R is 1.2R and 0.60 times 1R is 0.6R, leaving 0.6R per trade. A positive result means the system has an edge even with a majority of losing trades. Counting only frequency gives negative 0.2R, counting only the winner side gives 1.2R, and subtracting averages without weights gives 2.0R, all of which skip part of the formula.
  142. The correct answer is C (Theta, the steady erosion of time value as expiration approaches.): With the stock flat, price sensitivity is not the cause, so delta and gamma do not explain the decline. Vega could matter only if implied volatility had fallen, which the stem does not say. The steady loss for an option buyer as expiration nears is time decay, measured by theta.
  143. The correct answer is B (Use split-adjusted history so pre-split prices are divided by three, keeping the series continuous.): A 3 for 1 split triples the share count and divides the price by three, so the unadjusted series shows a false drop. Dividing earlier prices by three removes the break and keeps the history continuous. Multiplying them by three widens the gap. Dividend adjustments address cash payouts, not share count changes. Skipping one bar leaves every earlier price on the old scale, so the average and trendline remain distorted.

Practice Test 5 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Open interest ends at 320,000: 30,000 opened less 10,000 liquidated, mixed trades adding nothing.): Contracts where both sides open add 30,000 to open interest, and contracts where both sides close remove 10,000. The 5,000 mixed trades leave open interest unchanged because one side opens and the other closes. The net change is plus 20,000, giving 320,000. Ignoring liquidations gives 330,000, treating all volume as new gives 345,000, and double-counting the closes gives 310,000.
  2. The correct answer is D (22.5 percent, the fall from the 80,000 dollar peak to the 62,000 dollar trough.): Maximum drawdown is the largest peak-to-trough decline, measured from the peak: 80,000 minus 62,000 is 18,000, and 18,000 divided by 80,000 is 22.5 percent. The current drawdown is only 12.5 percent, dividing by the trough overstates the decline, and the recovery is not a drawdown.
  3. The correct answer is D (A short dip holding the prior low is minor, and a reversal needs a lower low.): An uptrend stays intact until price makes a lower low and a lower high, so a short dip that holds the prior low does not reverse it. Volume is a confirming tool rather than a trend definition, and trend structure applies to weekly charts. A touch of a prior low is a test of support, not a violation that confirms a reversal.
  4. The correct answer is C (A head and shoulders top target is the head's height added above the neckline): A head and shoulders top is bearish, so its measured target is projected downward from the neckline by the head's height, not added above it. The remaining statements are standard teaching: volume tends to be lighter on the right shoulder, a double top target is the peak to valley height measured from the valley, and a neckline close confirms the head and shoulders top.
  5. The correct answer is D (428,000, because new positions add 30,000 and closing positions remove 12,000): The 30,000 new-buyer and new-seller contracts add 30,000, the 12,000 mutual closings subtract 12,000, and the 10,000 transfer from an existing long to a new buyer changes nothing. The net is plus 18,000, so open interest rises from 410,000 to 428,000.
  6. The correct answer is D (When most participants are already bullish and invested, few new buyers remain to push prices higher.): Contrary opinion argues that when nearly everyone is bullish and already invested, few new buyers remain to push prices higher, leaving the market vulnerable. It is a warning about conditions, not a timing tool with a fixed holding period. Advisers do not sell by buying, and a bullish extreme warns about the bulls, not the bears.
  7. The correct answer is B (61.8 percent, since 100 less 30.9 is 69.1, a deep pullback testing the uptrend): The advance covers 50 points. Multiplying by 0.618 gives 30.9, and 100 minus 30.9 equals 69.1, which is almost exactly where the pullback stopped. A 61.8 percent retracement is deep and puts the trend under test, since moves past it suggest weakening. The lines near 80.9, 75 and 60.7 correspond to 38.2, 50 and 78.6 percent, and the pullback low sits far from each of them.
  8. The correct answer is A (Editing, which frames outcomes against a reference point, then evaluation of the edited prospects.): Prospect theory has an editing phase, in which outcomes are coded as gains or losses against a reference point and prospects are simplified, followed by an evaluation phase in which the edited prospects are valued with the value function and decision weights. The other orderings reverse the phases or invent steps.
  9. The correct answer is C (The joint hypothesis problem, since efficiency and the expected-return model are tested together): To call a return abnormal, the researcher must first define the normal return with a model such as the Capital Asset Pricing Model. Every test therefore examines market efficiency and the model at once, so an abnormal result could mean either is wrong. That is the joint hypothesis problem. Survivorship bias concerns missing failed securities, Fama's private information tests concern the strong form, and the arcsine law describes one-sided stretches in random series.
  10. The correct answer is D (The Industrial Average and the Railroad Average, which later became the Transportation Average): Dow published an average of industrial stocks and an average of railroad stocks, the second of which became the Transportation Average as railroads gave way to wider transport. The Utility Average was added in 1929, long after Dow died. The S&P 500 was created by another firm decades later. Dow's theory rests on the pair of industrial and rail averages confirming each other.
  11. The correct answer is D (A z-score of 2.00, found by subtracting the mean and dividing by the standard deviation.): The z-score is the observation minus the mean, divided by the standard deviation: (1.70 minus 0.10) divided by 0.80, which is 1.60 divided by 0.80, or 2.00. Dividing 1.70 by 0.80 gives 2.125 and ignores the mean. Dividing by the variance of 0.64 gives 2.5, and flipping the fraction gives 0.5. A two standard deviation move is uncommon but not extreme.
  12. The correct answer is C (Traders expect more movement than the quiet range showed, so volatility may expand.): Historical volatility looks backward at realized price changes, while implied volatility is derived from option prices and looks forward. The gap says the market expects more movement than the last six weeks delivered, and volatility cycles often move from compression to expansion. Neither measure gives direction, which rules out the bullish reading, and a gap before earnings is not an error.
  13. The correct answer is D (A right-translated cycle, with its peak in the second half, usually signals a bearish underlying trend.): Right translation, with the peak in the second half of the cycle, normally accompanies a bullish underlying trend, so calling it bearish is the false statement. The remaining statements are correct: left translation marks weakness, summation lets coincident lows deepen a decline, and the dominant cycle has the greatest influence on price in the span examined.
  14. The correct answer is A (Compare asset classes, then choose strong sectors, then strong industries, and finally pick leading stocks.): A top-down approach starts with asset allocation, such as stocks versus bonds, then moves to the strongest sector, then the strongest industries, and finally the leading stocks within them. Starting from individual stocks reverses the method and is bottom-up. Choosing a sector before the asset class puts the steps out of order. Ranking stocks before narrowing sectors and industries throws away the structure that makes the process top-down.
  15. The correct answer is D (Reporting r is descriptive, applying it to future days is inference, and an outlier skews r.): Computing r for the 40 observed days summarizes the data, which is descriptive statistics. Claiming it describes every future day generalizes beyond the sample, which is inference. The stray point can pull r noticeably because correlation is sensitive to extreme observations, even with 40 points. Saying the outlier has no effect, or that both claims are descriptive, misses either the distortion or the step from sample to future.
  16. The correct answer is D (Take the second setup, since its 2.5-to-1 reward-to-risk gives the higher expectancy at equal odds): The first setup risks 2 and targets 3, a 1.5-to-1 ratio. The second risks 2 for 5, a 2.5-to-1 ratio. With equal win probability, expectancy per unit risked is higher for the second. The stem fixes win probability as equal, so a nearer target does not change it, and both setups risk 2 per share.
  17. The correct answer is C (Use a candlestick chart, because its colored real body shows open to close at a glance.): A candlestick draws the open, high, low, and close in one symbol, and body color shows at once whether the session closed above or below the open. OHLC bars do show those four values but use ticks, not colored bodies. Heikin Ashi uses averaged values, and volume shows participation rather than whether the close beat the open.
  18. The correct answer is A (Crossovers give frequent false starts in a trading range, so the cross lacks trend evidence.): Inside a trading range the shorter and longer averages bunch together and cross often, producing false signals, and with no new high there is no trend evidence. Volume is a helpful confirmation but is not a rule that voids a cross, the upward cross is normally read as bullish, and the lack of a new high weakens the case rather than helping it.
  19. The correct answer is C (Columns appear only on a three-box reversal, so time is unscaled and columns span varying days): A point-and-figure chart has no time axis. A new column starts only when price reverses by the reversal amount, so one column can hold a single quiet day or many weeks. The volume-axis answer describes a different chart type, and the fixed-day and fixed-week answers force a calendar onto columns that do not keep one. Seven columns for 63 days simply means price changed direction six times.
  20. The correct answer is C (Accumulation, driven by knowledgeable investors buying from discouraged sellers before the improvement is widely recognized.): Dow described the first phase of a bull market as accumulation: informed investors buy when sentiment is poor, volume is light and the news is bad. Public participation comes later, when trend followers react to better earnings, and distribution occurs near the end of the advance. A secondary reaction is a correction within a primary trend rather than a phase of its own.
  21. The correct answer is A (The distribution phase, where informed holders sell into public enthusiasm near the end of the advance): Heavy volume, universal optimism, and informed selling mark the distribution phase, the last phase of a bull market. Public participation is the middle phase, when trend followers join and earnings improve, and the gains are strongest. Accumulation is the first phase with poor sentiment, and a secondary reaction is a counter-trend decline, not a phase of selling into optimism.
  22. The correct answer is A (Such a candle opens at the low and closes at the high, which shows strong buying.): A long white real body with no shadows, a white marubozu, opened at the low and closed at the high, so buyers controlled the whole session and the signal is strength. Exhaustion is not shown by a close at the high. Wide bodies show conviction rather than indecision, and a candle that opens high and closes low would be black.
  23. The correct answer is C (The shadow points up after an advance, so it is a shooting star, a bearish signal): A small body at the low of the session with a long upper shadow after an advance is a shooting star, and it warns that buyers were rejected near the high. The inverted hammer has the same shape but appears after a decline and is bullish. A hanging man has a long lower shadow, and a spinning top has fairly balanced shadows, so neither describes a lone long upper shadow.
  24. The correct answer is D (A five-day rally is a minor movement, since primary trends normally last over a year): Dow classified movements by duration and role, not by direction alone. A primary trend, the tide, normally lasts more than one year. A secondary trend, the wave, normally lasts about three weeks to three months. A move lasting five days is a minor movement, a ripple. Calling it primary because it went up ignores duration, and secondary trends do not last about a week.
  25. The correct answer is A (This is a band walk showing a strong trend, so upper-band closes are not reversal signals.): A squeeze is a period of narrow bands signaling low volatility ahead of a breakout. Here the bands are wide and price rides the upper band, which is a band walk typical of a strong trend. A touch of the band is not a sell signal by itself in such a trend. Expecting a fast reversion to the middle band applies a range-market assumption to a trending market.
  26. The correct answer is B (When nearly everyone is already bearish, few sellers remain to push price lower.): Contrary opinion holds that when nearly everyone is bearish, most who wanted to sell have already sold, so little selling pressure remains and a bottom is more likely. The VIX falling does not itself lift prices. Short covering is buying, not a supply of sellers, and sentiment readings do not predict earnings.
  27. The correct answer is A (Prepare to buy and wait for price confirmation at support, since extreme bearishness is contrarian.): Contrarians treat heavy bearishness and a high put/call ratio as signs that pessimism is near its peak and that most sellers have already acted. Sentiment identifies a condition, not a date, so the best approach is to prepare and let price confirm the turn at support. Complacency is shown by a low put/call ratio, not a high one.
  28. The correct answer is C (Gains are valued with a concave curve and losses with a convex curve, changing risk preference): Prospect theory measures outcomes as gains and losses from a reference point, here the purchase price of 50. The curve is concave for gains, so a sure gain is preferred, and convex for losses, so a gamble that might avoid a loss is preferred. That fits selling at 60 and holding at 40. Final wealth is the expected utility view, which prospect theory replaced.
  29. The correct answer is C (Long black candle, small gap-down candle, then a long white candle closing well into the first.): A morning star starts with a long black candle in a downtrend, followed by a small body that gaps lower and shows indecision, then a long white candle that closes well into the first body. The evening star is the mirror image at tops, and three white soldiers is a different three-candle advance. Without a strong third candle the reversal lacks confirmation.
  30. The correct answer is D (As independent trades accumulate, the average result tends to move toward the system's true expected value.): The law of large numbers says the sample average of independent, identically distributed outcomes approaches the expected value as the sample grows. It does not say that losses make later wins more likely, which is the gambler's fallacy. A sample of ten trades is far too small to expect convergence. Convergence is toward the system's expectancy, which here is positive, not toward zero.
  31. The correct answer is C (Signals lag the trend, because a definite reversal must be confirmed first): Waiting for confirmation and a definite reversal means signals show up well after turning points, which is the lag criticism. Subjectivity and the ambiguity of sideways line formations are real criticisms too, but neither is about timing. The claim that volume has no role is wrong, because volume confirming the trend is one of the basic tenets.
  32. The correct answer is C (Yes: the IQR is 3, the upper fence is 6.5, and 9 lies above that fence.): The IQR is 2 minus minus 1, or 3. The upper fence is the third quartile plus 1.5 times the IQR: 2 plus 4.5, or 6.5. Nine is above 6.5, so the rule flags it. A z-score near 2.6 would not trip a 3 standard deviation rule, but that is a different test, and 11 as a fence uses 3 times the IQR, a stricter cutoff for extreme outliers.
  33. The correct answer is D (Mental accounting for the separate accounts and framing for the changed wording of the same odds.): Treating profits and core capital as different pots is mental accounting, and reacting differently to the same odds stated as a win or a loss is framing. Anchoring and availability do not describe either behavior. Calling both mental accounting ignores that the wording changes the perceived choice, and representativeness concerns similarity to categories, not wording.
  34. The correct answer is B (Today's close is 6.5% above the close ten days earlier, showing positive, rising momentum.): Rate of change compares the current close with the close a set number of periods back. A reading of +6.5 in percent form means price is 6.5% higher than ten days ago, and a rising reading means momentum is increasing. It is not a distance from a moving average or a daily gain, and a positive value alone is not an overbought signal.
  35. The correct answer is A (Participation is narrowing, a negative divergence that undermines the new index high.): The index made a new high while the advance-decline line did not, more issues declined than advanced, and new highs shrank sharply. Fewer stocks are carrying the index, a negative divergence that warns the advance is fragile. A near miss on the line is not a confirmation, and fewer new highs do not signal hidden accumulation.
  36. The correct answer is A (The upper shadow is 2.80, and the body is black since close is below open.): The upper shadow runs from the top of the real body to the high. The close of 86.90 is below the open of 87.40, so the body is black and its top is the open, which gives 90.20 minus 87.40, or 2.80. Measuring from the close gives 3.30, and a body is white only when the close is above the open.
  37. The correct answer is B (Investors judge outcomes as gains and losses from a reference point and weigh losses more heavily.): Reference-point gains and losses with extra weight on losses come from prospect theory, which is the basis of behavioral finance. Traditional theory assumes rational agents who maximize expected utility, are risk averse, process information without bias, and see mispricing corrected by arbitrage. The wrong options are all traditional assumptions, so each is true of the standard framework and not the exception.
  38. The correct answer is D (Holders who bought near the old support now sell to exit at breakeven, supplying shares there): When support fails, those who bought there are left holding losses. On a return to their purchase price many sell to get out even, and that supply turns the old support into resistance. A trendline or moving average may coincide with the level, but neither explains why that specific price matters. Sellers from the earlier peak are not the group trapped at the old support level.
  39. The correct answer is B (Adapt to the bias by phasing sales gradually under a diversification limit she agrees to.): Endowment and status quo feelings come from emotion rather than faulty information, so information alone rarely fixes them, whereas cognitive errors respond to education. The best approach adapts to the bias with a gradual, agreed plan. Forced selling ignores the client relationship and waiting for a recovery keeps the risk in place.
  40. The correct answer is C (A secondary reaction in the primary uptrend; recognizing it helps investors resist fear selling.): A decline of about four weeks that gives back roughly half of the advance fits Dow's secondary movement, which lasts from three weeks to several months. The primary trend is judged to continue when the market makes a new peak afterward. Recognizing a correction as normal helps investors resist panic selling, whereas calling it a ripple ignores its length.
  41. The correct answer is A (Cap-weighted, because the larger decline in the $400 billion company outweighs the smaller company's gain.): Under cap weighting the $50 stock is the largest holding, so its 2% fall costs about $8 billion of value against a $2 billion gain from the 5% rise in the smallest-capitalization leader. Price weighting would rise because the $200 stock gains $10 against a $1 loss. Equal weighting would show about plus 1%. Survivorship bias is a data issue, not a weighting rule.
  42. The correct answer is B (Bands narrow, price breaks out, the bands widen, then price may walk the upper band.): Bollinger observed that volatility cycles between quiet and active periods, so a squeeze of narrow bands tends to precede a large move. The breakout is followed by wider bands as volatility increases, and in a strong trend price can ride along the outer band. Bands narrowing after a breakout or a walk preceding the squeeze reverses the logic.
  43. The correct answer is A (The close shows the final consensus of the session and which side held control.): The closing price is the last agreement between buyers and sellers for the session, so a close at the high shows buyers kept control to the end while a mid-range close shows balance. The high and low do carry information about how far each side pushed, and the close is not an average of other prices. Volume is a separate measure of participation.
  44. The correct answer is B (Assign probabilities, attach utility to outcomes, compute probability-weighted utility, then choose the highest.): Expected utility theory assigns probabilities, converts outcomes into utility, takes the probability-weighted sum for each option and picks the largest. Choosing by the best single outcome ignores probability. Reference points belong to prospect theory, and framing and regret belong to behavioral finance rather than the traditional model.
  45. The correct answer is C (Long black body, small body gapping lower, then long white body closing deep into the first.): The morning star starts with a long black candle that confirms the downtrend, followed by a small-bodied candle that gaps lower and shows indecision, and ends with a long white candle that closes well into the first body, showing buyers retaking control. The version with white, small, black is the evening star, a top pattern. Putting the small body first or last breaks the three-step shift from selling to indecision to buying.
  46. The correct answer is A (Buying pressure is weakening even as price edges higher, so fewer participants drive the advance.): A higher price high paired with a lower RSI high is bearish divergence, which shows the latest push took less momentum and that buying participation is thinning. It is a warning, not confirmation of a reversal, and it is not a bullish sign of broadening participation. The oscillator reflects current momentum, not a restatement of earlier price weakness.
  47. The correct answer is C (Confirm the percentage range is large, switch to a log scale, then draw the trendlines): A rise from 1,000 to 9,000 is a very large percentage change, so a log scale is preferred, and the trendlines should be drawn after the scale is chosen because lines do not carry from one scale to the other. Keeping arithmetic scale misjudges the size of the gains, and carrying or only partly redrawing lines leaves distorted slopes.
  48. The correct answer is D (The value function is concave for gains from the reference point, producing risk aversion in gains.): Prospect theory measures outcomes as gains or losses from a reference point and has an S-shaped value function, concave for gains. That shape makes a sure gain more attractive than a gamble of higher expected value. The other options reverse the risk attitude, flatten the value function, or reverse probability weighting.
  49. The correct answer is D (The public participation phase, when trend followers join the advance and business news turns favorable.): The second phase is public participation, where technical followers and the wider market join as earnings improve; it is usually the longest and largest part of the advance. Accumulation is the first phase and distribution the third. A secondary reaction is a separate concept, a countertrend move, and does not name a phase of the primary trend.
  50. The correct answer is A (It opened at 54.30 and closed at 55.10, an up day gaining 0.80.): On an OHLC bar the left tick is the open and the right tick is the close, so the open was 54.30 and the close 55.10, a gain of 0.80. Reversing the ticks gives a down day, using the top and bottom as open and close ignores the ticks, and the full range of 1.70 is high minus low, not the net change.
  51. The correct answer is C (Pessimism looks excessive, so the readings point toward a possible market bottom.): Contrarian analysis holds that the crowd tends to be most one-sided near turning points. An extreme put/call ratio together with heavy bearish survey results suggests selling pressure is near exhaustion. Following the crowd into more shorts reverses that logic, and sentiment indicators are most useful precisely at their extremes.
  52. The correct answer is D (It needs a larger counter-move before direction flips, filtering minor fluctuations.): The reversal amount sets how far price must move against the current column before a new column begins, so raising it from one box to three filters minor fluctuations. Box size is a separate setting, point and figure columns carry no fixed time span, and the chart does not use volume to decide a reversal.
  53. The correct answer is D (The euro strengthened, since one euro now buys more pounds, and EUR/GBP is a cross pair.): A currency quote states how many units of the quote currency one unit of the base currency buys. A rise from 0.8600 to 0.8700 pounds per euro means the euro gained against the pound. Major pairs include the U.S. dollar on one side, so a pair of two non-dollar currencies is a cross pair. Heavy trading volume or base currency status does not make a pair major, and a rising quote never signals a weaker base.
  54. The correct answer is B (Win rate ignores payoff size; here 0.72 times 0.4 minus 0.28 times 1.6 gives negative 0.16R.): Expectancy weights each outcome by its probability: 0.72 times 0.4R gives 0.288R of winners, and 0.28 times 1.6R gives 0.448R of losers, so expectancy is negative 0.16R per trade. A high win rate cannot offset losses four times the size of the wins. Swapping the payoffs, leaving the loss unweighted, or adding the loss term each produce a different number that no longer reflects the stated results.
  55. The correct answer is D (Endowment bias, an emotional bias, rooted in ownership feelings so training seldom removes it): Valuing an asset above its market price merely because it is owned, and willing to pay less to acquire it, is the textbook mark of endowment bias. Status quo bias is a preference for leaving things unchanged and does not involve a higher personal valuation. Loss aversion is close but concerns the pain of realizing losses. Emotional biases come from feelings rather than faulty reasoning, which is why education alone rarely fixes them.
  56. The correct answer is B (Foreign exchange trades on one central exchange that reports a single official volume figure): Foreign exchange is a decentralized dealer market with no central exchange, so there is no single official volume figure and data comes from dealers and brokers. The other three notes are accurate: futures report open interest, many bonds trade over the counter with thin volume data, and bitcoin trades continuously on several venues whose prices can differ slightly. The false note is the one that gives currencies a central exchange.
  57. The correct answer is D (A buy signal, because the shorter average crossing above the longer shows rising momentum.): In a standard two average system, the shorter average crossing above the longer one is the buy signal, because recent prices are rising faster than the longer term average. Reading it as overextended reverses the rule, and extra requirements such as a pullback or a rising slow average belong to filtered variants, not the basic system the stem describes.
  58. The correct answer is A (P is a squeeze before a bigger move; Q is a band walk in an uptrend.): Narrow bands mean volatility has contracted, and Bollinger's insight is that low volatility tends to be followed by high volatility, so P is a squeeze that warns of a larger move without giving direction. Repeated closes at the upper band while the bands widen are a band walk, which shows strong trend momentum rather than an automatic sell. Treating the squeeze as bearish adds a direction the bands do not supply, and calling P an expansion reverses what narrowing bands show.
  59. The correct answer is D (About 2.8 standard deviations below the mean, beyond 95% but inside 99.7%, so it is rare.): The distance from the mean is minus 4.1 minus 0.1, or minus 4.2 points. Dividing by the 1.5 standard deviation gives a z-score of minus 2.8. The Empirical Rule puts 95% of moves within 2 standard deviations and 99.7% within 3, so a 2.8 move is rare but not off the scale. Dividing by the variance of 2.25 would wrongly give 1.9.
  60. The correct answer is C (Charts need only prices set by active trading, so any liquid traded instrument qualifies): The flaw is the belief that earnings are required. Technical analysis needs only prices formed by active trading, which currencies, futures and ETFs all have. ETF prices come from trading, not from a chart rule, and policy influence is present in equities as well. A global session is still a continuous market that can be charted.
  61. The correct answer is C (Confirmation bias, seeking evidence that supports an existing view while discounting evidence that contradicts it): Reading only the supportive notes and skipping the contrary forecasts is selective searching, which is confirmation bias. Conservatism is related but would involve reading the new data and revising too little. Hindsight bias concerns past outcomes, and availability concerns ease of recall. The trader's routine filters the evidence before it can matter.
  62. The correct answer is A (Buyers absorbed the supply that halted the first rally, so demand now exceeds supply.): The circled X column rises above the earlier X column top, which shows buyers absorbed the supply that stopped the first rally, so demand exceeds supply. Column length measures price distance, not days, and the circle marks a breakout rather than a reversal. A shorter O column is not itself the evidence, since the breakout above the prior high is what matters.
  63. The correct answer is C (Standard deviation is the square root of variance, so it is stated in dollars.): Variance averages squared deviations, so its unit is squared dollars. Taking the square root returns the figure to dollars, which matches the units of the price series. Keeping squared dollars ignores the root. A percentage of price describes the coefficient of variation, a different statistic. The average absolute deviation is a distinct measure that does not equal the square root of variance.
  64. The correct answer is D (Tuning averages on 15 years and reporting the best result is valid out of sample evidence.): The question asks for the false statement. Choosing parameters that maximize results on the whole data set and then reporting that result is curve fitting, because the same data both built and judged the rule. The other three statements describe sound practice: a stated hypothesis with a criterion, validation on unseen data, and awareness that a fitted rule may not persist.
  65. The correct answer is A (Anchor at the 80 low and 120 high, draw ratios down, watch 104.7 to 95.3.): A retracement is drawn from the swing low to the swing high, then the ratios are applied to that 40-point move: 120 minus 0.382 times 40 gives 104.7, and 120 minus 0.618 times 40 gives 95.3. The levels must exist before the pullback so they can be anticipated. Zero-based measurement and upward projections answer different questions.
  66. The correct answer is C (The run does not by itself predict continuation, and drifting random walks can produce such runs): A martingale says the expected next price equals the current price given the history, so a past run does not by itself forecast continuation. A random walk with drift can generate long sequences of higher highs and lows by chance, so the chart is not proof of predictability. The distractors misread martingale as momentum, wrongly claim runs are impossible under randomness, or assume efficiency demands flat prices.
  67. The correct answer is A (A strong uptrend: rising ADX shows strength and +DI above -DI shows upward direction.): The ADX measures how strong a trend is without indicating its direction, and readings above about 25 typically indicate a trending market. The +DI and -DI lines supply direction, so +DI above -DI means upward pressure dominates. A rising ADX is not an overbought signal and does not rise only in downtrends.
  68. The correct answer is A (Many participants and continuous price discovery make prices reflect supply and demand, not isolated trades.): Technical analysis assumes prices result from the interaction of many buyers and sellers, so it needs liquidity, many participants, free price discovery, and reliable data. The thinly traded bond fails those conditions, since a handful of trades can distort the chart. Volatility can make signals noisier, trading costs affect profits, and a currency market works without a central closing price.
  69. The correct answer is D (Summation, the principle that the market's movement is the sum of all cycles acting together.): Summation says price is the sum of all cycles acting at once, so when several cycle lows coincide, their downward pushes add together and produce a larger decline than any one cycle explains. Synchronicity describes why the lows lined up, but the question asks how cycles combine into price. Harmonicity and nominality describe cycle lengths and relationships, not how they add up.
  70. The correct answer is A (An ascending triangle, which usually has a bullish bias and breaks upward.): An ascending triangle has a flat upper boundary and rising lower boundary, showing buyers pressing against a resistance level, and it usually breaks upward. A descending triangle is its mirror image, a rising wedge slopes up with converging lines and leans bearish, and a symmetrical triangle has both boundaries converging with no inherent bias.
  71. The correct answer is C (Felt value is negative 25, so the investor declines though the dollar expectation is plus 25.): The dollar expectation is 0.5 times 150 minus 0.5 times 100, which is plus 25. With the loss weighted twice, the felt value is 0.5 times 150 minus 0.5 times 200, or negative 25, so a loss averse investor declines. Dropping the weight gives plus 25, dropping the probabilities gives negative 50, and counting only the gain gives plus 75.
  72. The correct answer is C (Representativeness, classifying the company by resemblance to past winners): He sorts the company into a category of winners because of surface features and neglects the base rate of failure, which is representativeness. Availability is related, since it is based on ease of recall, but he is classifying by resemblance. Illusion of control and hindsight are other belief perseverance biases that do not match what he did.
  73. The correct answer is B (Emotional biases are best corrected by giving clients more data, since they come from faulty reasoning): The false statement says more data fixes emotional biases, which arise from feelings and not from reasoning errors. The statement about cognitive errors is true, since information can reduce those. Status quo bias and regret aversion are both accurate descriptions of emotional biases, so they tempt only a reader who doubts well known definitions.
  74. The correct answer is A (The option gains about 0.80 from delta, then delta rises to about 0.50 because of gamma.): Delta is the option's price change per one point in the stock, so 0.40 times 2 points is about 0.80. Gamma is the rate of change of delta, so a 2 point rise adds about 0.05 times 2, or 0.10, lifting delta to roughly 0.50. The option that says delta falls gets the gamma direction wrong for a long call, and the one that credits 0.10 to gamma and raises delta by 0.80 swaps the two Greeks. A one-for-one move would need a delta of 1.0, not 0.40.
  75. The correct answer is B (A gain of 2,400 dollars, since 16 points times 50 dollars times three contracts applies.): A short futures position profits when price falls. The index fell 16 points, from 4,516 to 4,500. Each point is worth 50 dollars on each contract, so 16 times 50 times 3 equals 2,400 dollars. Using only one contract gives 800, and leaving out the point value gives a meaningless 48. A loss would apply only if the trader had been long.
  76. The correct answer is C (Roughly 62, found by adding the 12-point flagpole to the 50 breakout price.): The flagpole runs from 40 to 52, a height of 12. The standard rule adds that height to the breakout point of 50, giving a target near 62. Adding it to 52 gives 64, using half the pole gives 56, and subtracting gives 38, which would suit a breakdown rather than an upside break.
  77. The correct answer is C (It would judge each holding on future expected utility, ignoring her purchase price): A rational expected utility investor looks forward, so each stock is judged on what it is likely to do next and the purchase price is a sunk fact. Ranking by gain, waiting for breakeven and banking gains early are all reference point habits, which describe her behavioral choices rather than the traditional model.
  78. The correct answer is B (The upper band is 89.00 and the lower band is 79.00.): Default Bollinger Bands sit two standard deviations from the 20-period average, so the offset is 2 times 2.50, which is 5.00. Adding and subtracting 5.00 from 84.00 gives 89.00 and 79.00. One standard deviation gives 86.50 and 81.50, three gives 91.50 and 76.50, and using the variance of 6.25 times two gives 12.50, producing 96.50 and 71.50.
  79. The correct answer is D (Gains are concentrated in the largest stocks while the typical stock lags, a narrow leadership warning.): A cap-weighted index leans on the largest companies, while an equal-weighted index treats all 500 alike. When the cap-weighted index makes new highs and the equal-weighted index goes sideways, gains are concentrated in a few large names while the typical stock lags, which is narrow leadership. Calling it a normal pause ignores that evidence. Volatility differences do not explain a full year of lag, and breadth divergences are warnings, not precise timing signals.
  80. The correct answer is B (A drop through 75 is not failure; price stopped near 69, the 0.618 level.): The swing is 50 points, so the 0.5 line is at 75 and the 0.618 line is at about 69.1, which is where the pullback stopped. Deep retracements to 0.618 are common in continuing advances. The levels come from the swing range, not from zero, no single ratio is the only support, and retracement lines apply within the swing.
  81. The correct answer is D (A long one-sided stretch is common in random series, so randomness is not rejected.): The arcsine law says random series tend to stay on one side of the starting level for long stretches, so 200 of 252 days is unremarkable and randomness is not rejected. Equal time is the popular misreading. A long stretch does not by itself show serial correlation, and a martingale places no limit on days spent above the start.
  82. The correct answer is A (It opened at 23.0 and closed at 26.5, a gain of 3.5 from open to close.): On an OHLC bar the left tick marks the open and the right tick marks the close, so this week opened at 23.0 and closed at 26.5, a gain of 3.5. The ends of the vertical line are the low (22.5) and the high (27.0), and the 4.5 difference between them is the range, not the gain. Reversing the ticks would turn a gain into a loss.
  83. The correct answer is A (Test the rule on many instruments and periods, since one record can arise from random streaks): A random walk does not forbid streaks, so a single profitable record proves little. Testing across many instruments and periods shows whether the edge repeats beyond chance. Adopting it on three years of data trusts luck, rejecting everything misreads the theory, and tuning on the same sample builds in data-snooping bias.
  84. The correct answer is B (Emailing her employer's client list to a personal account to solicit those clients later): Taking the client list is the violation because the list is employer property and using it to compete while still employed breaks the duty of loyalty. Making ordinary preparations on her own time, using general skills, and contacting clients afterward from memory or public sources are all permitted. Each of those looks like competing against the employer, which is why they tempt, but none uses employer records or resources.
  85. The correct answer is A (Margin is posted, gains and losses settle daily, an offsetting sale closes it, open interest falls.): A futures buyer posts a performance deposit called initial margin, not the contract value. The exchange marks the position to market every day, so gains and losses settle in cash. Most traders exit with an offsetting trade before expiry, and when a long and a short close out together, open interest falls.
  86. The correct answer is D (Long-term holders are not selling into weakness, which supports an accumulation reading weighed with the chart.): Blockchain records are public, so analysts can measure how long coins sit unmoved, and a rising share of long-held coins during a pullback is read as accumulation. It complements chart tools rather than replacing them, which is why a technician still uses the trendline and Fibonacci level. The data come from the ledger, not from a dealer network. Holding through weakness is not distribution, which would show coins moving to exchanges for sale.
  87. The correct answer is D (That is non-confirmation, and Dow considered the closing price the most important): When one average makes a new high and the other fails to, the averages are not confirming each other, so the signal is non-confirmation. Dow regarded the closing price as the most important price of the day, because it reflects where participants settled after the trading. The intraday high and the opening price carry less weight in the theory. Both parts of the claim need correcting, and only one option corrects both.
  88. The correct answer is C (Propose selling in scheduled tranches over several quarters and tie proceeds to goals she has named.): Endowment is an emotional bias, so the sensible approach is to adapt the plan to the client rather than try to argue her out of it. A staged sale reduces risk gradually and the goal link reframes proceeds as serving her aims. A valuation report treats a feeling as a knowledge gap. Selling everything against her wishes harms the relationship, and doing nothing leaves the unsuitable concentration in place.
  89. The correct answer is C (ADX measures trend strength, not direction; -DI above +DI shows the strong trend is down.): ADX rises when a trend is strengthening and says nothing about whether it is up or down. The direction comes from the DI lines: with -DI at 31 above +DI at 14, downward movement dominates, so the strong trend is down. ADX is not an overbought or oversold gauge, and the DI lines show direction, not strength alone.
  90. The correct answer is B (Intrinsic value is 4 (54 minus 50), then subtract the 3.00 premium, leaving a 1.00 gain): A call's intrinsic value at expiration is the stock price minus the strike, here 54 minus 50 equals 4. The premium paid up front must then be subtracted, since it is the cost of owning the option, giving 4 minus 3 equals 1.00. Adding the premium overstates the result, and ignoring it reports gross rather than net profit. Subtracting the stock price from the strike would give the intrinsic value of a put.
  91. The correct answer is D (Look-ahead bias: the rule trades at the close using earnings released after the close): The rule uses information that did not exist at the moment of the trade: earnings released after the close are used to buy at that same close. That is look-ahead bias, and it can create excellent results without any real edge. Survivorship, tuning, and costs can distort tests, but nothing in the stem points to them.
  92. The correct answer is D (A reading above 80 means price must fall next, as stochastic cannot stay overbought.): Stochastic can stay above 80 for a long time in a strong advance, so an overbought reading does not mean price must fall. The other statements are accurate: it places the close within the recent range, overbought readings can persist, and the common defaults are a 14-period lookback with 80 and 20 lines.
  93. The correct answer is B (A bubble fed by investors copying each other, with price far beyond fundamentals before crashing): Accelerating prices, rising volume, public excitement, and a collapse on peak volume describe a bubble fed by herding. The decline is the crash phase, far from a secondary reaction, which comes inside an orderly primary trend. Efficient pricing would not require a parabolic run followed by a collapse without news. The disposition effect is about individual selling patterns and does not explain the parabolic advance.
  94. The correct answer is C (Buy on the first upward cross, sell on the downward cross, buy on the next): The basic rule buys when the faster average crosses above the slower one and sells when it crosses below. Over this sequence that means buy, sell, buy, which also shows how a crossover system can whipsaw in a range. Holding through the downward cross or reversing the signals departs from the rule.
  95. The correct answer is C (88, because the 40 percent adjustment adds 8 points to the anchor.): The gap between the anchor of 80 and the fundamental value of 100 is 20 points. Closing 40 percent of it adds 0.40 times 20, or 8 points, giving 88. Her target is still 12 points below fundamental value, which illustrates insufficient adjustment. Applying 60 percent, 40 percent of value, or a negative adjustment would give other numbers.
  96. The correct answer is B (About 76 points, from 24 percent divided by the square root of 252, applied to 5,000.): The VIX is quoted as an annualized percentage, so a daily figure divides by the square root of 252, about 15.87. That gives 24 divided by 15.87, or about 1.51 percent, and 1.51 percent of 5,000 is about 76 points. Dividing by 252 shrinks the figure far too much. A monthly divisor of 12 gives 2 percent, or 100 points, and using 24 percent unscaled gives the annual swing of 1,200 points.
  97. The correct answer is D (Stand aside until ADX turns up, since a low and falling reading signals no trend.): An ADX of 14 and falling says the market is range bound, and a trend-following system works poorly there whatever the DI lines show. A +DI lead alone is not a signal when ADX is low. A falling ADX describes fading trend strength, not a downtrend. Waiting for 40 is too strict, because trends are tradable once ADX turns up from a low reading and rises through about 20 to 25.
  98. The correct answer is D (Euro is the base and dollar the quote, so the position gains when the euro rises.): In a currency pair the first currency is the base and the second is the quote, so 1.0850 means one euro costs 1.0850 dollars. Buying the pair is buying euros, which gains when the euro rises against the dollar. The colleague reversed the convention. Interest rate differentials can add carry, but they do not change that the position is a directional bet on the euro.
  99. The correct answer is A (It is not an outlier, being under the upper fence of 2.55, Q3 plus 1.5 IQR.): The IQR is Q3 minus Q1, or 1.5 minus 0.8, which is 0.7. The upper fence is Q3 plus 1.5 times the IQR: 1.5 plus 1.05 equals 2.55. Because 2.4 is below 2.55, it is not an outlier by this rule. Using a single IQR gives 2.2 and would wrongly flag it. Comparing with the median is not the rule, and using a multiplier of three reaches the right conclusion with the wrong fence of 3.6.
  100. The correct answer is D (The pullback to 69 retraced about 38 percent of the rally, stopping at the second line.): The rally is 50 points, and the pullback of 31 points from 100 to 69 is 31 divided by 50, or 62 percent, not 38 percent. The 0.382 line sits at 80.9, and 69.1 is the 0.618 line. The other three statements are correct reads of the chart, which is why they are the tempting but wrong choices here.
  101. The correct answer is C (A breakaway gap, as traders rushed to establish positions once price escaped the long consolidation.): A gap that opens as price leaves a long consolidation on heavy volume is a breakaway gap, and the volume shows new participants rushing in rather than a drift within the range. A common gap occurs inside quiet ranges and fills fast, which does not fit the heavy volume. Runaway gaps appear mid-trend and exhaustion gaps near the end of a long move, but this stock had no prior trend to accelerate or exhaust.
  102. The correct answer is C (The transportation average, because goods must be shipped, so transport strength confirms industrial demand.): Dow paired the Industrial Average with the Transportation Average because goods that are made must be moved to market, so a high in one that the other fails to match is a warning. Utilities and composites were not part of his original confirmation pair, and a broader index does not test the logic of production followed by shipment.
  103. The correct answer is D (A triple top breakout buy signal, since this column cleared two equal peaks at 57.): The circled column rises to 58 after two earlier X columns peaked at 57, so price has cleared two equal tops. That is a triple top breakout, a buy signal. Stalling at 57 would have meant a sell setup only if price had dropped below the lows instead. The breakout needs only one box above the tops; the three-box figure is the reversal size, not the breakout size. The O column before it simply set up the rally.
  104. The correct answer is B (Hold but tighten the stop, treating the doji as a warning needing next-candle confirmation.): A doji after a rally signals indecision and a possible tiring of the trend, but it needs next candle confirmation. Holding while tightening the stop respects both facts. Selling half treats the warning as a partial reversal, widening the stop adds risk when a warning has appeared, and a gap down is not the only valid confirmation.
  105. The correct answer is C (Survivorship bias, since failed or dropped companies are missing and the results are overstated): Using only today's index members drops companies that failed or were removed, which is survivorship bias and overstates the result. The other biases are real, but the stem gives no sign that the start date was chosen, that many strategies were tried, or that parameters were tuned, since buy and hold has none.
  106. The correct answer is B (The analyst shows conservatism by under revising; the colleague shows confirmation bias by filtering evidence.): Conservatism means updating beliefs too little when new information arrives, which fits the analyst's small estimate changes. Confirmation bias means selecting and weighting evidence that agrees with the existing view, which fits the colleague. The behaviors are easy to swap, since both protect an existing view, but one concerns the amount of updating and the other concerns the choice of evidence.
  107. The correct answer is D (Because r is high, one extreme outlier could not materially change the fitted slope.): Both r and the least squares slope are sensitive to outliers, so a single extreme point can shift them noticeably even when r looks high. The other statements are true: the sign is negative, 0.85 squared is about 0.72, and forecasts outside the observed range are less reliable. The false statement is the claim that a high r makes the fit immune to outliers.
  108. The correct answer is B (Many traders watch the same levels, so shared orders can create a reaction): The reaction near a retracement level is explained by many traders watching the same numbers and placing orders together, not by a law of nature. The ratio does not measure volume, institutions do not publish obligatory levels, and no evidence shows most pullbacks stop at 61.8 percent. The level is a shared reference, not a guarantee.
  109. The correct answer is C (Decline had new shorts confirming it; the bounce is short covering, not fresh buying.): Falling price with rising open interest means new shorts are being added, confirming the downtrend. A bounce with open interest falling means positions are being closed, which points to short covering rather than new buyers. That makes the bounce a weak advance within the larger decline.
  110. The correct answer is A (As a possible bullish setup, but wait for a higher low or other confirmation first): Contrary opinion treats extreme bearishness as a setup for a rally, because most sellers have already sold. Sentiment gives a zone, not a date, and price is still making lower lows, so confirmation such as a higher low is needed before buying. Shorting with the crowd follows the majority at the extreme, and the put/call ratio is itself a standard sentiment measure that needs no insider data to be valid.
  111. The correct answer is D (Subtract the head-to-neckline height from the neckline break, giving a downside target near 60.): The measuring rule for a head and shoulders top takes the vertical distance from the head to the neckline, 20 points, and projects it downward from the neckline break at 80, giving about 60. Adding it above the head reverses the direction of this bearish pattern. Starting from the head's peak or using the shoulder gives the wrong base or size, and the pattern is a reversal, not a continuation.
  112. The correct answer is C (About 62 percent was retraced, reaching the 0.618 line near 69, a common support area.): A retracement is measured from the high back toward the low: 100 minus 69 is 31 points, and 31 divided by the 50 point advance is about 0.62, so the 0.618 line at 69.1 has been reached. The 19 points above the low is the part of the move retained, not the part given back, which tempts a reading of 38 percent. Extensions project beyond the swing high, so they do not apply to a pullback.
  113. The correct answer is C (0.90, and it is a white (up) body because the close is above the open.): The real body spans open to close: 102.10 minus 101.20 is 0.90. The close is above the open, so the body is white or up. The full range of 2.10 includes both shadows, and 1.70 runs from the low, which is the lower shadow plus the body. Shadow length does not set the color.
  114. The correct answer is D (Sell P to lock in the gain, keep Q to avoid making the loss real): The disposition effect is selling winners too early and holding losers too long. Loss aversion makes realizing a loss painful, so the trader keeps Q and sells P to lock in the gain. Cutting the loser is the technician's rule and the opposite of the effect. The house money effect concerns risk taking with winnings, and mental accounting treats positions as separate accounts rather than one combined portfolio.
  115. The correct answer is A (Hold the position, since a minor dip is not a definite reversal while both averages confirm.): Dow held that a trend persists until a definite reversal, and a two day dip in one average after joint new highs is a minor movement. Confirmation requires the averages to agree in direction, not to match in size. Selling or shorting on a small dip treats noise as a reversal signal.
  116. The correct answer is A (A bullish engulfing pattern marks a bottom only after a decline, not after an advance.): A reversal pattern needs a trend to reverse, and a bullish engulfing candle signals a bottom only after a decline. After a nine week advance there is no bottom to mark, so buying on it is a flaw. The pattern does not require a gap or repeated candles, and a bearish version, not the bullish one, would suggest a top after a rally.
  117. The correct answer is D (0.05 from stock divided by benchmark, rising because the stock is outperforming while the benchmark declines.): A relative strength ratio is the stock's price divided by the benchmark's price, so 80 divided by 1,600 gives 0.05. A rising ratio line means the stock is doing better than the benchmark, which here happens while the benchmark falls, so the stock need not be rising in absolute terms. Dividing the other way gives 20 and flips the meaning of the line. Averaging the two prices gives (80 + 1,600) / 2 = 840, and 840 / 1,600 = 0.525, which is not a relative strength measure.
  118. The correct answer is D (A buying climax, where heavy emotional buying exhausts demand and often marks a short-term top.): Rising prices on shrinking volume already showed weak participation. A spike on triple volume followed by a reversal that closes far below the high on even heavier volume is the signature of a buying climax: the last eager buyers were absorbed by sellers. Heavy volume alone would confirm a trend, but here the close at 36 undercuts it. A selling climax occurs at lows, and a VWAP pullback does not require a failed spike on record volume.
  119. The correct answer is C (The relative strength line leading price is bullish, and price may soon follow it higher.): A relative strength line that makes a new high while price lags shows the stock is outperforming its benchmark, and relative strength often leads price. That is a bullish signal that price may follow. Waiting for price to confirm throws away the lead the line offers. The line belongs to the stock, not the sector. An overbought reading comes from oscillators such as RSI, which differ from a ratio against a benchmark.
  120. The correct answer is C (About 0.618, which is the reciprocal of the golden ratio of 1.618.): Dividing a number by the next larger one gives 34/55 = 0.618 and 55/89 = 0.618, so the quotients converge on 0.618, the reciprocal of 1.618. Dividing by the number two places higher gives about 0.382, which is a separate ratio. Putting the larger number on top gives 1.618. The 0.500 level is not derived from the sequence.
  121. The correct answer is D (Define the rule, record net-of-cost returns, simulate random walks, then compare the rule's result): A fair test fixes the rule first, measures its result net of costs, and then asks how often a pure random walk would produce a result as good, using a simulated distribution. Tuning on the test data, ignoring costs, and testing on the fitting sample all build in a bias that makes a rule look better than it is.
  122. The correct answer is D (Average the data, square deviations, divide the sum by n minus 1, then take the root): The sample standard deviation starts with the mean, squares every deviation from it so negatives do not cancel, sums them, divides by n minus 1 for a sample, and takes the square root to return to the original units. Summing raw deviations gives zero. The shortcut that subtracts the mean late does not produce the same result, and using the median with n describes a different measure.
  123. The correct answer is B (State a testable hypothesis, then test it on data not used to find the pattern.): The scientific method turns an observation into a hypothesis and tests it out of sample so that the discovery data cannot confirm itself. Optimizing the length on the same two years is curve fitting, and popularity among analysts is not evidence. Trading a full position puts capital at risk before the idea has been checked.
  124. The correct answer is A (The 250 days are a sample, and the full 12-year history is the population.): A population is the entire group being studied, here the whole 12 year history, and a sample is a subset used to learn about it, here the 250 days. Calling the days the population tempts because they were measured directly, but measurement does not make a subset complete. Parameter and statistic describe numbers, not groups, and a parameter belongs to a population. Treating the history as another sample ignores that it is the full group of interest.
  125. The correct answer is A (The exponential average gives more weight to recent prices, so it responds to the jump sooner.): The exponential average applies larger weights to recent prices, so it moves toward a sudden jump faster, while the simple average spreads a jump across all 14 equal weights. Neither average weights by volume, which is a VWAP feature, and the smoothing factor makes the exponential average faster, not delayed.
  126. The correct answer is D (When law and the Standards differ, members must comply with the stricter of the two): Standard I(A) requires compliance with applicable law and, where the Code and Standards set a higher bar, with the Code and Standards, so the stricter rule governs. Using the Standards only when law is silent, only when adopted, or only with a report to CFA Institute each lets a member follow a weaker rule, which is why convenience cannot guide the choice.
  127. The correct answer is C (State the hypothesis, define rules, test on history, confirm on separate data, then trade or revise.): The scientific method starts with a hypothesis, turns it into precise testable rules, tests it on historical data, and then confirms it on data not used to build it. Choosing the best of many variations before stating a hypothesis is data mining. Trading live first risks capital before any evidence exists, and a backtest run afterward only confirms what is already known.
  128. The correct answer is B (Stocks fall, traders buy index puts, option prices and implied volatility rise, and the VIX climbs.): When stocks drop sharply, traders buy index puts for protection, which lifts option prices and therefore implied volatility, and the VIX rises. The VIX is derived from option prices, so it cannot be rebuilt from past closes alone. Treating the VIX as the starting cause flips the order. Selling puts for income would lower implied volatility, which is the opposite of what happens in a sell-off.
  129. The correct answer is B (Restated to 50.00, so the one-day change is a gain of 3.0 percent.): Dividing 150 by 3 gives 50.00, and the next close of 51.50 is up 1.50, which is 1.50 divided by 50, or 3.0 percent. Leaving the 150 gives a fall of 98.50 over 150, or 65.7 percent. Multiplying by 3 gives 450, and 51.50 against 450 is a fall of 88.6 percent. Dividing by 2 gives 75, and 51.50 against 75 is a fall of 31.3 percent.
  130. The correct answer is D (A term over the next one gives 0.618; over the term two ahead it gives 0.382.): Check with 21 and 34: 21/34 is 0.618, 21/55 is 0.382 and 13/55 is 0.236, so the one-ahead ratio is 0.618, two ahead is 0.382 and three ahead is 0.236. The reciprocal 1.618 appears when dividing a term by the one before it. Shifting each offset by one place mislabels all three levels. Neighbor ratios settle near 0.618, not 0.5.
  131. The correct answer is D (One rising average is not confirmation, and volume is a supporting check she cannot dismiss.): Dow Theory needs both averages to signal the same direction, so one rising and one flat is not confirmation. Volume is a supporting check she cannot dismiss. A minimum trend length is a separate idea, a flat average does not by itself mean a reaction, and volume never replaced the second average.
  132. The correct answer is B (Accumulation base, markup, distribution top, then markdown, matching points A through D.): Wyckoff's cycle runs accumulation, markup, distribution and markdown. Point A sits on the low base where informed buyers accumulate, point B is the advance, point C is the top where supply is distributed, and point D is the decline. Distribution cannot precede markup because the top forms after the advance. Starting the sequence at markup or at the distribution top would leave point A on a base mislabeled as a rally or a top.
  133. The correct answer is D (The coupon rate, the fixed yearly interest quoted as a share of face value): The fixed percentage of face value paid as interest is the coupon rate, so 4 percent of face is the coupon. Yield to maturity depends on the 98.50 price and so differs from the coupon. Par value is the principal returned at maturity, not the interest rate. The credit spread compares a bond's yield with a government benchmark and is unrelated to the interest formula.
  134. The correct answer is A (A bull flag has resolved upward, and the prior advance is likely to resume.): A flag is a short, sloping consolidation against a sharp prior move, and it normally forms on declining volume before volume expands on the breakout. That is the textbook volume signature for a continuation pattern, not distribution. A wedge or double top would show different geometry and a reversal bias.
  135. The correct answer is B (Rising open interest in an advancing market is bearish, since longs will liquidate.): Rising prices with rising open interest is a bullish confirmation because new money is entering and supporting the advance, so calling it bearish is the exception. Open interest rises when both sides open new contracts, a transfer of an existing long to a new buyer leaves it unchanged, and an advance with falling open interest points to short covering instead of fresh buying.
  136.
