The correct answer is D (VIX spikes tend to coincide with market lows, so extreme readings often mark selling climaxes.): The VIX measures the 30-day volatility implied by S&P 500 options, and it generally rises when the index falls. A sharp spike reflects fear, and fear peaks have often lined up with market bottoms. That makes the analyst's forecast of continued decline weak, and a spike is not a calm-market reading.
  137. The correct answer is D (Pennant: converging lines form a small triangle, whereas a flag has parallel boundaries.): Both flags and pennants follow a sharp move and have light volume. The difference is shape: a flag has parallel lines slanting against the trend, and a pennant has converging lines forming a small triangle. This formation converges, so it is a pennant. Light volume in a consolidation does not signal reversal, and the lines here do not both rise.
  138. The correct answer is B (26.11, found as 36 minus 0.618 times the 16-point advance.): The advance is 36 minus 20, or 16 points. Then 0.618 x 16 = 9.888, and 36 - 9.888 = 26.11. Measuring 0.618 up from the low gives 29.89, which is actually the 0.382 retracement level. The midpoint is 28.00 and 0.236 x 16 = 3.776 gives 32.22.
  139. The correct answer is A (A martingale needs independent, identically distributed price changes, which is stricter than a random walk.): The independence and identical distribution requirement belongs to the random walk, the stricter model; a martingale only requires the expected next price to equal the current price given the information set. The other three statements are true. The arcsine law explains why random series often appear to trend for long stretches.
  140. The correct answer is A (Price equals 101.25 percent of face value, so each bond is worth 1,012.50): Bond prices are quoted as a percentage of face value, so 101.25 means 101.25 percent of 1,000, or 1,012.50 per bond, a small premium to par. It is not a spread in basis points, not a dollar price per bond, and not a ratio of yield to coupon. Those readings confuse the price quote with yield terms.
  141. The correct answer is D (21 divided by 13 gives about 1.615, close to 1.618 and converging as terms grow): The sequence is built by adding the two previous terms, and the ninth term is 21 while the eighth is 13. Dividing 21 by 13 gives 1.615, which is already near the limiting value of 1.618, and later pairs such as 144 divided by 89 land closer still. The value 13 divided by 21 is 0.619, the reciprocal, not the Golden Ratio. Dividing by the term two places back gives 2.625, and the ratio is 1.5 only for the early pair 3 and 2, not for every pair.
  142. The correct answer is A (A fall to 44, three boxes of 2 points from the top, starts an O column.): With boxes of 2 points, a three-box reversal is 6 points. From the top at 50 the price must reach 44 before an O column starts, with the first O drawn one box below the top. Using 3 points counts points rather than boxes, one box ignores the reversal rule, and 42 requires four boxes.
  143. The correct answer is A (Traditional theory holds that investors systematically misjudge probabilities and therefore often depart from expected utility maximization.): Systematic misjudgment of probabilities is what behavioral finance documents, so attributing it to traditional theory is the false claim. Traditional theory does allow random individual errors that cancel out, assumes correct updating, and values outcomes by final wealth. Those three are accurate, though each can look behavioral to a hasty reader.

Practice Test 6 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is B (Gravestone doji, a warning that buyers failed and price closed back at the low): With the open, low and close together and a long upper shadow, the candle is a gravestone doji, and after an advance it shows buyers pushing up before sellers returned price to the open. A dragonfly doji is the mirror image with a long lower shadow. A long-legged doji needs shadows on both sides, and a shooting star has a small but visible body, which this candle lacks.
  2. The correct answer is C (Hold existing longs but wait to add until the Transports also close above their prior high.): Dow wanted both averages to confirm, and the Transports have not yet exceeded their prior high, so the sensible course is to hold and wait for confirmation. A lack of confirmation is a warning, not a bear signal. Confirmation applies throughout a trend, and Dow treated the closing price as the key price of the day.
  3. The correct answer is C (Distribution phase, with non-confirmation warning that the primary uptrend may be ending.): Euphoria, heavy volume, and a flood of new issues mark the third phase, when informed investors sell to the public. Dow required both averages to make new closing highs to confirm a bull trend, so the Transports lagging is non-confirmation and a warning. Accumulation happens when sentiment is poor and volume is light. One average making a high is not confirmation.
  4. The correct answer is A (Do not buy: the trigger fired but the trend filter failed, so conditions are not met.): A trigger starts the signal and a filter removes the occasions when the signal is not wanted, so both must be satisfied for a trade. The filter requires a rising 200-day average, and a flat or slightly falling one does not qualify. Nothing in the rule makes a failed filter a short signal or a reason for a partial position.
  5. The correct answer is C (Weak form: past prices and volume are already reflected, so chart analysis cannot earn excess returns): The weak form covers past prices and volume, which is what technical analysis uses. The semi-strong form extends to all public information, and the strong form adds private information. The distractors each shift a definition to the wrong form: semi-strong is not limited to past prices, the strong form includes private facts so insiders cannot beat it, and the weak form says nothing about private information.
  6. The correct answer is C (No, the three-day average already led yesterday, 46.33 against 45.20, so today only extends it): Today the five-day average is (44+46+45+48+52)/5 = 47.00 and the three-day average is (45+48+52)/3 = 48.33. Yesterday they were (43+44+46+45+48)/5 = 45.20 and (46+45+48)/3 = 46.33. The faster average was already above the slower one yesterday, so no new crossover occurred today. Comparing only today's values or the slope of one average misses that the order did not change.
  7. The correct answer is D (System B, with expectancy near 60 dollars per trade even though it wins less often.): Expectancy equals win rate times average win minus loss rate times average loss. System A gives 0.6 times 100 minus 0.4 times 150, which is 60 minus 60, or zero. System B gives 0.4 times 300 minus 0.6 times 100, which is 120 minus 60, or 60 dollars. A high win rate is tempting, but it does not offset a poor reward to risk ratio.
  8. The correct answer is A (Range 4.80 and net change of plus 1.60 from open to close): The range is the high minus the low: 66.00 minus 61.20 equals 4.80. The net change from open to close is 64.10 minus 62.50, which equals plus 1.60. The figure 3.50 is 66.00 minus 62.50, 2.90 is 64.10 minus 61.20, and 1.90 is 66.00 minus 64.10, so each wrong pair swaps in a different pair of prices.
  9. The correct answer is A (A common gap, which fills quickly and has little forecasting value.): A gap that appears within a trading range without news, and fills almost at once, is a common gap. Technicians give it little forecasting significance because it does not represent a change in the supply and demand balance. Breakaway gaps leave a range or pattern on heavy volume, runaway gaps appear in the middle of a trend, and exhaustion gaps appear near the end of an extended move.
  10. The correct answer is D (A secondary reaction inside the primary uptrend, lasting weeks to months, leaving the primary trend unchanged.): Secondary reactions last from about three weeks to several months and typically retrace one third to two thirds of the prior move. A five-week decline retracing half of a leg fits that description, and the intact rising pattern of peaks and troughs shows the primary uptrend remains. Minor movements are shorter than about three weeks. A retracement of this size alone does not signal a primary reversal.
  11. The correct answer is B (13,000, a rising line that confirms the index gain with broad participation): The daily net advance is 1,800 minus 1,200, or 600, and unchanged issues do not count. Adding that to 12,400 gives 13,000. A rising line alongside a rising index confirms broad participation. Using all advancers, including unchanged issues, or subtracting the net each produces a wrong value.
  12. The correct answer is D (Hedging, which locks in a selling price to reduce the risk of lower prices at harvest.): A producer who sells futures against a crop fixes the price in advance and offsets the risk of a falling market, which is the core hedging use. A speculator would hold the position without owning the crop, and would typically buy if expecting a rise. Arbitrage needs offsetting positions in two related prices. Futures pay no coupon or dividend.
  13. The correct answer is A (Find deviations from the mean, square and sum them, divide by n minus 1, then root.): The deviations from the mean always sum to zero, so they must be squared before they are added, which is why skipping the squaring step fails. After summing the squares, dividing by n minus 1 gives the sample variance, and the square root returns the units to those of the prices. Averaging absolute deviations produces the mean absolute deviation, a different measure.
  14. The correct answer is A (Compute +DM and -DM, form +DI and -DI, derive DX, then smooth DX into ADX.): Wilder first measures the upward and downward directional movement, scales smoothed values by true range to get +DI and -DI, then uses the gap between them relative to their sum to form DX. ADX is a smoothed average of DX and measures trend strength, not direction. The other orders reverse the dependencies or leave out the DX step.
  15. The correct answer is C (An r of negative 0.80 shows a weaker relationship than 0.50, because its sign is negative.): The sign of r gives direction and its size gives strength, so negative 0.80 is a stronger relationship than positive 0.50, only inverse. Squaring 0.70 gives 0.49, so about 49 percent of variation is explained statistically. Correlation does not establish cause. One extreme point can distort r noticeably, which is why a scatterplot should be inspected first.
  16. The correct answer is A (Left translation, a peak before the midpoint, signals a weak larger cycle and possibly lower lows.): A peak that arrives before the midpoint of the cycle is left translation, which Hurst linked to weakness in the next larger cycle and a higher chance that the following low breaks the prior one. Right translation is the reverse and describes a strong, upward-biased cycle. Nominality accepts that actual cycle lengths vary around a nominal period, so 31 to 33 weeks does not discard it. Summation says price is the sum of many cycles, not one.
  17. The correct answer is C (Arbitrage is limited by costs and noise trader risk, so rational traders cannot always correct mispricing): Behavioral finance explains the persistence through limits to arbitrage: shorting costs and noise trader risk stop rational traders from correcting the price. A correct forecast of earnings is the traditional claim and conflicts with the stem. Arbitrageurs may ride momentum in some cases, and loss aversion matters, but neither is the standard explanation.
  18. The correct answer is B (Its weekly returns spread more widely around the average, so outcomes vary more weekly.): Standard deviation measures how far observations typically sit from their mean, so it is a gauge of variability, not of direction or average level. Both funds share the same mean, so the difference is dispersion alone. ETF B's weekly results scatter roughly three times as widely. Greater dispersion may or may not be rewarded, and it says nothing about drift or smoothness.
  19. The correct answer is C (The closing price is the least important price of the day, since intraday extremes decide.): Dow treated the closing price as the most important price of the day, so calling it least important is not part of the theory. Confirmation by both averages, secondary reactions that retrace part of the move, and volume as supporting evidence are all standard Dow tenets that a quick reader might doubt.
  20. The correct answer is B (A squeeze of low volatility, followed by volatility expansion as the bands widen): Bollinger Bands are set two standard deviations from a 20 period average, so narrow bands mean low volatility. Bollinger's insight is that low volatility is followed by high volatility, and the close outside the band on a wide bar marks the expansion. Narrow bands are not high volatility, a close outside a band is not automatically a sell, and narrowing says nothing about the average failing.
  21. The correct answer is D (Accumulation follows the final bear low and starts a new advance; a bear rally fails.): Accumulation is the first phase of a new bull market, with informed buying after the final low, whereas a bear market rally is a secondary reaction that retraces some of the decline before the bear resumes. The other options swap the location, the participants, or the durations of the two ideas.
  22. The correct answer is C (About 1.618, the Golden Ratio, whose reciprocal of 0.618 is the central retracement ratio): Fifty-five divided by 34 is 1.6176, which rounds to 1.618. That quotient converges on the Golden Ratio as the sequence grows. Its reciprocal, 1 divided by 1.618, is 0.618, and the other common ratios are derived from it: 0.382 is 1 minus 0.618 and 0.236 comes from 0.618 squared times 0.618. Reversing the ratio and its reciprocal, or naming 0.382 as the reciprocal, is wrong.
  23. The correct answer is C (Leveraged exposure with a loss limited to the premium, since the buyer risks only that amount.): A long call buyer pays the premium up front, controls the shares with less money, and can lose no more than that premium. Premium income and assignment risk belong to the option seller. Time decay works against the buyer, and a call's delta is usually below one and changes with price.
  24. The correct answer is A (Minor ripples usually outlast secondary reactions and are used to define the primary trend): Dow described ripples as the shortest swings, lasting less than about three weeks, so they cannot last longer than secondary waves or define the primary trend. That makes the first statement the exception. The primary tide, the secondary wave of three weeks to three months, and the minor ripple treated as noise are all correct.
  25. The correct answer is C (The stock falls more slowly than the index, giving positive relative strength despite a weak trend.): A ratio line rises whenever the stock outperforms the benchmark, even if both fall. A stock that slips 4% while the index drops 10% has a rising ratio. The ratio is built only from the two prices, so it contains no volume information, and absolute and relative trends are separate questions.
  26. The correct answer is B (In a price-weighted index, the company with the largest market capitalization has the most influence.): A price-weighted index gives influence according to share price, so a high-priced stock counts more regardless of how large the company is, and the largest company by market capitalization does not automatically dominate. Capitalization weighting does let larger companies move the index more. Equal weighting assigns identical weights at rebalancing. The false statement is the one that credits market capitalization with driving a price-weighted index.
  27. The correct answer is B (Close the expiring contract and buy the next contract month, where liquidity is now concentrated.): A futures roll means closing the expiring contract and opening the same position in a later month. Open interest moving to the next month shows that is where traders and liquidity now sit. The exchange does not roll positions automatically, and a calendar spread or an unnecessary wait does not preserve the original exposure.
  28. The correct answer is C (Because r is negative, r squared is also negative at -0.36, showing an inverse explained share.): This asks for the false statement. R squared is the square of r, so (-0.60) squared is +0.36 and a squared value is never negative. The other three statements are true: r squared drops the sign so it cannot show direction, 1 minus 0.36 leaves 64 percent unexplained, and an imperfect correlation still allows days when both rise. Each can look wrong at first glance, but each is correct.
  29. The correct answer is D (Standard deviation is expressed in squared units of the data, the same way variance is.): This asks for the false statement. Variance is in squared units, but standard deviation is its square root and returns to the original units, such as index points or percent. The other three statements are true: the units match the data, equal means can hide very different dispersion, and a zero value means no spread at all. The false statement is the one that gives standard deviation squared units.
  30. The correct answer is D (About 74, projecting the 12 point rise from 50 to the gap midpoint further): A runaway or measuring gap tends to occur near the middle of a move, so the distance already covered is projected again. The gap midpoint is 62, the move from 50 is 12 points, and 62 plus 12 gives a target near 74. Doubling the projection gives 86, halving it gives 68, and adding price levels gives 112, which is not a distance at all.
  31. The correct answer is A (Harmonicity: neighboring cycle lengths are related by small whole-number ratios, usually two to one.): Harmonicity says that adjacent cycle lengths are related by a small whole-number ratio, most often two, which is exactly the 10, 20 and 40 week pattern. Summation concerns how cycles add together to produce the price line. Synchronicity concerns cycles of different lengths making lows together, and proportionality links a cycle's length to the size of its swings. Each is a genuine Hurst principle, but only harmonicity describes the ratio between lengths.
  32. The correct answer is C (The averages fail to confirm, so the Industrials' new high is suspect as a bull signal.): Dow theory asks both the Industrial and Transportation averages to exceed their prior peaks, so the Transports' 240 point shortfall means no confirmation. Non-confirmation is a warning, not a completed bear signal, which rules out calling a primary reversal. The Industrials alone cannot confirm anything, and a same-month rise is not the same as both averages making new highs.
  33. The correct answer is C (About 2.55, which rejects pure chance at the 5 percent level and challenges weak-form efficiency.): The excess is 118 minus 100, or 18 wins, and 18 divided by 7.07 gives about 2.55, beyond the 1.96 cutoff for a 5 percent level. Using 10 as the deviation gives 1.80 and dividing by the variance or the mean gives 0.36 and 0.18. A reliable edge challenges weak-form efficiency, though costs may erase it.
  34. The correct answer is A (Standard deviation volatility has dropped below ATR-based volatility, a squeeze that often precedes a larger move.): Bollinger Bands use the standard deviation of closes while Keltner Channels use ATR, which includes gaps and intraday ranges, so the two measure volatility differently. When the Bollinger Bands tighten inside the channels, close-to-close dispersion has fallen below true range volatility, which is the classic squeeze setup. A squeeze gives no direction by itself, and it does not mean the indicators measure the same thing or that price has stopped moving.
  35. The correct answer is A (The horizontal axis represents equal time units, so each column is one trading day.): Point-and-figure charts plot price against changes in direction, with no fixed time axis, so a column can span many days or only a few. A new column begins when price reverses by the reversal amount, here three boxes or $3. X columns record rising prices and O columns record falling prices. The statement that each column is one trading day is the exception.
  36. The correct answer is C (Few participants joined the move, so the breakout lacks buying conviction and may fail): A breakout is more reliable when volume expands, because heavy participation shows conviction. A move on volume well below average shows little demand behind it, so it may fail and drift back below 24.50. A close just above resistance is a small margin, light volume is not strength here, and the first day is the day that tests participation.
  37. The correct answer is C (Client pay tied to her work can create a conflict, so written employer consent is required): Standard IV(B) requires written consent from the employer before accepting compensation or benefits from outside parties for services related to her employment, because they can conflict with the employer's interests. The client being an existing one does not remove that. There is no small-amount exception, oral approval is not the written consent required, and the rule is not limited to competitors.
  38. The correct answer is D (Open interest rose 30,000 contracts, and tick counts stand in for currency volume): Open interest is the number of contracts still outstanding, and 430,000 minus 400,000 is a rise of 30,000. The 180,000 figure is traded volume, a different measure. Foreign exchange has no central exchange and no complete volume figure, so technicians often use tick counts as a stand-in. Treating ticks as an official volume, or placing currencies on a central exchange, misdescribes that market.
  39. The correct answer is A (Semi-strong form: public information is priced in, while nonpublic information can still earn profit.): The manager's failure to profit from public releases fits semi-strong efficiency, and the director's profits show private information is not fully priced, which rules out the strong form. Weak form is also consistent, but it is not the strongest form supported. Insider gains say nothing about whether past prices predict future ones, so they do not break the weak form.
  40. The correct answer is B (Short covering is driving the rally, since falling open interest means positions are closing.): Open interest counts contracts outstanding. When it falls during a price rise, traders are closing positions, and a rally driven by shorts buying back is less durable than one with new long money. Rising volume alone does not separate the two cases, and falling open interest means more contracts closed than opened.
  41. The correct answer is C (Rounded decline and recovery to the prior high, a small handle pullback, then a rim breakout.): A cup and handle starts with a rounded base where price falls and recovers to the earlier high, then forms a brief pullback called the handle before breaking out above the rim. The order matters because the handle comes after the cup. Skipping the handle, reversing the order or inverting the cup describes different formations.
  42. The correct answer is B (Stay out, because the broken support is now resistance and the rejection at D confirms it.): After support is broken, the old level tends to become resistance. The rally at C and the rejection at D show sellers defending that level, which confirms the role reversal and argues against rebuying. Assuming buyers will defend a level they just gave up ignores the break at B. A rally to a broken level is not a pullback in an uptrend, because the uptrend ended at the break. Two touches already give the signal.
  43. The correct answer is C (Extreme VIX readings often coincide with fear-driven market bottoms, so watch for price stabilization first.): The VIX is the 30-day volatility implied by S&P 500 option prices, and it tends to jump when fear peaks, which is why very high readings have often lined up with market bottoms. That makes the contrarian view the best planning base, while still waiting for the price chart to stabilize. Treating the spike as a forecast of continued decline mistakes a fear gauge for a trend signal. Calling the VIX a realized measure confuses it with historical volatility, and reading 38 as an expected annual drop misreads an annualized volatility figure as a return forecast.
  44. The correct answer is B (Herding, which means copying others instead of doing independent analysis.): The manager bought because rivals were profiting, which is imitation of others and therefore herding. Anchoring concerns starting values, overconfidence concerns self-assessed skill, and regret aversion concerns avoiding painful outcomes. Herding fits the stem's focus on following peers and the crowd.
  45. The correct answer is D (Set up automatic payroll contributions and separate accounts so saving occurs before spending choices come up.): Self-control bias is an emotional bias, so the advisor adapts the structure around the client instead of expecting willpower or knowledge to change her behavior. Automatic contributions remove the decision. Showing a shortfall and requesting promises both rely on the client resisting the urge, which is the very weakness. Taking more risk to offset low saving raises the chance of a bigger shortfall.
  46. The correct answer is A (VWAP weights each price by traded volume, so the heavy bar pulls it more.): VWAP is cumulative price times volume divided by cumulative volume, so a bar with heavy volume pulls it proportionally more. The 20 bar simple average gives each bar one equal weight whatever the volume. VWAP does not trim outliers or weight bars equally, and volume weighting does not belong to the simple average.
  47. The correct answer is D (A white real body of 2.30 with 0.40 shadows at each end, showing buyers in control): The real body runs from the open at 84.20 to the close at 86.50, which is 2.30, and the close above the open makes it white. The upper shadow is the high of 86.90 minus the close of 86.50, or 0.40, and the lower shadow is the open of 84.20 minus the low of 83.80, also 0.40. The 3.10 figure is the whole range, and 2.70 comes from measuring the upper shadow from the open.
  48. The correct answer is B (Either the market is inefficient or the model is wrong, and the test cannot say): The joint hypothesis problem means an efficiency test also tests the pricing model, so an abnormal return can reflect an inefficient market or a wrong model and the test cannot tell which. Beta error is only one possible model flaw, test type does not remove model dependence, and an abnormal return is defined against the model, not the market.
  49. The correct answer is A (High short interest is latent buying demand from covering, so contrarians see a potential bullish setup.): Short interest counts shares sellers must eventually buy back, so a very high reading is latent buying demand that can fuel a rally, which contrarians read as a potential bullish setup. The analyst treated shorts as reliable guides, the opposite of the contrarian reading. An 18% reading is high rather than normal, and a falling volume condition is not part of the theory.
  50. The correct answer is C (A sample, and the average is a statistic that estimates the population parameter.): The 3,000 issues are the population, and the 40 randomly chosen stocks are a sample drawn from it. A figure computed from a sample is a statistic and it estimates the population parameter, the average return of all 3,000. Being computed from real data does not make a statistic a parameter.
  51. The correct answer is C (Summation: coinciding troughs of several cycles added their pressures into one large decline.): Summation says the price at any time is the sum of every cycle acting together, so three lows landing in the same window pile their downward forces into one large drop. Nominality only says similar cycle lengths show up across markets and says nothing about decline size. Translation concerns where the peak sits within a cycle, not how lows combine. Harmonicity describes whole-number ratios between neighboring cycles and does not force deeper lows.
  52. The correct answer is A (Point changes depend on each index's level and divisor, so percentage changes should be compared.): A move of 400 points on 40,000 is 1 percent, and 40 points on 4,000 is also 1 percent, so the two indexes performed the same. Weighting methods and member counts differ and can tempt a candidate, but they do not change the basic problem that raw points depend on each index's level and divisor. Percentage changes put both on one scale, which is why they are the right comparison.
  53. The correct answer is A (Start from an initial reference value, then move toward new information, usually stopping short): Anchoring and adjustment starts from a reference value, here the old target, and moves toward new information but usually stops short. Overreaction does the opposite, regret driven revision is a different emotional pattern, and fitting a case to a typical past example is representativeness.
  54. The correct answer is D (A marginal close on light volume is not a decisive break, so reversal is unconfirmed.): Resistance can become support after a decisive violation, but a decisive break is judged by margin, time and volume, and a small one-day close on light volume does not qualify. Polarity works in both directions, and round numbers are often significant levels. Calling a close a false print denies the data instead of waiting for confirmation.
  55. The correct answer is C (A secondary reaction inside the primary downtrend, so wait for the rally to fail before buying): A four-week rally that recovers 37 percent of a decline matches Dow's secondary reaction: three weeks to three months and about one third to two thirds of the primary move. Being longer than a minor movement does not make it primary, and one third is the low end of the usual range, not a reversal trigger. It is also not a precise stopping point, so shorting at once is premature.
  56. The correct answer is D (The disposition effect, which stems from loss aversion and the wish to avoid realizing a loss.): The disposition effect is selling winners too early and holding losers too long. Loss aversion makes realizing a loss painful, so the trader postpones it, and fear of losing a gain pushes the quick sale of winners. Status quo bias would not explain the quick selling of winners, and the house money effect concerns taking more risk after gains.
  57. The correct answer is B (A martingale needs only zero expected change; a random walk also needs independent, identically distributed changes.): A martingale needs only zero expected change given the past, so volatility can vary. A random walk also needs independent and identically distributed changes, which fail when volatility rises after large down days. Normality is not required by either, and independence with identical distribution is the extra random walk condition.
  58. The correct answer is A (Multiply close minus prior average by two over eleven, then add to prior average.): The exponential average uses a multiplier of 2 divided by (period plus 1), which is 2 over 11 for ten periods. The new value equals the prior average plus the multiplier times the difference between the close and the prior average. A multiplier of one over ten belongs to Wilder smoothing, and recomputing a simple average first defeats the recursive design.
  59. The correct answer is B (Identify trend and level, await a confirmed breakout, set an ATR stop and size, then trail.): A disciplined sequence identifies the trend and the level, waits for a confirmed breakout, then sets an ATR based stop and sizes the position from that distance before trailing the stop. Entering at the level skips confirmation, sizing by past gains ignores volatility, and placing the stop after entry leaves risk undefined at the moment of commitment.
  60. The correct answer is B (Sparse trading makes chart patterns more reliable because fewer participants mean less noise): Sparse trading makes charts less reliable, not more, because a few orders dominate the prices and volume. Wide spreads, auction prints that hide tradable levels, and thin volume that confirms little are all true cautions, so each can tempt a reader who expects the false item to be technical rather than the reliability claim.
  61. The correct answer is C (The newest close gets a larger weight in the exponential average, so it reacts faster): An exponential average gives the newest close a larger weight than any older close, so a sudden gap moves it sooner, while the simple average gives each of fifty closes a one fiftieth weight. Both lengths are fixed at fifty days, so a shorter window is not the cause. The decay factor belongs to the exponential average, and its weights are not equal.
  62. The correct answer is D (A very low put/call ratio shows heavy fear, which contrarians read as a buying opportunity.): A low put/call ratio means few puts are being bought relative to calls, which signals complacency and optimism, so contrarians grow cautious. Calling it heavy fear reverses the meaning. The other three statements are standard: VIX spikes mark panic near lows, commercials are treated as the informed side in COT data, and lopsided bullish surveys warn contrarians.
  63. The correct answer is D (She is risk seeking in losses, as the convex loss side predicts, despite worse expected value): The gamble has an expected loss of 0.5 times 11,000, which is 5,500, larger than the sure 5,000. Choosing it anyway shows risk seeking in losses, the convex part of the prospect theory value function. Calling the gamble's expected loss smaller is an arithmetic error, and overconfidence is not what the theory predicts here.
  64. The correct answer is C (Munehisa Homma, an 18th century Japanese rice trader traditionally credited with it): Munehisa Homma, a rice trader in Japan in the 1700s, is traditionally credited with the price analysis from which candlesticks developed. Steve Nison is the tempting answer because he brought candlesticks to Western audiences in the 1990s, but that is popularization. Charles Dow shaped American trend analysis, and de la Vega wrote about the Amsterdam market, neither of which is the Japanese rice origin.
  65. The correct answer is D (Regret aversion: avoiding the pain of repeating a mistake keeps her holding, driven by feeling.): Her decision is driven by the emotional sting of the earlier sale and a wish never to feel it again, which is regret aversion. The flaw is that fundamentals, not feelings, should decide whether to hold. Overconfidence would show in forecasts, endowment bias in valuing owned shares, and status quo bias would not hinge on the earlier sale.
  66. The correct answer is D (Abnormal returns cannot be earned consistently after risk and costs; one good year can be luck.): The Efficient Markets Hypothesis says excess returns cannot be earned consistently after risk and costs, so one good year can be luck and does not refute it. It does not predict identical results for every manager, and a single year of public data success is not a weak form failure.
  67. The correct answer is A (Fear peaks when most holders have already sold, leaving few sellers, so prices can turn upward): Contrarians reason that when fear is widespread, most who wanted to sell already have, leaving few sellers and making a rebound likely. Insider selling and fair value are not the logic, and a VIX level is a warning of extremes rather than an exact bottom. Waiting for sentiment to turn neutral abandons the contrarian view that extremes are the signal.
  68. The correct answer is A (Sellers pushed price sharply lower, but buyers rejected those lows and closed near the open.): A long lower shadow with a small body means price traded far lower during the session but recovered. That shows buyers absorbed the selling and rejected the lows, which is why such candles near a decline are watched as possible turning points. A flat balance reading overlooks the rejection. Treating the shadow as a gap or thin trading misreads how the candle forms.
  69. The correct answer is D (More than two standard deviations below the mean, outside the central 95 percent range): The z-score is (minus 1.1 minus 0.02) divided by 0.50, about minus 2.24. That is more than two standard deviations from the mean, so the move lies outside the central 95 percent band of the Empirical Rule. It is not past three standard deviations, which would need a drop of more than about 1.48 percent. A one-sigma reading would call it typical, which it was not.
  70. The correct answer is C (Spikes often mark panic lows, but the VIX shows fear, not a bottom, so await confirmation): The VIX is the 30-day expected volatility of the S&P 500 implied by SPX option prices, quoted as an annualized percentage. High readings often cluster near panic lows, but they are not a timing signal on their own, so confirmation from price is prudent. It is not a forecast of a 38 percent decline and it is not realized volatility.
  71. The correct answer is D (The euro strengthened against the dollar, since one euro now buys more dollars): In a pair quote, the first currency is the base and the quote shows how many units of the second currency buy one unit of the base. A rise from 1.0850 to 1.0950 means one euro buys more dollars, so the euro gained against the dollar. A pair is a relative price, so one currency's gain is the other's loss.
  72. The correct answer is B (Doubling the loss gives a felt value of 0, below $40, so loss aversion explains it): Expected value is 0.5 x 200 + 0.5 x (-100) = $50, but the client weights losses twice as heavily: 0.5 x 200 + 0.5 x (-200) = 100 - 100 = 0. A felt value of 0 is below the sure $40, so the choice is consistent with loss aversion. The $50 figure ignores the weighting, $150 doubles the wrong side, and minus $100 forgets the 50 percent probability on the loss.
  73. The correct answer is C ($5.50, the distance from the prior close to today's high, the largest of the three.): The three measures are high minus low, which is 3.50; high minus prior close, which is 5.50; and prior close minus low taken as an absolute value, which is 2.00. Wilder's true range is the greatest of these, so it is 5.50. The gap makes the true range larger than the bar's own range.
  74. The correct answer is B (Framing means the wording of a choice has no effect when the payoffs are identical.): Framing means identical payoffs described as gains or losses lead to different choices, so wording does matter and the statement denying that is false. Anchoring from arbitrary numbers, availability favoring striking events, and mental accounting of dividends are all accurate, though their detail may make a reader suspect them.
  75. The correct answer is A (A tests support, B breaks it, C rallies back to the level, D is rejected.): The polarity principle says a support level that is violated tends to become resistance. The sequence is a test of support, a break below it, a rally that returns to the broken level, and a rejection there. The sequence with a breakout and resumption describes a throwback in an uptrend instead, and the other options scramble or invert the events.
  76. The correct answer is A (The index fund, since public information analysis cannot be expected to cover the extra 1.1 percent): If prices already reflect all public information, an active manager analyzing public data has no expected edge, so the extra 1.1 percent in fees is a drag, favoring the low-cost index fund. Reading news quickly does not help once prices are already adjusted, and semi-strong efficiency also covers past price patterns. Fees matter regardless of what kind of information is used.
  77. The correct answer is C (The price-weighted index falls about 8 percent, because P's high share price carries most weight.): Price weights are 200 of 250, or 80 percent for P, so a 10 percent fall in P cuts that index by 8 percent. Cap weights give P only 20 of 300 billion, about 6.7 percent, so that index falls roughly 0.67 percent, not 6.7 percent. An equal weighted index gives P one third, a drop near 3.3 percent. Mixing up which weighting uses share price, value or equal shares produces each tempting wrong figure.
  78. The correct answer is B (Rational investors respond differently to the two phrasings because the underlying outcomes differ.): The two brochures describe one outcome, so a rational investor would respond identically, which makes the claim of different rational responses false. The other statements correctly describe that framing can reverse preferences and that gain wording feels safer, even though the facts are the same.
  79. The correct answer is B (The median resists extreme values, so it shows a typical trade better than the mean.): One 400,000 share trade pulls the mean far above where most trades sit, while the median depends on the middle ranked value and is barely moved. The median does not use the sizes of every value, which is why it resists outliers. It is not the most frequent value (the mode), and both measures can be used on samples and populations.
  80. The correct answer is C (Gains and losses measured from a reference point, with a steeper slope for losses): Prospect theory defines value over changes from a reference point, concave for gains, convex for losses, and steeper for losses (loss aversion). The option describing final wealth with steady concavity is classic expected utility. The option with a reference point but equal steepness removes the asymmetry, which is the theory's central point, and the option about linear probabilities mixes value with the separate probability weighting function, which is nonlinear rather than linear.
  81. The correct answer is A (2.25, because the new price sum of 180 divided by 80 gives the divisor.): After the split the prices are 60, 80 and 40, which sum to 180. To keep the average at 80, the divisor must be 180 divided by 80, or 2.25. Leaving the divisor at 3 would drop the average to 60, an artificial fall caused by the split. Charles Dow created the averages, and the divisor adjustment is how the Industrial Average handles splits.
  82. The correct answer is B (Seed with a 10-day simple average, multiplier 2 over 11, then update from the prior value.): The standard exponential average starts from the simple average of the first ten closes. The multiplier is 2 divided by (10 + 1), about 0.1818, and each new value equals the close minus the prior average, times the multiplier, plus the prior average. A multiplier of 1 over 10 belongs to Wilder smoothing, and linear weights belong to the linearly weighted average. Recomputing a simple average each day removes the recursion that defines the exponential method.
  83. The correct answer is C (Signals lag the turning points, and the interpretation of confirmation is subjective.): The skeptic raises two standard criticisms: confirmation comes after much of the move, and analysts can read confirmation differently. Each other pair keeps one true idea and adds a false one, such as needing earnings data, early signals, or no discretion, which Dow Theory does not involve.
  84. The correct answer is D (About 2.10, the new price sum of 140 divided by the unchanged 66.67 level.): After the split the prices are 60, 60 and 20, summing to 140. To keep the level at 66.67 the divisor must be 140 / 66.67 = 2.10. Leaving it at 3.00 would drop the level to 46.67, which is the artificial change the adjustment prevents, and the 1.50 and 2.33 answers use the wrong targets.
  85. The correct answer is B (Volume stays modest within the range, then expands at the breakout, showing strong participation.): A healthy breakout is usually preceded by quiet trading inside the range and followed by a surge in volume as new buyers commit. That expansion shows participation behind the move. A breakout on shrinking volume is suspect rather than confirmed. A sequence that raises volume in the range and lowers it at the breakout runs opposite to the normal pattern.
  86. The correct answer is D (Weak form, because only past market data are already reflected in current prices.): The weak form says past prices and volume are already in current prices, so technical analysis based on them cannot earn abnormal returns, while fundamental analysis still might. The semi-strong form extends this to all public information and the strong form to private information too. The Adaptive Markets view is an alternative to efficiency, not one of its forms.
  87. The correct answer is D (Variance carries the same units as the returns, so it reads as a percentage.): The false statement is the one about units: variance is the average squared deviation, so its units are squared percentage points and it cannot be read as a percentage. Taking the square root gives the standard deviation, which does return to the original units. The 1.5 times IQR rule and the meaning of a zero z-score are both standard and true.
  88. The correct answer is B (Wilder smoothing, which acts like a 27-period exponential average and lags more.): Wilder smoothing adds one fourteenth of the new value to thirteen fourteenths of the prior average, which is a multiplier of 1/14 rather than the 2/15 used by a standard 14-period exponential average. That makes it equivalent to an exponential average of roughly 27 periods, so it reacts more slowly. A simple average uses a fixed window, and a linearly weighted average uses declining integer weights.
  89. The correct answer is C (Keltner Channel width is set by the standard deviation of closing prices, as Bollinger width is.): This is a NOT true question. Bollinger Bands use a multiple of the standard deviation of closes around a simple moving average, while Keltner Channels use a multiple of the average true range around an exponential average. So the claim that both rely on standard deviation is false. The other statements correctly describe each tool, and ATR is why Keltner width reflects gaps.
  90. The correct answer is D (A nonlinear link is ruled out, since an r near zero means the series are unrelated.): Pearson's r measures only straight-line association, so a curved or U-shaped relationship can sit alongside an r near zero. The other conclusions follow directly: 0.05 squared is 0.0025, little is explained, and forecasts would be poor. The unsupported conclusion is that a near-zero r proves there is no relationship of any kind.
  91. The correct answer is A (Identify the trader group, compare its net position with historical extremes, then wait for price confirmation.): A common approach is to select the trader group, measure its net position against past extremes, and treat the extreme as a condition that needs price confirmation. Acting on the extreme alone risks being early, and confirming first removes the point of an extreme reading. Commercials are typically followed at extremes while small traders are the group usually faded.
  92. The correct answer is A (A drop to 45 is required; smaller moves are filtered as noise, isolating real supply shifts.): A new column of Os starts only when price falls by the reversal amount, here three boxes of 1 point, so a drop from 48 to 45. The low of 46 fell just two boxes, so it is treated as noise. This filtering is the purpose of the method: it ignores time and small moves and plots only price changes large enough to reflect a real shift in supply and demand. Closing-only recording, a weekly time requirement and volume confirmation are not part of how the columns are built.
  93. The correct answer is B (The disposition effect, selling the winner early while holding the loser until it returns to breakeven.): Selling the winner while holding the loser to reach breakeven is the textbook disposition effect, driven by loss aversion and regret about realizing a loss. Mental accounting is a defensible partner idea because the positions are viewed separately, but the stated breakeven target is the specific clue. House money would have her gamble with the gain, and confirmation bias is not described in her statement.
  94. The correct answer is C (The 680 point pullback is about 57 percent of the advance, a normal secondary reaction.): The advance is 5,200 minus 4,000, or 1,200 points, and the pullback is 5,200 minus 4,520, or 680 points. 680 divided by 1,200 is 0.567, inside the one-third to two-thirds band of 400 to 800 points. That is consistent with a secondary reaction within a continuing primary trend. A reversal would need a retracement beyond that band and confirming signals.
  95. The correct answer is D (Prices now spread more widely around their 20-day average, so volatility has increased.): Standard deviation measures how far observations typically sit from their mean, so a rise from 1.2 to 3.4 dollars means wider dispersion and higher volatility. It carries no information about direction, which is why an uptrend reading fails, and a steady average does not mean calm trading. Variance is the square of the deviation, so it rose from about 1.4 to about 11.6, far more than the deviation itself.
  96. The correct answer is C (Selling climax, panic liquidation that often marks a low, mirroring a blow-off top): A selling climax is a burst of panic selling on extreme volume at the end of a decline, and it often marks a low. It mirrors the blow-off top, which ends an advance. Heavy volume on ordinary declines confirms a downtrend, but an extreme session followed by a sharp reversal is a climax, not a breakout or simple confirmation.
  97. The correct answer is B (Tighten the stop or trim the position, since bearish divergence warns that upward momentum is fading): A higher price high with a lower RSI high is bearish divergence: momentum is not confirming the new price. It is a warning, not a confirmed reversal, so tightening risk fits better than adding or exiting everything. Divergence can occur without RSI reaching 70, so waiting for an overbought level is unnecessary.
  98. The correct answer is A (Treat the spike as possible capitulation, but wait for price confirmation like a higher low.): Sharp VIX spikes often occur near market bottoms because panic and capitulation selling push option demand to extremes. The spike is a warning that fear may be near a peak, not a precise timing signal, because the VIX has stayed high for weeks while prices kept falling. Buying the full position at once ignores that limit. Waiting for a return to 20 usually means missing much of the rebound, and ignoring the VIX discards a useful fear gauge.
  99. The correct answer is C (The stock is outperforming the benchmark, since its price rose along with the benchmark): Both prices rising does not mean outperformance. The ratio line falls when the stock gains less than the benchmark, which signals relative weakness. The other statements are standard: managers prefer rising ratio lines, and absolute and relative trends can diverge.
  100. The correct answer is C (Tests of return predictability, which examine whether past returns and other variables forecast future returns): In his revised scheme Fama replaced weak-form tests with tests of return predictability, which include past returns and other forecasting variables. Event studies took the place of semi-strong form tests and tests for private information replaced strong-form tests. The joint hypothesis problem is the general limitation that applies to all of these tests, not one of the three categories.
  101. The correct answer is B (The predicted return is 3.0 percent, but r squared of 0.35 leaves most movement unexplained.): Substituting 2 percent gives 0.4 plus 1.3 times 2, or 3.0 percent. An r squared of 0.35 means the index explains only 35 percent of the stock's movement, so 65 percent is unexplained and the forecast is rough. The slope measures sensitivity, not reliability, and the intercept belongs in the equation.
  102. The correct answer is A (Alert at 62.92, the 100 high less 61.8 percent of the 60 point advance): The advance is 100 minus 40, or 60 points. A 61.8 percent retracement is 0.618 times 60, or 37.08 points, and subtracting that from the high of 100 gives 62.92. The 77.08 level is the 38.2 percent retracement (22.92 points down), the 70.00 level is the 50 percent midpoint, and 137.08 is a 161.8 percent extension projected above the swing, which is a profit target rather than a pullback level.
  103. The correct answer is D (Disposition effect, with a 500 dollar combined gain: 1,500 on B less the 1,000 loss.): Stock A is down 10 points on 100 shares, a 1,000 dollar loss, and stock B is up 15 points, a 1,500 dollar gain, so the net is 500 dollars. Selling the winner while holding the loser to avoid realizing a loss is the disposition effect. House money describes risk taking after gains, and the other numbers drop a leg or add magnitudes.
  104. The correct answer is C (Falling open interest near expiration usually reflects positions closing or rolling, so it is not bearish): Open interest counts outstanding contracts, and it declines naturally as an expiration date nears because traders close out or roll their positions into the next month. Used alone, that decline says little about direction. The other readings treat a routine expiry effect as a trend signal or as a reason to abandon the chart, though a technician would typically shift attention to the next contract month.
  105. The correct answer is A (Candlestick charting, which Steve Nison later introduced to Western traders in the early 1990s): The records of open, high, low and close with a shaded body and thin shadows describe candlestick charting, brought west by Steve Nison. Renko ignores time and uses bricks, Heikin Ashi smooths bars with averages, and bar charts record the same prices without a body and are not tied to Osaka rice trading.
  106. The correct answer is C (Cycle lengths vary around the nominal, so the next low may come early or late.): Hurst's principle of variation says actual cycle lengths deviate around the nominal period, so a low expected in four weeks may arrive early or late, and the date is better treated as a window. Cycle lengths are not fixed, a low is a normal measuring point, and no standard ten percent lag exists in cycle analysis.
  107. The correct answer is B (The weak form holds but the semi-strong form does not; Eugene Fama formalized the hypothesis.): Weak form efficiency says past prices and volume carry no exploitable information, which matches the first finding. Semi-strong efficiency says public information is already in prices, so excess returns from statements contradict it. Fama formalized the hypothesis, while Markowitz is known for portfolio theory and Malkiel for popularizing the random walk view.
  108. The correct answer is A (A gain of 500 dollars, since the dollar is the quote currency.): In a pair, the first currency is the base and the second the quote, and profit is expressed in the quote currency. The move from 1.0850 to 1.0900 is 0.0050, or 50 pips. On 100,000 euros, 0.0050 times 100,000 equals 500 dollars, which is 10 dollars per pip. Buying the base currency gains when the pair rises.
  109. The correct answer is A (Supports the semi-strong form but contradicts the strong form, since private data lags): The semi-strong form says prices reflect all public information, and the finding that public earnings releases offer no excess returns agrees with it. The strong form adds private information, and insiders profiting from it contradicts that form. Public releases yielding nothing does not contradict the semi-strong form. Insider gains show private data is not fully reflected, so they cannot support the strong form, and they do not reject the other two forms.
  110. The correct answer is B (The value function is concave for losses, so people grow risk averse facing a loss): The value function is convex for losses, so people tend to seek risk to avoid a sure loss, and the claim of concavity for losses is false. Concavity for gains, overweighting of small probabilities and shifting reference points are all part of prospect theory, though each can look surprising.
  111. The correct answer is B (A 70 percent win rate means positive expectancy whatever the average win and loss sizes.): Expectancy depends on win rate and on the size of wins and losses together, so a 70 percent win rate can still lose money if losses are large. A 50 percent loss needs a 100 percent gain to recover. Fixed small risk per trade reduces the chance of ruin. At 3 to 1, a 35 percent win rate gives 0.35 times 3 minus 0.65 times 1, or positive 0.40R per trade.
  112. The correct answer is C (Consider a long entry on the breakout with a stop back inside the bands.): A squeeze shows volatility at a low, and a close outside the bands after it points to the start of a directional move, so a long with a stop back inside the bands matches the signal. Fading the move as overbought applies a range-market idea to a breakout. Waiting for widening or a pullback to the middle band misses the entry, because the bands widen as the move begins.
  113. The correct answer is D (Confirmation needs a close below the $52 low between the peaks, so shorting now is premature.): A double top is confirmed only when price closes below the trough between the two peaks, here $52. Price at $53.50 has not done that, so the pattern is incomplete and the short is premature. The $44 target is correct once confirmed, since the height of 8 is subtracted from the $52 confirmation level. A double top is a reversal pattern, and adding the height would project upward.
  114. The correct answer is B (Anchoring and adjustment: she fixes on 60 and adjusts too little for the new evidence.): Anchoring and adjustment describes starting from a reference number and moving too little from it when facts change; here the purchase price of 60 is the anchor. Confirmation bias involves selective searching, which is not described. Mental accounting concerns separating positions, and availability would push her to forecast further declines, the opposite of what she is doing.
  115. The correct answer is D (Expected utility judges final wealth; prospect theory judges gains and losses from a reference point.): Expected utility evaluates final wealth, while prospect theory evaluates gains and losses from a reference point, weights losses more heavily and transforms probabilities. Each other statement misplaces loss aversion, probability treatment or curve shape between the two frameworks.
  116. The correct answer is A (An exhaustion gap, late in the move and quickly filled, warns the advance is ending.): An exhaustion gap appears near the end of a long move, often on heavy volume, and is closed within a few sessions. This stock had already risen from 20 to 55 over eight months, and it closed below the gap two sessions later. A breakaway gap launches a new trend from a base. A runaway gap in mid-trend normally stays open, and a common gap occurs inside a range, not after an extended advance.
  117. The correct answer is D (An ascending triangle, a continuation pattern expected to resolve with a breakout above $42.): Equal highs at $42 form a flat top, and the rising pullback lows form an ascending lower trendline, which together make an ascending triangle. After an advance and with shrinking volume it is a continuation pattern, resolving upward on a breakout with rising volume. A rectangle needs flat support, a rising wedge needs both lines rising, and a double top has only two peaks with a deeper middle pullback.
  118. The correct answer is A (A gain of $900, which is 18 points multiplied by the $50 contract multiplier.): Profit on a futures contract equals the price change in points times the contract multiplier: 18 x $50 = $900. The index level of 4,800 does not enter the profit calculation, though 4,800 x $50 = $240,000 is the notional value. Using one dollar per point ignores contract size, and a $250 multiplier belongs to a different contract.
  119. The correct answer is A (New buyers and new sellers opened fresh positions, creating additional outstanding contracts): Open interest counts outstanding contracts, and it rises only when a new buyer and a new seller both open positions. When a long and a short both close, open interest falls, and when a position passes from one trader to another it stays the same. Volume counts every contract traded, so higher volume alone does not raise open interest.
  120. The correct answer is B (Measure from the head's low to the neckline and add that distance at the breakout.): The measuring rule takes the vertical distance from the head to the neckline and projects it from the breakout point on the neckline, upward for an inverse pattern. Subtracting it suits a top, and starting from the head's low gives a target that lies below the breakout. The width between shoulders is not the measure used.
  121. The correct answer is A (41,910, found by adding the net advancing issues of 610 to yesterday's line): The cumulative advance-decline line adds each session's advances minus declines to the running total. Here that is 1,820 minus 1,210, or 610, and 41,300 plus 610 equals 41,910. Adding the 270 unchanged issues gives 42,180, subtracting the net gives 40,690, and adding only advances gives 43,120, each resting on a nameable mistake.
  122. The correct answer is D (Volume usually expands steadily through a flag, then dries up when price breaks out.): Continuation patterns show shrinking volume while price consolidates and a pickup in volume on the breakout, which is the reverse of the statement about flags. The other statements are standard: flags and pennants are short formations after sharp moves, triangles show contracting volume until the breakout, and the cup and handle is bullish with a shallow handle. The false statement misplaces both the rise and the dry-up in volume.
  123. The correct answer is A (Piercing line, a bullish reversal because buyers recovered more than half of the prior body): A piercing line is a white candle that opens below the prior low in a downtrend and closes more than halfway up the prior black body without exceeding its open. Because the close stays below that open, it is not an engulfing pattern, and because it opens below the prior low, it is not a harami. Dark cloud cover is the bearish mirror image and forms after an advance.
  124. The correct answer is D (Unadjusted data records the split as a false 50 percent crash, distorting trendlines and averages.): A 2 for 1 split halves the share price with no change in the company's value, so the raw history shows a 50 percent drop that never happened to holders. Adjusting divides earlier prices by 2, which keeps the series continuous. The other options reverse the adjustment, claim value changed, or say the raw data hides gaps when it creates one.
  125. The correct answer is A (Close above 15,610, a rise of more than 70 points from today's close): Confirmation means the second average also exceeds its own prior closing high: 15,610 - 15,540 = 70, so the Transports must close above 15,610. Matching the Industrials' 270 points gives 15,810, and matching their 0.71 percent (270 / 38,150) gives about 15,650, but Dow Theory compares each average to its own prior high. There is no two-close rule.
  126. The correct answer is C (An emotional bias, which education corrects poorly, so the plan should be built to accommodate it): The client knows the analysis was wrong, so the problem is not missing information but a feeling about selling, which marks an emotional bias. Emotional biases come from feelings and resist correction by education, so advisors usually adapt the plan to them. Cognitive errors from reasoning or memory respond better to training and records, which the failed sessions suggest is not the case here.
  127. The correct answer is D (Anchoring and adjustment, because he fixes on the old high and judges value against it): His judgment of value starts from the 52 week high and adjusts too little, which is anchoring. He buys rather than holds, so the disposition effect does not fit. The stem does not describe a search for supporting evidence or a comparison to past rebounders either, though both biases are real.
  128. The correct answer is D (The weak and semi-strong forms hold, but the strong form is rejected by the insider results): Past prices and public news earning nothing is consistent with the weak and semi-strong forms. Insiders profiting from private information contradicts only the strong form, which says all information including private is reflected. Insider gains say nothing against public information being priced, a single failure does not reject the other forms, and insiders profiting is the evidence that the strong form fails.
  129. The correct answer is B (Point C, the third touch, validates the line; D warns the trend may weaken.): Two points define a trendline and a third touch, point C, validates it by showing the line was respected again. A close through the line at D warns the trend may weaken, but a single violation does not confirm a reversal. A second touch only completes the line, and a close through it is not a buying signal.
  130. The correct answer is A (62, found by adding the 12 point flagpole to the breakout at 50): The flagpole runs from 40 to 52, so it is 12 points. The measuring rule adds that distance to the breakout point of the flag: 50 plus 12 equals 62. Adding it to 52 gives 64 and overstates the move. The prior high of 52 is only the first resistance, and the flag depth of 4 is not the measuring distance.
  131. The correct answer is A (MACD subtracts a 26-period EMA from a 12-period EMA and signals with another 26-period EMA.): The MACD signal line is a 9-period exponential average of the MACD line, not a 26-period average, so the keyed statement is false. Rate of change does compare the current close with an earlier close. A bearish divergence is a higher price high with a lower oscillator high. On-balance volume adds or subtracts the full session volume according to the close direction.
  132. The correct answer is D (True range is 3 and the new ATR is about 2.07, using Wilder's smoothing method.): True range is the greatest of high minus low (2), absolute high minus prior close (3) and absolute low minus prior close (1), so it is 3. Wilder's update is (2.00 times 13 plus 3) divided by 14, which is 29 divided by 14, or 2.07. Averaging only two numbers ignores the 13 prior periods. Taking the smallest measure misses the gap entirely.
  133. The correct answer is A (Breakaway, then runaway, then an exhaustion gap marking the likely end of the advance): The gap out of a long base on heavy volume is a breakaway gap, the mid-advance gap is a runaway gap, and the late gap on very heavy volume that fails to hold is an exhaustion gap. Labeling the base gap common ignores that common gaps occur inside trading ranges. Putting exhaustion in the middle or runaway first reverses the natural life cycle of a trend.
  134. The correct answer is D (Retest after trading costs on small illiquid stocks and across non-January months first.): The sensible step is to retest net of trading costs on small illiquid stocks and across the calendar before committing capital. Learning from live trading is costly, large stocks may not show the same returns, and a January only plan rests on an untested explanation of a result that may be a cost or seasonal artifact.
  135. The correct answer is D (60 a year, and the bond sells at a premium since price exceeds face value.): The coupon payment is the coupon rate times face value, so 6 percent of 1,000 is 60 a year, and it does not change when the price moves. A bond trading above face value, here 1,200 against 1,000, sells at a premium. Multiplying the coupon rate by the 1,200 price gives 72, which is not the payment. A discount means the price is below face value.
  136. The correct answer is B (The law of large numbers: sample averages converge toward the true mean as independent observations grow): With only 10 trades, a 60 percent win rate is well within random variation. As trades accumulate, the law of large numbers pulls the sample average toward the true 50 percent. The central limit theorem describes the shape of the distribution of sample means, not forcing a value. Independent trades do not owe losses after wins.
  137. The correct answer is D (No, since the transport close of 15,050 is 150 points under its prior close of 15,200): Dow Theory treats the closing price as the most important price, so confirmation requires both averages to close above their prior closing highs. The transports closed 15,200 minus 15,050, or 150 points, below the mark. The intraday spike to 15,260 does not count, there is no closeness tolerance, and the industrial gain of 500 points (about 1.4 percent) is irrelevant because Dow set no minimum percentage.
  138. The correct answer is D (Variation: real cycle lengths drift around the nominal period, so a 33-week low is not failure): Hurst's principle of variation says actual cycle lengths and amplitudes wander around their nominal values, so a low arriving a few weeks early is expected and does not by itself cancel the cycle. Summation describes how cycles add together, harmonicity links neighboring cycles by a small whole number, and proportionality ties amplitude to wavelength. None of these says a shorter interval invalidates the 40-week cycle.
  139. The correct answer is A (An edge is found, traders exploit it, competition erodes the profit, and participants seek new edges): Under the Adaptive Markets Hypothesis an edge appears, is exploited, erodes as competition grows, and participants then adapt by seeking new edges, so efficiency varies over time. Permanent efficiency, lasting secrecy and a return to the old strategy each miss the adaptive step that restarts the cycle.
  140. The correct answer is B (Sentiment and herding can push prices away from fundamental value, so the gap may persist.): Behavioral finance argues that sentiment and herd behavior can keep prices away from value, and that arbitrage is limited and risky. The traditional view treats rational agents as setting prices to value, which is the first alternative. The other options assume that professionals can profit safely or quickly from the gap, which behavioral finance disputes.
  141. The correct answer is D (Fewer buyers are driving each advance; momentum is fading, which warns of a possible top.): Price made a higher high while RSI made a lower high, a bearish divergence. It shows that each advance is supported by weaker momentum, meaning fewer participants are pushing price higher. That is a warning that the advance may be ending, not proof that it has. A reading under 70 does not cancel the divergence, an immediate short ignores the need for confirmation, and divergence is an early warning rather than a lagging signal.
  142. The correct answer is B (The 3% rule: a close above 51.50 confirms the breakout.): The 3% rule requires a close beyond the level by 3% to screen out false breakouts. Three percent of 50.00 is 1.50, so the confirming close is above 51.50. The ATR filter would use the stock's own volatility, giving 50.90 here, and the time filter counts sessions instead of distance. Three points above 50 is a 6% move, not 3%, which is the arithmetic slip behind the 53.00 choice.
  143. The correct answer is A (Non-confirmation is a warning, but the primary uptrend stands until both averages signal a definite reversal.): Dow required the two averages to confirm each other, so a lagging Transportation average is a caution. His other tenet is that a trend persists until a definite reversal signal, and that needs both averages. The colleague confuses warning with signal. The other options stretch the discounting and volume tenets in ways Dow did not.

Practice Test 7 Answer Key

  1. The correct answer is D (The 1.8% is historical volatility, and the 2.6% is implied volatility taken from option prices.): Historical volatility is calculated from past returns, such as the 30 session standard deviation of 1.8%. Implied volatility is backed out of current option prices and reflects expected future movement, here 2.6%. Option prices do contain some influence from recent history, which makes the extrapolation view tempting, but they price future expectations. The VIX applies to S&P 500 options, so it does not describe one stock's options.
  2. The correct answer is A (Price paused above the 0.382 line, so a bounce is plausible, with 1.0800 below.): The swing is 0.0800, so 0.382 times 0.08 is about 0.0306 and the line sits near 1.0894. Price at 1.0900 is just above that line, which is a shallow retracement, and the 0.5 line at 1.0800 is the next level below. A retracement line suggests an area where buyers may appear, but it does not forecast a particular extension target.
  3. The correct answer is B (Herding, because imitation by many investors creates persistent trends that show up as chart patterns.): Herding explains the euphoric imitation and fear of missing out in the stem, and the persistent trends it creates leave patterns that a technician can trade. The disposition effect restrains rallies, conservatism fits slow drift but not euphoria, and representativeness does not account for the crowd copying described.
  4. The correct answer is C (About 50 days; observing 90 suggests fatter tails than normal, so the model understates extreme moves.): The Empirical Rule puts about 95 percent of values within two standard deviations, leaving about 5 percent, or 50 of 1,000 days, outside. Seeing 90 such days is nearly double the expectation and points to fat tails, which a normal model understates. The 320 figure comes from the 68 percent band and the 3 figure from the 99.7 percent band. Treating 90 as noise is the tempting second reading, but a persistent excess is evidence against the model.
  5. The correct answer is B (Expectancy is plus $60 per trade, but 10% risk per trade makes a deep drawdown likely.): Expectancy is 0.40 times $900 minus 0.60 times $500, which is $360 minus $300, or plus $60 per trade. The edge is thin, though. Six losses in a row are not unusual with a 60% loss rate, and risking 10% each time would remove roughly half the account. Positive expectancy does not protect against ruin when position size is too large.
  6. The correct answer is C (Assign a colleague to argue the bear case, record contrary evidence, and review preset exit criteria): The manager is showing confirmation bias, so the fix is a process that forces contrary evidence into the decision, such as a designated devil's advocate and preset exit rules. Selling everything on two misses swings to the opposite error, and adding to the position acts on conviction instead of evidence. Waiting another quarter leaves the filtering in place, and he has already seen two misses, not one.
  7. The correct answer is A (A long-legged doji, because the body is only 0.0001 and both shadows are far longer.): The real body is 1.0851 minus 1.0850 = 0.0001, essentially a doji. The upper shadow is 1.0910 minus 1.0851 = 0.0059 and the lower shadow is 1.0850 minus 1.0790 = 0.0060, so both shadows are long and nearly equal, which defines a long-legged doji. A dragonfly doji has the open and close at the high, a gravestone has them at the low, and a spinning top has a visible body.
  8. The correct answer is D (The default middle band is a 20-period exponential average, with bands two ATRs away.): Default Bollinger Bands use a 20-period simple moving average as the middle band and place the outer bands two standard deviations away, not two average true ranges from an exponential average; the true-range version describes Keltner Channels. A squeeze marks low volatility that often leads to expansion, the bands widen as standard deviation rises, and a close outside a band can start a band walk rather than a reversal.
  9. The correct answer is B (Loss aversion, an emotional bias where realizing a loss hurts more than an equal gain pleases.): Her stated reason is the pain of making the loss real, which is the core of loss aversion. Anchoring on 48 is a defensible reading, but it would be a cognitive error expressed through a value estimate, and she gives no estimate. Representativeness and overconfidence need a belief about the future, which her statement does not contain.
  10. The correct answer is B (62, found by adding the 12-point flagpole to the breakout point at 50.): The flagpole is 52 minus 40, or 12 points. The usual rule adds that distance to the breakout from the flag, 50 plus 12, giving 62. Projecting from the pole's top gives 64, a retest target ignores the measuring rule, and doubling the pole gives 74, which has no basis in the pattern.
  11. The correct answer is A (Buy at A, sell at B, buy at C, then sell at D, alternating each crossover.): The system buys when the 20 bar average crosses above the 50 bar average and exits when it crosses below, so the four labeled crossovers alternate buy, sell, buy, sell. Shorting is not part of the stated rules, reversing the signals trades against them, and holding through the exit at B ignores the stated exit condition.
  12. The correct answer is A (Hamilton, Rhea, Schaefer and Nelson organized the ideas; the averages were Industrial and Railroad.): Dow's successors Hamilton, Rhea, Schaefer and Nelson developed and organized his editorials into the theory. The two averages he published were the Industrial average and the Railroad average, now called the Transportation average. A utility average was added decades later, and Edwards, Magee, Murphy and Pring wrote about technical analysis in later periods.
  13. The correct answer is B (Primary tide, over a year; secondary wave, weeks to months; minor ripples, under three weeks.): The primary movement, the tide, lasts a year or more; the secondary reaction, the wave, runs three weeks to several months and retraces part of the tide; minor movements, the ripples, last under three weeks. Understating the durations confuses a secondary reaction with a minor one. Swapping primary with secondary, or secondary with minor, keeps the durations but attaches them to the wrong names.
  14. The correct answer is B (Hold and tighten the trailing stop, since RSI can stay overbought in strong trends.): In strong uptrends, RSI can remain above 70 for extended periods, so an overbought reading is a caution rather than a sell signal by itself. Confirmation from MACD and the rising average supports holding while protecting gains with a tighter stop. Selling everything or shorting ignores trend evidence, and adding on the premise that momentum cannot reverse ignores the risk of a turn.
  15. The correct answer is D (Herding, where investors imitate the crowd until buying is exhausted and a sharp reversal follows.): Herding explains why imitation feeds on rising prices and publicity, producing a long trend that ends abruptly when new buyers run out. Conservatism explains slow drift but not euphoria, loss aversion concerns holding losers, and anchoring would restrain bidding rather than extend a mania.
