  16. The correct answer is A (Availability leads people to weigh evidence by true frequency, ignoring how easily examples come to mind.): The false statement is the one about availability. The availability bias makes people judge likelihood by how easily examples come to mind, such as vivid recent events, rather than by true frequency. The other three statements correctly define framing, mental accounting and anchoring.
  17. The correct answer is D (Futures contracts, whose long charts are built by rolling a continuous series across expiring months.): Futures contracts expire, so a long chart is a constructed series that links contract months, and the linking method affects levels and gaps. Mergers, index revisions and currency rollover can all create data issues, but none involves an expiration that forces successive contracts to be spliced.
  18. The correct answer is A (The spike and reversal fit a possible selling climax, though price confirmation is still needed.): The VIX is the 30 day volatility implied by S&P 500 options and tends to surge as the index falls. A jump to 42 followed by a retreat as the index bounces is the classic look of a selling climax, but one day does not confirm a low, so price confirmation is needed. A single lower VIX day does not prove the decline is over, a high VIX marks fear rather than complacency, and a rising VIX alone does not forecast acceleration.
  19. The correct answer is B (Gravestone doji, a possible top signal because buyers could not hold the highs of the session.): Open, low and close at the same level with a long upper shadow is a gravestone doji. After an advance it shows buyers pushing up and then losing the whole move. The dragonfly is its mirror image, with a long lower shadow. A shooting star has a small real body, and a long-legged doji has long shadows both ways.
  20. The correct answer is D (Distribution, where informed holders sell into strong demand while price stalls near the high.): Wyckoff's four phases run accumulation, markup, distribution and markdown. A point at the top of a rally with heavy volume but stalled price shows informed holders passing shares to a eager public, which is distribution. Markup is the earlier climb, accumulation is the quiet base, and markdown starts only after distribution fails.
  21. The correct answer is D (Implied volatility gauges expected move size, not direction, so low VIX cannot rule out a drop): The VIX is derived from S&P 500 option prices and covers the next 30 days, so it is forward looking and fits a one month horizon. It measures expected magnitude in either direction, not direction, so low readings do not prevent a sharp drop. Low readings can mark complacency, yet that is a caution rather than a forecast of falling prices. The other options misstate the horizon or calculation method of the VIX.
  22. The correct answer is C (About 64 percent of variation is linked to the index, though correlation is not causation.): The coefficient of determination is r squared, and 0.80 squared is 0.64, so about 64 percent of the variance is shared. A high correlation shows association, not cause, because a third factor such as the broad economy could drive both. Reading r directly as the explained share overstates the link, and one minus r is not a meaningful quantity here.
  23. The correct answer is D (Between 14,000 and 16,000, the points one-third and two-thirds of the way back down the advance.): The advance is 6,000 points. One-third is 2,000, so the pullback ends near 16,000; two-thirds is 4,000, giving 14,000. Measuring up from the low flips the direction, applying the fractions to 18,000 uses the price instead of the advance, and a one-half upper limit gives 15,000, which shortens the stated range.
  24. The correct answer is A (Positive skew, with a few large gains pulling the mean above the median.): When a few large values stretch the right tail, they pull the mean above the median, and the distribution has positive skew. Skew is named for the tail direction, not for where most observations sit. Kurtosis describes tail thickness, not the gap between mean and median. The median is resistant to extreme values, so large gains lift the mean, not the median.

The open-to-close distance of 85.70 minus 84.20 equals 1.50 and is the real body, not the range. The values of 1.90 (86.10 minus 84.20) and 2.30 (85.70 minus 83.40) each measure only part of the session.
  26. The correct answer is D (Option traders expect larger moves than recent prices showed, likely pricing the coming earnings event.): Historical volatility looks backward at realized price changes, while implied volatility is backed out of option prices and reflects expected future movement in either direction. A gap of 13 points before a scheduled event shows demand for protection or speculation. It is not a forecast of direction, and the options are relatively expensive, not cheap, because 35 exceeds 22.
  27. The correct answer is B (Confirmation bias, because she seeks supportive evidence and discounts contrary evidence, instead of just updating slowly.): She actively selects which evidence to read and discounts the opposing notes, which is confirmation bias. Conservatism is a defensible reading, since both keep a prior view, but a conservative analyst accepts the new information and merely updates too little. The facts mention no classification by resemblance and no belief that she controls the outcome.
  28. The correct answer is B (Investors wanted more pay for default risk and fled to Treasuries, lifting the spread.): The credit spread is compensation for default and liquidity risk, and in a credit scare investors sell corporates and buy Treasuries, which lifts corporate yields and lowers Treasury yields. Inflation expectations affect both issuers, liquidity is only part of the story, and the Treasury yield moved by 0.4 points against 1.4 for the corporate bond, so the Treasury leg cannot account for the spread widening.
  29. The correct answer is D (A falling wedge breakout, suggesting the decline may reverse on supporting volume.): Both lines slope down and converge, which defines a falling wedge. After a decline it usually resolves upward, and a close above the upper line on rising volume supports that reading. A flag has parallel boundaries, a channel does not narrow, and a symmetrical triangle has one line rising and the other falling.
  30. The correct answer is D (A leading momentum oscillator that turns early but gives false signals in persistent trends.): Stochastic is a momentum oscillator that compares the close with the recent range, and it frequently turns before price, which makes it a leading indicator. The cost is false signals when a trend keeps the reading pinned at an extreme. Moving averages are the lagging, trend-following tools, and volume indicators use volume, which stochastic does not.
  31. The correct answer is C (Bubbles form only when investors weigh fundamentals carefully, so crowd imitation plays little role.): The behavioral account says bubbles grow through imitation, overconfidence and feedback, so the claim that crowd imitation plays little role is inconsistent with it. Feedback from rising prices, fast shared selling in crashes and confidence built by media coverage are all consistent with that account.
  32. The correct answer is B (Old resistance is now support, so the holding retest favors a long entry.): After price closes decisively above resistance on heavy volume, that level tends to become support. The return to 2,003 on light volume that held and bounced is a classic throwback test, so a long with a stop under 2,000 has a clear reference. A light-volume pullback does not make the breakout false, and the polarity principle rejects the idea that the old high keeps acting as resistance.
  33. The correct answer is C (A bull trend continuing: the reaction held above the prior low and the next high rose.): The reaction held above the earlier reaction low and the next high exceeded the first, so Dow would read the primary bull trend as continuing. The Transportations also made a new high, so the confirmation requirement is met. A reaction is not a reversal and one pullback does not make a range.
  34. The correct answer is B (Moves beyond two standard deviations occurred far more often than a normal curve's roughly 5 percent.): A move of 24 ticks is two standard deviations, and a normal distribution puts about 5 percent of observations beyond that, or roughly 12 or 13 of 250 sessions. The observed 31 sessions is about 12 percent, well above expectation, which is the signature of fat tails or excess kurtosis. The 0.3 percent figure belongs to three standard deviations. Skew describes asymmetry, and extremes in both directions point to heavy tails, not skew.
  35. The correct answer is C (Require written disproof conditions for the thesis, then review the opposing reports against them.): The trader shows confirmation bias, seeking support and discounting contrary evidence. Pre-committing to written disconfirming conditions forces a fair look at the opposing reports. Cutting the position based on loss alone, adding to it, or reciting hindsight stories do nothing to make his evidence review more balanced.
  36. The correct answer is C (The accumulation phase, in which informed buyers build positions while public sentiment remains poor): A quiet, narrow base after a long decline with poor news and informed buying is accumulation, the first phase of a bull market. Public participation comes later as improving earnings draw trend followers. Distribution is the final phase, when informed investors sell into optimism. A secondary reaction is a counter-trend move against the primary trend, not a flat base at the end of a decline.
  37. The correct answer is A (Weak participation makes the breakout less reliable and more prone to failure.): A breakout above resistance needs expanding volume to show new buyers are willing to pay up, so flat volume leaves the move with little conviction and a higher chance of failure. The pullback idea tempts because volume does matter on reactions, but breakouts need the surge. Open interest is a futures measure and does not replace volume. Thin supply is a real idea, yet a quiet bar does not prove supply is gone.
  38. The correct answer is A (Positive skew, with a long right tail pulling the mean above the median): When a few large positive values stretch the right tail, the mean is pulled above the median, which is positive skew. Negative skew would show the reverse, so naming skew from where the bulk sits misleads. Kurtosis describes tail weight and peak sharpness, not asymmetry. The median resists extreme values, so large gains raise the mean above it rather than lifting the median.
  39. The correct answer is D (Subtract the mean, divide by 3 for a z-score near negative 2.3, then use 95 percent.): The z-score is the observation minus the mean, divided by the standard deviation: (negative 6 minus 1) divided by 3 is about negative 2.3. A z-score beyond 2 lies outside the 95 percent band, so the drop is unusual, roughly a one in forty event for one tail. Skipping the mean gives a different value, multiplying instead of dividing is not a z-score, and comparing 2.3 with the 68 percent band, which ends at 1, would wrongly sound routine.
  40. The correct answer is D (The alpha may come from a flaw in the pricing model, since both are tested together): Every efficiency test is also a test of the expected return model, so a four percent alpha may reflect an omitted risk factor in CAPM and not inefficiency. Portfolio sorts use public accounting data, event studies are one of several tests, and the stem gives no reason to treat the alpha as sampling noise.
  41. The correct answer is C (The level 40, once support, now tends to act as resistance after the decisive break.): Role reversal says that once support is broken decisively, the same price area tends to become resistance, because holders who bought near 40 look to exit at breakeven when price returns. The heavy volume on the close at 38 makes the break decisive. Repeated tests strengthen support only while it holds. Levels can and do swap roles, and the breakdown low at 38 is not the level that flips.
  42. Neither average has closed above its prior closing high, so there is no confirmed new high in either one. Confirmation does not require the same calendar day. Distribution is the third phase of a primary trend and is not defined by new highs, and intraday highs do not outrank closes.

The 61.8 percent level is 120 minus 24.72, which is 95.28, the next level below 99. The 144.72 figure is an extension above the high, not a retracement.

That is deeper than the typical one third to two thirds, so the pullback is more than ordinary and a reversal of the primary trend becomes a real possibility, though not a confirmed one.   45. The close alone tempts as the final consensus, but it needs the open for context. Extremes can come from single prints, so they carry less weight. The close adds information beyond the range because it shows where the session ended.
  46. The correct answer is B (A real development draws attention, rising prices attract new buyers, overconfidence takes hold, then prices reverse): A bubble usually begins with a real development that attracts attention, then rising prices draw in new buyers, then overconfidence and the belief that this time is different, and finally a reversal. Putting overconfidence first removes the trigger, and putting the reversal before the late buyers or ending with the trigger reverses cause and effect.
  47. The correct answer is B (Stock A's advance spans more distance, a 50% gain against about 17% for stock B.): A log scale spaces prices by ratio. The move from 20 to 30 is a 50% increase, and 60 to 70 is only about 16.7%, so stock A covers more vertical distance. Equal dollar gains look equal only on an arithmetic scale. The ratios 1.5 and 1.167 are not equal, so the claim of equal distance fails.

Multiplying 0.0070 by 100,000 euros gives 700 US dollars, which is also 70 pips at 10 dollars per pip. Dividing by 1.0920 would convert the profit to euros, and the other values misplace the decimal.
  49. The correct answer is C (Measure gaps between lows, average to about 20 weeks, then project a window near week 83.): The gaps between lows are 21, 18 and 21 weeks, which average 20. Adding 20 to the last low at week 63 projects week 83, and because cycle lengths vary she should treat it as a window around that week. Projecting first and checking later reverses the logic and treats the date as exact. Using the shortest gap biases the forecast early, and averaging the dates themselves gives a meaningless week 33.
  50. Leaving 80.00 unadjusted shows actual traded prices but creates a false 50 percent drop on the chart. The opening price of 40.50 reflects the next day's trading, and multiplying by 2 is the adjustment for a reverse split, not a regular split.
  51. The correct answer is D (Descriptive statistics summarize data in hand; inferential statistics generalize from a sample to a population.): Computing the 250 day average is descriptive because it summarizes the observed data. Using it to estimate the average for all days is inferential because it generalizes from a sample to a population. Inference is about generalizing, not forecasting, and both kinds can use numbers or charts. The swapped definition, the forecast split and the numbers versus charts split all misstate the distinction.
  52. The correct answer is C (Extreme fear readings have often coincided with market bottoms, so watch for a reversal.): VIX measures the 30 day volatility implied by S&P 500 options, annualized, and it rises when investors rush to buy protection. Spikes to extreme levels have often occurred near selling climaxes and bottoms, although timing is imperfect. It is not a price target. Treating the spike as only a continuation signal ignores that panic tends to exhaust itself.
  53. Dow did not require the confirming closes on the same day, only that the signal appears in both averages. A 4 percent dip within a bull market is a typical secondary reaction, not a primary reversal, and the duration of minor movements has no bearing on whether new highs confirm.
  54. Momentum crashes after rebounds, the fading size effect and the adaptive view that anomalies erode as participants learn are all fair statements, though each is a nuance that can look suspect.
  55. Currency pairs pause on weekends and follow a conventional close, index futures have an official settlement price, and a dual listed stock has official closes on each venue.
  56. Waiting for a trendline break tempts because it is a real signal, but the warning exists before it. A divergence is a caution, not a confirmed reversal. Accumulation usually shows rising volume on advances, not shrinking bars under each higher price.
  57. The correct answer is A (It is built from S&P 500 option prices and states expected annualized volatility over 30 days.): The VIX uses a wide set of S&P 500 index option prices and expresses expected volatility over the next 30 days as an annualized percentage, so a reading of 31 means elevated expected swings. The futures and realized-change description describes historical volatility, which looks backward. The breadth description is a different indicator that never uses option prices. The directional description fails because implied volatility says how large moves may be, not whether they are up or down.
  58. The correct answer is B (Supply at 75 has been absorbed, so demand finally exceeds the sellers who capped the price): Repeated failures at 75 show sellers meeting each rally, and a rise to 76 means demand has absorbed that supply, which is why a triple top breakout is a buy signal. The reversal rule tempts because new columns do form, but it describes switching direction. The catapult adds a pullback after the breakout, which the stem does not describe. The 75 level is resistance, not a support line.
  59. The correct answer is D (Herding makes trends fade quickly, as copying investors bring prices back to value.): Herding tends to prolong trends and push prices away from value, so the claim that it makes trends fade quickly is false. Overconfidence lengthening trends, gaps in crashes and support or resistance near remembered prices are consistent behavioral explanations, though each is less familiar than the herding point.
  60. The correct answer is B (It trades at a discount: price and yield move inversely and its coupon trails market yields): Bond prices and yields move in opposite directions. The 5 percent coupon is fixed, so when market yields rise to 6.5 percent the price falls below par to offer a competitive yield, a discount. A premium needs yields below the coupon, a fixed coupon does not hold price at par, and the stem gives a general rise in market yields, not a credit problem at this issuer.
  61. The correct answer is D (The 14-month rise is primary, the 7-week decline secondary, and the daily swings minor.): Dow described three movements: the primary trend lasting a year or more, secondary corrections lasting from about three weeks to several months that retrace a portion of the primary move, and minor movements lasting less than three weeks. A 7-week decline that gives back 40% of the gain fits a secondary correction. Daily swings are minor. Seven weeks is too long to be minor and too short and shallow to be a new primary trend.
  62. The correct answer is A ($3.00 per share, the $6.00 of intrinsic value less the $3.00 premium that was paid.): The call is $6.00 in the money at expiry because $56 exceeds the $50 strike. The buyer paid $3.00 for it, so the net profit is $6.00 minus $3.00, which is $3.00. The breakeven price is $53.00, so a close at $56 is comfortably profitable. Premium is a cost to the buyer and never a return.
  63. Calling it a top contradicts that. Its upper line is flat, not falling, and apex volume is not the classifying feature. Directional neutrality describes a symmetrical triangle.
  64. The correct answer is B (Quiet base, accelerating trend, euphoric blow-off top, then a sharp decline as the herd exits.): Herding builds in sequence: a quiet base with informed buying, an accelerating trend as imitators join, a euphoric blow off on the heaviest volume just before the peak, and a sharp decline as the herd leaves. Each other order puts the climax before the trend or after the decline, which breaks the volume clue.

The closing price rule, secondary reactions and divergence as a warning are all genuine parts of the theory that a quick reader may doubt.
  66. The correct answer is D (The EMA is 45.0, above the SMA of 44.0, because the latest close carries more weight): The SMA is (40 plus 43 plus 49) divided by 3, or 44.0. The EMA is 0.5 times 49 plus 0.5 times 41, or 45.0, which exceeds the SMA because the latest close is weighted more.   67. The correct answer is D (Price movement is the sum of cycles of all lengths, combining into one irregular line): Summation says observed price movement is the sum of cycles of all lengths acting at once, which explains the irregular combined line. Harmonicity concerns the size ratio between neighbouring cycles, synchronicity concerns lows lining up, and proportionality links length to amplitude. Each is a real Hurst principle, so each tempts, but none explains why the combined line looks complicated.
  68. The correct answer is A (About 1.58, the square root of 10 divided by 4, using n minus 1.): Squared deviations are 1, 4, 4, 1 and 0, summing to 10. The sample variance is 10 / 4 = 2.5 and its square root is about 1.58. Dividing by 5 gives 1.41, the population version; 2.50 is the variance itself; and 3.16 is the square root of 10 with no division.
  69. The correct answer is A (Correlation measures joint movement, not cause, so a third factor or coincidence can create it.): Pearson's r describes the strength and direction of a linear relationship and says nothing about its source. A third variable or coincidence can produce a high r with no causal link. No r squared threshold establishes cause, a high r does not imply curvature, and r squared of 0.7225 is the statistical share of variation explained, which is still not proof that one series drives the other.
  70. The correct answer is C (The primary uptrend remains intact, and the two-week dip is only a minor movement against it.): An uptrend is defined by rising peaks and rising troughs, and the major swing lows are still higher. A two-week dip is a minor movement, which Dow Theory describes as lasting less than about three weeks. A secondary reaction lasts weeks to months, so that label overstates this dip. One lower weekly low inside rising swings does not change the primary trend.
  71. The correct answer is B (Volatility compressed then expanded, so the close above the band shows momentum, not a sell signal.): Narrow bands mean volatility is low, and a squeeze often precedes an expansion in volatility. A close above the upper band after a squeeze usually reflects a strong move, and the bands themselves do not mark a sell point. Band width is a measure of volatility, so ignoring it throws away the main information.
  72. The correct answer is A (The purchase price is his reference point, so in the loss domain he seeks risk.): Prospect theory measures outcomes against a reference point, here the 50 purchase price, so the position sits in the loss domain where the value function is convex. In that domain people prefer a gamble to a sure loss, which explains holding and adding. A reference point at the current price would not produce this loss-driven risk taking.
  73. A trailing stop lets the trader participate while limiting damage. High valuation alone is a poor timing signal, and removing stops or ignoring the chart ignores the risk the herding view highlights.
  74. The correct answer is B (The note holder bears the issuer's credit risk, while the ETF holder owns fund shares): An ETN is an unsecured debt note, so its value depends on the issuer's creditworthiness as well as the index. An ETF holds assets in a fund, so shareholders have a claim on those assets rather than on the sponsor's credit. The swapped exposure, the shared guarantee and the no risk view all fail because the note is a promise of the issuer while fund shares are ownership in assets.
  75. The correct answer is A (52.00, using a multiplier of 2 over 6, weighting the new close by one third.): The EMA multiplier is 2 divided by (N plus 1), so for 5 days it is 2/6, or 0.3333. The new EMA equals the prior EMA plus the multiplier times the difference: 50.00 plus 0.3333 times 6.00 gives 52.00. Using 2/5 gives 52.40, using 1/5 gives 51.20, and a simple average of the two values gives 53.00.

Adding the percentage gives 49.44, which lies above support. Three points gives 45.00, a different filter, and 2 percent gives 47.04, which does not match the 3 percent rule.
  77. The correct answer is D (An uptrend, since each peak and each trough sits above the one before.): The basic definition of an uptrend is a series of higher highs and higher lows, and both appear here. A channel is a tool drawn on the chart, not the name of the condition. The pullbacks are secondary reactions inside the trend, not the trend itself. Confirmation between averages is a Dow rule for signals and does not change what the price sequence is called.
  78. The correct answer is A (Not confirmed, since a 3% close needs to exceed 82.40 and the actual close was 81.50.): The 3% rule measures how far a close must travel beyond the broken level. Three percent above 80.00 is 82.40, and a close of 81.50 is only 1.9% above. The intraday high does not count, the percentage applies to the resistance level and not the prior close, and the rule has nothing to do with consecutive closes, which is a separate time filter.
  79. The correct answer is D (Hamilton applied Dow's ideas in editorials and Rhea later organized them into tenets): Dow died in 1902 and never set out a finished theory. William Peter Hamilton, his successor as editor, applied the ideas in editorials and in The Stock Market Barometer of 1922. Robert Rhea then organized them into the clear list of tenets in his 1932 book. Nelson wrote earlier in 1903 and coined the phrase, and Schaefer is linked to the mid-century revival, so neither role fits the other options.
  80. The correct answer is D (R squared describes fit to past points and does not justify extending the line far ahead.): R squared reports how closely the line fits the observed 12 points, not whether the relationship will persist. Extrapolating six months from 12 weeks of data goes well outside the sample, where trends often change. A small sample is a real concern but there is no fixed minimum of 30, a high fit describes the past, and a different curve would still extrapolate beyond the data.
  81. The correct answer is A (It erodes confidence that all participants compete with equal access to information, harming the market): The Standard protects the fairness of markets: if some participants trade on information others cannot obtain, confidence in prices falls, whatever the trader's own research showed. Employer loyalty, fair dealing among clients and record retention belong to other Standards. Having good independent research does not remove the unfair information advantage.
  82. The correct answer is A (A 61.8 percent retracement is near 62.92, so 70 is only 50 percent.): The swing is 100 minus 40, or 60 points. A 61.8 percent retracement gives 100 minus 0.618 times 60, or 100 minus 37.08, which is 62.92. A pullback to 70 is 30 points, exactly 50 percent of the swing. The 77.08 figure measures 61.8 percent upward from the low, a common slip, and retracements work in uptrends as well as downtrends.
  83. The correct answer is B (The result is probably overstated, because firms that failed or were dropped are missing.): Using only current constituents builds in survivorship bias: companies that went bankrupt or were removed never appear, so the sample leans toward winners. Costs and optimization are real backtest hazards and would also lower results, but nothing in the stem describes them. A long sample does not cure a selection problem, so only the missing failed firms fit this case.
  84. The correct answer is D (The close finished below the open for that week): In the usual Western convention a filled or black body means the close was below the open for the period. The body color says nothing about the prior period's close, the size of the range or the order of the high and low, which is why those readings are tempting but not supported. Only the relation between that period's own open and close sets the color.
  85. The correct answer is D (A high r shows association, not cause; a shared driver could explain both.): Correlation measures how closely two series move together, not why, and both copper and industrial stocks may respond to global growth. A lead lag test may add evidence but does not prove cause. The r of 0.92 means about 85 percent shared variation after squaring, not 92 percent, and no length of history turns correlation into causation. A guarantee cannot follow from an association.
  86. The correct answer is C (Buyers controlled the session from open to close, with short shadows showing little opposition.): A white body means the close was above the open, here 2,010 versus 1,980, a 30 point body against shadows of only 4 and 2 points. Buyers controlled the session with little opposition. A shooting star needs a long upper shadow, a hammer needs a long lower shadow, and above average volume adds weight to the move rather than signalling exhaustion.
  87. The correct answer is A (Look-ahead bias, because the entry uses information unavailable at trade time, so it cannot be traded.): Buying at the day's low requires knowing the low before the day ends, which no real trader can do. That is look-ahead bias, and it explains the implausible win rate. Survivorship bias and small samples can inflate results too, but nothing in the stem points to them, whereas the entry price is plainly unattainable.
  88. The correct answer is C (For a volatile series the arithmetic mean never exceeds the geometric mean, understating compounded growth.): For any return series with variation, the arithmetic mean is greater than the geometric mean, so it overstates compounded growth rather than understating it. Descriptive statistics report on the sample at hand, and inferential statistics extend to a population. The median resists extreme values better than the mean does, and the geometric mean is the correct average for compounding. Only the claim about the arithmetic mean is reversed.
  89. The correct answer is B (The unadjusted series records a real 50 percent loss at the split, so adjusting is unnecessary.): A split halves the price but doubles the shares, so holders lose nothing and the unadjusted drop is an artifact that makes adjusting necessary. Scaling volume, avoiding moving averages across mixed scales and multiplying earlier prices after a reverse split are all correct practice.
  90. The correct answer is C (Herding and greed drive buying into the peak, then fear and loss aversion drive selling.): Crowd imitation and greed feed the rise, and fear plus loss aversion drive the fast, correlated selling, which leaves a parabolic rise and steep collapse. The disposition effect, conservatism and mental accounting each fail to explain panic selling, because they describe holding, slow updating or separate accounts.
  91. The correct answer is C (Each value includes older prices, so it only confirms turns after they occur.): A moving average is built from past prices, so it must wait until new data outweighs the old before it changes direction, and that built-in lag means it confirms turns. A longer period adds lag rather than removing it. An exponential average reduces lag but cannot remove it. A simple average does not weight by volume, so bar volume plays no part.
  92. The correct answer is B (Stock prices jump to a new level within minutes of a surprise earnings release): A fast jump to a new level after surprise public news is what an efficient market predicts, so it is not an anomaly. The size effect, drift after earnings surprises and long horizon reversal are recurring patterns that challenge efficiency, even though each can be mistaken for ordinary pricing.
  93. The correct answer is C (Low volatility tends to give way to high volatility, so a larger move becomes more likely.): Volatility moves in cycles, and long quiet periods are commonly followed by expansion. The compression does not say which way the stock will move, only that a larger move becomes more likely. Low implied volatility reflects calm expectations, not an expected large move, and volatility alone gives no direction.
  94. The correct answer is A (Check for outliers and a linear shape, square r to get 0.64, then fit the line): Before using a regression, inspect the scatterplot for outliers and a roughly linear pattern, since r can mislead otherwise. Then compute r squared, 0.80 squared equals 0.64, to see how much variation is explained, and fit the line. Correlation does not prove causation, and r squared is the square of r, not its square root.
  95. The correct answer is A (Homma's rice trading, then Dow's editorials, then Nison's book introducing candlesticks to the West): Homma traded rice in Osaka in the 1700s, Dow wrote his editorials around 1900, and Nison's 1991 book brought candlesticks west. Putting Dow first tempts those who see Dow Theory as the root of charting, and putting Homma last tempts those who date candlesticks by the book that popularized them. Both reverse the real chronology.
  96. The correct answer is D (Treat the extreme pessimism as a possible bottoming condition and wait for price confirmation.): Contrarians read crowded pessimism as a condition that often precedes a low, because most sellers have already acted. Sentiment indicates conditions, not exact timing, so waiting for price to confirm a turn is sensible. Following the put buyers would be trading with the crowd at the extreme. Buying blindly at the open assumes timing precision these readings do not offer.
  97. The correct answer is D (It held near 38.2 percent, about 3,371, a shallow pullback typical of a strong uptrend): The advance is 3,600 minus 3,000, or 600 points. At 38.2 percent the retracement is 600 times 0.382, or 229.2, so the level is 3,600 minus 229.2, or 3,370.8, matching the pullback low of 3,370. A 38.2 percent retracement is shallow, and strong trends often stall there. The 61.8 percent level is near 3,229, and 50 percent is 3,300 and is not a Fibonacci ratio. Shallow pullbacks on rising volume do not signal that the rally has ended.
  98. The correct answer is C (It isolates performance relative to the benchmark, revealing leadership or lag that price alone hides): A ratio line strips out the benchmark's effect so the analyst sees whether the stock leads or lags its group, which a price chart alone cannot show. It is not a trend signal, since the ratio can rise or fall regardless of price direction. Momentum measures compare a stock with its own past, and adjustment for corporate actions is a separate data task that the ratio does not perform.
  99. The correct answer is C (Risk aversion for gains and risk seeking for losses, judged from a reference point): Prospect theory says outcomes are judged as gains or losses against a reference point, and the value function is concave for gains and convex for losses. People therefore tend to take the sure gain and gamble to avoid a sure loss. The reversed pattern and the use of final wealth are traditional utility errors. Equal weighting contradicts loss aversion, and probability weighting does not by itself make people risk seeking in both cases.
  100. The correct answer is B (Place the stop at chart invalidation, measure risk per unit, then divide 2,000 by it.): The risk budget is 1% of 200,000, or 2,000, and the stop at 48.00 gives risk per unit of 2.00, so the size is 1,000 units. The stop must come from the chart first because it marks where the idea fails. Sizing from the contract count, from margin, or from a profit target all fix the position before the loss point is known.
  101. The correct answer is D (An evening star, a bearish reversal warning as buyers lose control.): A long white candle, a small-bodied candle and a long black candle closing deep into the first body is the evening star, a bearish reversal formation at the top of an advance. The small middle candle shows indecision and the third candle shows sellers taking over. The morning star is its bullish counterpart at lows, three black crows involve three consecutive long black candles, and a harami has only two candles.
  102. The correct answer is A (A strengthening uptrend, then a weakening uptrend driven by short covering, then a strengthening downtrend.): Rising price with rising open interest means new money is entering the advance, which is strengthening. Rising price with falling open interest means shorts are covering rather than new buyers arriving, which weakens the advance. Falling price with rising open interest means new short positions are being added, confirming the decline. The other orderings misread the direction of open interest in one or more phases.
  103. The correct answer is B (After an advance this shape is a hanging man, a bearish warning; hammers follow declines): A hammer and a hanging man share the same shape, a small body at the top with a long lower shadow, and only the prior trend separates them. After an advance the shape is a hanging man and warns of a possible top. A shooting star has the long shadow above the body. The trend length argument and the gap rule are tempting extras that the pattern definitions do not contain.
  104. The correct answer is C (ADX rises only while price is rising and falls when price declines, so it shows direction.): ADX measures trend strength, not direction, so it can rise in a strong downtrend as readily as in an uptrend, which makes the statement tying ADX to rising price the false one. The other three statements are standard: a rising ADX with -DI on top is a strengthening downtrend, a low ADX signals a market without a trend, and DI crossovers while ADX is low are weak signals.
  105. The correct answer is B (Hold, because the stop sits at $55.20 and today's close is above it.): Two ATRs equal 2 x 1.40 = $2.80, and subtracting from the highest close gives 58.00 minus 2.80 = $55.20. A close of $57.10 is above that level, so the rule says hold. Adding the ATR puts the stop above the market, which is nonsense. A trailing stop is measured from the highest close and is never lowered, and a 1.55 percent pullback (0.90 / 58.00) is well inside normal noise.
  106. The correct answer is A (Public participation has begun with both averages confirming, so favor long positions): The quiet sideways trading on low volume was accumulation. Both averages closing above their reaction highs on rising volume is confirmation, and it marks the start of the public participation phase, when trend followers join and business news improves. A Dow technician would favor the long side. Waiting for lower prices ignores the confirmation, and distribution comes late in a bull market, after prolonged gains and heavy public enthusiasm.
  107. The correct answer is C (Fair dealing, because clients must be treated equitably when recommendations and changes in them are disseminated): Standard III(B) governs how recommendations and changes to them are communicated and acted on, requiring that clients have a fair opportunity to act. Giving some clients the news hours earlier is a dissemination problem. Suitability and loyalty are real duties but address advice fit and conflicts, while confidentiality concerns client information, not research timing.
  108. The correct answer is A (Upside breakout, throwback to the old resistance now acting as support, then resumption of the advance): After an upside breakout, a return to the broken level is called a throwback, and the old resistance becomes support. Here buyers defended 40.50 and price made a new high at 49, so the advance resumed. A pullback is the term for a retest after a downside breakdown, and the retest here held rather than failed.
  109. The correct answer is B (Independent steps still produce streaks by chance, so a ten-day run is possible): Independence means one day does not change the odds of the next, yet chance alone still produces long runs, so the strategist's claim of impossibility is the flaw. Drift, sampling frequency and normality each sound like part of the random walk story, but none implies streaks are expected, tests are void, or runs are ruled out.
  110. The correct answer is B (Volume usually contracts as the triangle narrows, then expands when price breaks out of it.): Triangles are pauses in a trend, so participation typically dries up as price coils, and the breakout then arrives with a surge in volume as the stalled side gives way. Rising volume during the squeeze would contradict the pause. Low volume after the break would leave the move unconfirmed. Volume contracting only at the apex misreads the way it fades as the range narrows.
  111. The correct answer is D (MACD is trend-following, built from moving averages; RSI is a bounded momentum oscillator): MACD is the difference between two exponential averages, so it follows trend, while RSI compares average gains and losses and is bounded between 0 and 100. An RSI above 70 in a strong trend often just shows persistent strength, not a reversal call. Neither tool is built from volume, and both describe price.
  112. The correct answer is A (The coupon stays 50 a year, so at 900 current yield is near 5.6 percent.): The coupon rate is set at issue, so the bond keeps paying 50 a year no matter what its price does. Current yield divides that coupon by the market price: 50 divided by 900 is about 5.56 percent, and yield to maturity is higher still because the bond also returns par at maturity. Dividing by face value just reproduces the coupon rate. A discount price raises yield, it does not lower it.
  113. The correct answer is C (Efficiency tests also test the pricing model, so the abnormal return may be model error): The joint hypothesis problem means an abnormal return against the CAPM can reflect a wrong risk model instead of inefficiency. Data snooping and trading costs are real concerns that deserve checking, but neither is the objection aimed at the stated test. Momentum is a past price rule, so it speaks to weak form and semistrong efficiency alike.
  114. The correct answer is A (Price walking the upper band reflects strong upward momentum with elevated volatility, not a sell signal.): When closes keep pressing the upper band while the bands stay wide and tilt upward, volatility is high and the trend is strong, a pattern known as walking the band. A touch of the band is not an automatic sell, because strong trends can remain at an extreme for a long time. Wide bands do not by themselves predict contraction, and the middle average keeps rising in such a trend, so it has not stopped.
  115. The correct answer is D (A black candle with a 4-point body, a 3-point upper shadow, and a 1-point lower shadow): The close of 105 is below the open of 109, so the candle is black, with a body of 4 points. The upper shadow runs from the body top at 109 up to the high of 112, which is 3 points, and the lower shadow runs from the body bottom at 105 down to the low of 104, which is 1 point. The body is not the full 8-point range.
  116. The correct answer is D (Candlesticks or OHLC bars show opens, highs and lows, exposing the gap-and-recover struggle.): A closing line connects one price per session, so it cannot show sellers pushing the open lower and buyers reversing the move. Candlesticks and OHLC bars add the open, high and low, which reveals the repeated gap and recovery. An opening line loses the recovery. Point and figure ignores time and opens. Volume shows participation, not where price traded.
  117. The correct answer is B (Volume 10, 4, 4; open interest changes of up 10, unchanged, down 4.): Volume counts each contract traded once, so it is 10, 4 and 4. Monday creates 10 new long and short contracts, so open interest rises by 10. Tuesday only moves a long position from one holder to another, so open interest is unchanged. Wednesday closes both the third trader's long and part of the second trader's short, so open interest falls by 4.
  118. The correct answer is B (Survivorship bias: the test excludes firms that were delisted or dropped from the index, inflating results): Testing only today's index members leaves out firms that failed or were dropped, which is survivorship bias. Look ahead bias and data snooping are genuine testing errors, and costs matter in practice, but the stem describes none of them. Only the missing failed companies follows from how the sample was chosen.
  119. The correct answer is A (About 0.382 and 0.618, since 34/89 is 0.3820 and 34/55 is 0.6182.): A Fibonacci number divided by the next term approaches 0.618, and divided by the term two places later approaches 0.382. Here 34/89 = 0.3820 and 34/55 = 0.6182. Dividing by the term three places later gives about 0.236, which explains the 0.236 level. The 50 percent level is a Dow-based retracement and does not come from the sequence.
  120. The correct answer is A (Confirmed by the close above the neckline, with a minimum objective of 48): The head-to-neckline height is 36 minus 24, or 12. A close at 37 above the neckline at 36 on heavy volume confirms the pattern, and the minimum objective is 36 plus 12, or 48. A right shoulder low above the head is normal in an inverse pattern, and measuring from the shoulder or doubling the height departs from the rule.
  121. The correct answer is B (Point A, the accumulation phase, where informed investors buy while most of the public stays pessimistic.): The labeled base before the markup is the accumulation phase, which Dow described as informed investors buying when news and sentiment are poor. Point B is the public participation phase, the middle and longest leg. Point C is distribution near the top. A secondary reaction is a countertrend move inside a primary trend, not the start of one.
  122. The correct answer is C (House money effect, taking added risk with recent gains as though they were not his capital): Treating recent profits as free money and raising risk is the house money effect, a form of mental accounting. Regret aversion and loss aversion would push him toward caution, not larger size, and status quo bias would leave position size unchanged. The quoted reasoning, that the money is the market's, is the signature of the house money effect.
  123. The correct answer is D (The Adaptive Markets Hypothesis, because efficiency varies as participants adapt and edges appear then fade): Lo's Adaptive Markets Hypothesis treats efficiency as changing over time: traders adapt, edges are found, then competition erodes them. That matches momentum that works and then weakens. The Fractal Markets Hypothesis focuses on investment horizons and liquidity. Semi-strong efficiency and the random walk both deny that such persistent patterns should exist.
  124. The correct answer is D (Each column covers a fixed number of trading days, so column width measures elapsed time.): The false statement ties columns to time. A column ends only when a reversal of the required size occurs, so one column may span days or months and time is not plotted. The other three statements are standard: a reversal needs three 2 point boxes, or six points, in either direction, and a bullish support line runs at 45 degrees from a bottom. The question asks for the exception.
  125. The correct answer is B (People judge outcomes by final wealth, not by gains and losses from a reference point): Prospect theory codes outcomes as gains and losses from a reference point, so the final wealth statement is the false one. The other three can look wrong to someone who assumes risk aversion everywhere, but concave gains, convex losses and distorted probability weights are all standard parts of the theory.
  126. The correct answer is B (Heavy insider buying is a bearish sign, because insiders usually buy only near market tops.): Insider buying is generally viewed as a bullish sign because insiders know their companies and tend to buy when they see value, not at tops, so the bearish claim is the false one. The other three are standard sentiment readings: low put call ratios warn of complacency, lopsided bullish surveys near highs are contrarian cautions, and a VIX spike after a drop often precedes a rebound.
  127. The correct answer is D (The U.S. dollar gained on the Canadian dollar, and the breakout is bullish for the pair.): A quote of 1.3600 means 1.36 Canadian dollars per U.S. dollar, so a rise to 1.3900 means the base currency, the U.S. dollar, gained. Closing above resistance is a standard bullish breakout for the pair. A higher number does not mean the quote currency strengthened, buying more Canadian dollars per U.S. dollar shows strength, and chart patterns on currency pairs are still analyzed even without central volume.
  128. The correct answer is C (Late buyers chase the gap, supply overwhelms them, and trapped buyers end the advance.): Gaps near the end of a long move reflect late buying. When price jumps higher after a long run, the latest buyers are chasing, supply can absorb the demand, and those buyers risk being trapped, which is the psychology of an exhaustion gap. A breakaway, common or runaway reading each fits a different position in the trend.
  129. The correct answer is B (A secondary reaction, the wave inside the primary tide, lasting three weeks to several months.): A countertrend decline of three weeks to several months that retraces a portion of the primary advance is a secondary reaction. Primary movements last a year or more. Minor movements last under three weeks. A line is a sideways formation, not a retracement of half an advance.
  130. The correct answer is A (That investors are rational, so their errors cancel instead of being shared): The argument rests on investors being rational so that errors cancel, which behavioral finance contests directly by showing biases are shared and push prices together. Limits to arbitrage and price speed are related behavioral arguments, but they are secondary, and the risk free asset belongs to portfolio theory rather than to this debate.
  131. The correct answer is D (Because the win rate is under 50 percent, the system must have a negative expectancy): Expectancy is 0.40 times 2 minus 0.60 times 1, which equals 0.2 times the amount risked, a positive edge. Win rate alone does not determine profitability, because reward-to-risk matters too. Ten losses at 1 percent cost about 10 percent, and larger sizing increases drawdown and ruin risk without changing expectancy per unit of risk.
  132. The correct answer is C (The transports did not confirm the industrials' new high, a warning the advance may weaken.): Dow Theory expects both averages to make new highs in the same direction before a trend is considered confirmed. Here the industrials made a new high while the transports closed below their high, which is non-confirmation. It is a caution about the strength of the trend, not an automatic end of the primary trend.
  133. The correct answer is B (Restate both strategies in one common form, such as ending capital, since the outcome is identical): Keeping 90 percent and losing 10 percent are the same result worded differently, and the split decision shows framing bias. Restating both in a common form, such as ending capital per 100 invested, removes the frame so the committee judges the substance. Approving the better-sounding one repeats the bias, and relying on memorable examples adds another.
  134. The correct answer is D (Note Chart 1 is straight for steady growth, so it is logarithmic; draw the trendline there.): A series compounding at a constant percentage rate plots as a straight line on a logarithmic axis and as an upward curve on an arithmetic axis. Chart 1 is therefore the log chart. For a ten-year view with large cumulative moves, log scaling keeps equal percentage changes at equal distances, so the trendline belongs there. The arithmetic chart exaggerates later moves, and calling the straight line arithmetic reverses the properties of the two scales.
  135. The correct answer is A (Convert to daily returns, take their standard deviation, then multiply by the square root of 252.): Historical volatility is the standard deviation of returns, so returns must come first. That daily figure is scaled to an annual one by multiplying by the square root of 252 trading days, about 15.87. Taking the deviation of price levels measures the trend rather than the variation of returns. Averaging returns measures drift rather than spread, and dividing by 15.87 would shrink the figure instead of scaling it up.
  136. The correct answer is D (Ordinal data, because the ranks show order but not how far apart the strengths are.): Ordinal data carries a rank order, so rank 1 is stronger than rank 2, but the distance between ranks is unknown and may differ across the list. Equal steps in rank number do not mean equal steps in strength, a starting rank of 1 is not a true zero, and ranks carry order that mere category labels do not. Interval and ratio data require meaningful distances.
  137. The correct answer is B (The momentum effect, which challenges the weak form because past prices appear to help predict returns.): Momentum means winners keep winning, using nothing but past price information, which directly contradicts the weak form. The size effect is about market capitalization, the January effect is a seasonal pattern, and the value effect concerns low valuation ratios that are public information, so it points at the semi-strong form rather than the strong form.
  138. The correct answer is A (A 27 period EMA, because Wilder's factor 1/14 equals 2 divided by 28): A standard EMA uses a smoothing factor of 2 divided by (N plus 1), while Wilder smoothing uses 1 divided by N. Setting 1/14 equal to 2/(N plus 1) gives N plus 1 equal to 28, so N is 27. A 14 period EMA reacts faster, a 28 period EMA misses the minus-one step, and a 7 period EMA is far too fast.
  139. The correct answer is C (A filter: a condition that must hold before the crossover trigger is allowed to act.): The crossover is the trigger that starts the trade, and the 200-day condition only permits or blocks it, which is what a filter does. Calling the 200-day average a trigger is tempting because it is a signal itself, but it does not start the trade alone. A value rule sets price or magnitude thresholds, not a permission test, and a backtest is a separate step that evaluates the whole rule set on past data.
  140. The correct answer is C (Thin trading and few participants mean prices record isolated trades, not broad supply and demand): Thin trading means each recorded price is one isolated trade rather than the outcome of broad supply and demand, so patterns are unreliable. Short history, odd chart formats and missing fundamentals each sound like a chart problem, but none explains why prices from a handful of trades fail to reflect the market.
  141. The correct answer is D (3.40, the absolute difference between the day's high and the prior close.): Wilder's True Range is the greatest of three values: high minus low, which is 65.40 minus 64.00, or 1.40; the absolute difference between high and prior close, which is 65.40 minus 62.00, or 3.40; and the absolute difference between low and prior close, which is 64.00 minus 62.00, or 2.00. The greatest is 3.40. The open minus prior close of 2.50 is not one of the three measures.
  142. The correct answer is A (Fair dealing covers how advice is spread across clients; suitability fits advice to each client's circumstances): Fair dealing is about equitable treatment in dissemination and trade allocation and does not promise identical results. Suitability is about fitting each recommendation to the client's circumstances, objectives and constraints. Swapping their scope, demanding equal allocations or merging them each misstate what the Standards cover.
